INT: Today is June the 9th, 1998. The survivor being interviewed is Kitty Hart-Moxon. The interviewer is Corinne Oppenheimer. We're in Birmingham, England. And the language will be English. [PAUSES FOR 4 SECONDS] Today's date is the 9th of June, 1998. My name is Corinne Oppenheimer. And I'm interviewing Kitty Hart in Birmingham, England. [PAUSES FOR 13 SECONDS] All right, good morning. Can you introduce yourself, please? KHM: My name is Kitty Hart-Moxon. I live in Birmingham, England. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] I was born on the 1st of December, 1926 in a town bordering Germany, Czechoslovakia, but the country of origin was Poland. But the name of the town has a dual name. It's Bielsko in Polish. But it was also known as Bielitz in-- in German. And the-- the county was Silesia.  INT: Thank you And how old are you today? KHM: Well, I am going on for 72.  INT: Did you have a middle name at home when you were born? KHM: No, I never had a middle name.  INT: No. Can you tell me about your grandparents? What were their names, your maternal grandparents? KHM: Well, my maternal grandmother's name was Anna. And she was born not all that far away in a holiday resort called Ustron. My grandfather's name was Joseph Werber. He was a farmer. And originally my mother's side of the family actually came from Slovakia. I don't remember my grandfather. I very, very vaguely remember his farm. But apart from that, I don't remember my-- my maternal grandfather. Because he died when I was very young. 
INT: What was your mother's name? KHM: My mother's name was [? Lola ?] Rosa Felix.  INT: And where was she born? KHM: And she was born on this farm outside the town of Bielsko, a little place called Czechowice, which housed a very small Jewish community at the time.  INT: When was she born? KHM: My mother was born on the 18th of February, 1890.  INT: Do you know what her origins-- the origins of the family name came from? KHM: No. I-- I don't know the origins of my maternal grandparents' name apart from the fact that my grandfather came somewhere from Slovakia.  INT: What was the family like? What occupation did they have? KHM: Well, they were farmers.  INT: What type of education did your mother receive? KHM: Well, my-- my-- my mother was brought up on the farm. And she had to walk in those days all the way to Bielsko to-- to the school. And eventually she was lodged with relatives in the town-- in the town of Bielsko. She had a sister. And both the girls attended school in the town. Eventually my-- my grandfather wasn't very happy about having two girls. And he tried to bring them up as two boys. In those days, girls didn't have professions. But my-- my aunt, that's my mother's sister, eventually studied medicine in Vienna. And my mother studied English in England. And she came to England in 1911 and studied the language at Bedford College in London and eventually became a teacher of English, teaching students, preparing them for state examinations, not just primary or secondary school, but quite advanced English teaching. 
INT: What's your strongest memory of your mother? KHM: Well, my mother died post-war in 1974. And she lived with me since liberation.  INT: Right. KHM: So we've had a very close relationship.  INT: Right. What about your paternal grandparents' names? KHM: I remember very little of my paternal grandparents. They both lived in the-- in the city of Bielsko right in the middle of the town. And I do recall the place where they lived. My grandfather, my father's father, had a business of agriculture products. And he had warehouses in many parts of the country. My grandfather died when I was very young. And I don't recall my paternal grandmother at all. But my father was born in the town of Andrychów. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] And of course, his name was Felix. And there were many, many Felix's in that little town of Andrychów. And it was best known for a brewery. They had a very large brewery in that town of Andrychów. Originally, the family, my paternal family going back to perhaps 300 years, came from Italy. And it had been said that one of my ancestors was called to the King of Poland, because he was a sort of doctor. And this is how the family originated in that town of Andrychów.  INT: What was your father's name? KHM: Well, my father's name was Karl Felix, which in English is Charles. He studied law. But on the death of my grandpa-- grandfather, he took over the business, the running of the business. And he ran it together with his sister in the-- in the town of Bielsko until the outbreak of the war. 
INT: And when was he born? KHM: I think my father was born in 1888. And at that time, of course, this wasn't Poland at all. It was part of the Austrian-Hungarian Empire. And both my parents were Austrian. And so were my grandparents in fact. This part of the country only became Poland after the Second-- First World War, 1918. But, of course, it had changed hands many times over the centuries. And therefore, you had people of Polish extraction, German, and Czech extraction in the town. But my father's main education was German. And he went to university in Vienna and studied law.  INT: What's the strongest memory of your father at that time? KHM: My father was a very busy business man. And I didn't see all that much of my father. I didn't see all that much from my mother, because she was teaching. So both of my parents were very busy people. And I was very much looked after by nannies and housekeeping staff. But I did have a very close relationship with my father. And in fact, my father would run behind my bicycle, because he would never let me go on my own. And so he-- he was very close to me. And of course, during the war, he didn't want to leave us. Which he had the opportunity to go away, but he wouldn't go. The last relationship that-- the last contact or my lasting impression of my father is the when I actually parted with him for good. 
INT: We'll hear more of that later. Do you know how your parents met? KHM: Well, of course, my father had connections with farming communities. Because he was dealing in flour, and agricultural products, and seeds. He was like a seed merchant. And my grandfather was a farmer. So I take it they met, because of-- of business connections. And my father had a mill in the city where he used my father's products. And of course, this is, I think, how they actually met. But they didn't marry until after my father returned from the war. He fought in the Austrian army. And he was a-- a captain in the-- in the army. He wasn't actually, I think, not on the fighting front. He was in charge of one of the cities that was occupied at the time.  INT: Did you have any other children in the family? KHM: I had a brother who was five years older than myself. So my brother and I didn't have all that much in common, because five years was a rather long gap. When he was 15, I was only 10. So I didn't see an awful lot of my brother as such. And he still went to a German school. And of course, when I went to school, this part was already Poland. And all the children of my age had to attend a Polish school at the time or Polish language, whereas his language was still very much predominantly German. 
INT: What language did you speak at home? KHM: Well, I was brought up on German, obviously because my parents' mother tongue was German. So I mostly spoke German in town and with my friends. And I didn't actually learn the language, Polish language, until I went to school.  INT: What age? KHM: In fact, I learned-- sorry?  INT: Sorry, what age was that? KHM: Well, I don't know what age I started school. I started nursery school at the age of two, which my mother ran actually, a nursery school in English. And I heard and learned all the English nursery rhymes when I was two years old. But I didn't actually learn the language as such. But I could understand and speak-- at that time as much as you can speak when you're two years old-- quite a lot of English. And it wasn't, as I say, until I went to school at probably at the age of five or so, or six, that I actually came across Polish as a language.  INT: Can you describe your home you had at the time at all? KHM: Well, I was born in a very large house, which was built by my grandfather. And he also built this mill. And in fact, the street was called Mill Street, [NON-ENGLISH] in Polish. And the house was enormous. It had about 12 flats. And most of them were occupied by members of my grandfather's-- on my maternal grandfather's-- maternal father's side. The Werber family had many, many apartments in there. And I don't recall the burning down of the mill, which was burnt down across the road. But I do recall living in this enormous house with different flats and all these members of the family. And I think we moved when I was about five or six years old. And we moved to another apartment house. And eventually, just 1937 or 1938, my father built another apartment house, my father, with my uncle. And they-- and then we moved again. So I think I remember three locations pre-war. 
INT: What was the place called that you moved to? KHM: Well, the last address that I remember was a place called [NON-ENGLISH] I think. I-- I-- I don't know. I think the last place was called [NON-ENGLISH]. But I-- names have changed so much every time the occupation had changed. When the Russians took over the town, they changed the names. When the Poles came back, they changed the names. Under communism, it was different. Now, it's reverted to a pre-war name. So names have changed very much. Names of the streets have changed in the town.  INT: Can you say what your family's economic status was at that time? KHM: I can say very little about my parents' economic state except that it was a very comfortable living. And we did quite a lot of traveling. I remember traveling with my father when I was very small to Vienna, to different exhibitions and fairs. And we had marvelous holidays, mostly in Czechoslovakia and-- and abroad and mostly in Czechoslovakia actually. Very little travel was actually done in Poland. Although I remember I recall one holiday on the Baltic. And we had quite a lot of domestic staff. And I had nannies. So I concluded or I conclude today that it was a very comfortable existence.  INT: Was that typical of the Jews at that time in your area? KHM: The-- the-- the town itself was a very small and compact little town. And as I've said before, it had a German population, a Polish population, Czech population, and the Jewish population. And it was a twin town divided by a river. On the one side of the river were pretty well Orthodox an Orthodox, small Orthodox community or known as Biala. On the Bielsko side was a more progressive and liberal Jewish community. It was a very wealthy little town because it had a textile industry, which was originally established by the Jews, who came from many parts of the world and set up textile factories in the town. And we had many industrialists, very wealthy industrialists. It was also in German hands. Not so much in Polish hands-- in Jewish hands and in German hands. It was a very prosperous little town. And it had wonderful location. Because it was at the foot of the mountains. And people came for the holidays, to the outskirts of the town. 
INT: What sort of religious education did you have? KHM: Well, I went to a primary school which was a Jewish school. And my Jewish education really stems from that because we had already modern Hebrew classes and religious classes. And it was a Jewish environment. We didn't have a secondary Jewish school in the town. And most of the Jewish children didn't go to the state secondary school, but-- I'm talking about girls. The older girls went to a girls school. Well, most of the Jewish girls went to this particular girls school known as The Convent School of Notre-- de Notre Dame. And it was actually a convent-- convent boarding school, a private-- as you would call here in England, public school actually, but not a state school. And this-- this school, people came from all over the county and boarded at the school. In fact, we had two girls, two twin girls, descendants of the emperor of Vienna, of Austria, of the Hapsburgs that were in my class. And they had very large estates outside the town. And they came every day to the school. So it was a very well renowned school.  INT: Did you board at school? KHM: No, I didn't board. I-- I was a day pupil. And I only had one year at that school. It was my secondary school. And it was just the last year, 1938, 1939.  INT: And did you-- were there other Jewish girls in the school? KHM: Yes. Well, as I said, most of the-- 
INT: Oh, OK. KHM: --German-- most of the Jewish girls attended this convent school rather than any of the other state schools.  INT: Would you know why that was? KHM: I presume it may have had something to do with the underlying antisemitism in some of the state schools. And this was a purely religious sort of enclosure.  INT: Did you experience any antisemitism yourself? KHM: Well, I didn't experience any antisemitism in the-- in the school at all. In fact, I had no idea about antisemitism whatsoever at my age. If you recall, I was only 12 years old going on for 13 when the war broke out. I do recall some-- an event, shall we say, an-- an occasion, an incident that occurred in 1938. I must explain that our city was very sports orientated. And we had the largest swimming pool in the country, Olympic pool. And all the championships took place in-- in the city. On that particular occasion, there was I think it was the international championships. I was trying to-- I think it was Poland or Hungary. And we had the president of Poland there and all the dignitaries. And all the different teams were marching just as you'd have at the Olympic games. And my team, which was the either the Maccabi or the Hakoah swim team-- swimming team, we were marching. And just as we were marching past the enclosure, we were stoned. And they were screaming and shouting, Jews out. And this rather startled me personally. I suppose some of the people already knew what it was all about. But that was my very first encounter with antisemitism pre-war. 
INT: Was it-- who was stoning you? KHM: I think part of the audience that came to watch the championships. Because what they did resent very much-- that the previous year, my club produced the Polish champion, a Jewish girl called Trauda Dawidowicz. She became the Polish champion. And it was very much resented. Although to be fair, she was honored even to-- up to today. Because outside that swimming pool, today there is a statue of the girl in bronze, which is still there to this present day.  INT: And what did-- what did she excel in, which sport? KHM: She-- she got the Polish championship in crawl. I'm not sure if it wasn't actually international. She was the Polish champion. Yes.  INT: In swimming? KHM: In swimming, yes. I-- I was in a junior team at that time. Because, you know, you had to be-- I think it was under 15s or something. She was just 15 when she won this. But I was just-- I was younger. I was in the junior team. But I won the-- I had the third place. I won the bronze in the junior team on that occasion. Yeah.  INT: Did you have many non-Jewish friends at the time? KHM: Most of my non-Jewish friends were in school itself. But most of my outside school were within the Jewish community.  INT: Did your friends go to your home too? KHM: Yes. I had many, many friends coming to my home, yes. 
INT: What about your home? Did you keep-- did your parents keep a kosher home? KHM: No. No, my parents were very liberal. They certainly belonged to the Jewish community and had Jewish friends. But they were not strictly religious. And they didn't keep a kosher home. In fact, I-- I didn't know about a kosher home until I came to Lublin during the war and met the Orthodox Jews. I-- I didn't know anything about kosher home, kosher meals.  INT: How did you celebrate your birthday? KHM: Oh, we had a great big birthday parties every year, and fancy dress parties, and fancy dress parties on ice-- on ice, some very exciting birthdays indeed.  INT: Where did your family do their shopping? KHM: Well-- what-- what I recall and because we were so very close to the Czech frontier, very much all the shopping was actually done in Czechoslovakia. Although I do recall going with my mother to local markets and doing local shopping. But as I say, my mother was very busy teaching. She had pupils coming to the house as well, preparing, as I said, people for the English state examinations. Much of the shopping was done by the woman who-- we had like a sort of living in maid. And she used to do a lot of shopping and most of the cooking. So I do recall that.  INT: At meal times, did you all sit down to meals together? KHM: Yes, we always had meals together. Yes. As far as I recall, yes. 
INT: Can you describe a typical meal in your house? KHM: No.  INT: Who sat where? KHM: I don't think I could do that. I just recall my-- my father being extremely fussy about his coffee. He-- he insisted on making his own coffee and grinding his own coffee beans. That's about all I can recall.  INT: Do you remember any particular foods? KHM: Yes, my mother's cooking was very much oddly Austrian orientated. And the cooking is-- what she cooked after the war was what she sort of used to cook pre-war. So I still continue with the tradition very much so. And I-- I do still use many, many of her own recipes.  INT: What literature did you have in the house? Do you know? KHM: I don't think I can recall very much. You must remember I was 12 years old. And my interests were very much sport orientated. And everything else I wasn't particularly interested in.  INT: What about your parents? Did they have newspapers? KHM: Oh, yes. They had a huge circle of friends. And they used to belong to a dancing club. And so they used to go out with their friends to different dances. There was-- it was great big emphasis on ballroom dancing. The same with my brother, he used to go to these ballroom classes. And of course, I wasn't allowed anywhere near. Because I was too-- told I wasn't old enough to participate in any of these activities. 
INT: How often did your parents go to synagogue? KHM: Well, I recall going to synagogue on Yom Kippur and Rosh Ha-Shana new year. And I do recall a Seder. Well, these are the two events that I can recall going to synagogue.  INT: What observances did you do in the home, your parents? KHM: None at all. None at all.  INT: How was the Jewish life in the community organized? Were there specific clubs? KHM: Oh, we had many, many Jewish clubs, social clubs. And we had to a-- next to the synagogue we had a very large building known as the Bialik House, which contained a theater, a stage. And to me the most important was that it had the gymnastic club, which I belonged to, and this marvelous gym. And we'd-- and different performances were staged in this particular building. So it was-- it was, in effect, a cultural center with many, many activities.  INT: What did your family do in the evenings? KHM: Well, I was put to bed. So I really don't know, except that my parents used to go out socializing quite a lot. But I was, as I say, looked after by nannies and the maids. And I didn't see much of my parents in the evenings.  INT: Can you recall if you had any goals at that age? You're just coming up to the-- KHM: My own? My own personal goals?  INT: Mmm. KHM: All my personal goals were directed at certain sports. That-- that was my-- I had a one-track mind. Because I belonged to the gymnastic club from about the age of two, and to the swimming club, and to the athletic club, and to the ski club. And I really wanted a career in physical training. So all my activities were directed that way. I had no-- I-- I did have vague ideas of maybe studying dentistry, because my aunt was a dentist. But I didn't have any serious thoughts about that. I really wanted to pursue a physical training career at some stage. 
INT: Do you-- can you recall whether you had a Jewish theater in your town? KHM: Well, I said, a Jewish theater was within the Bialik cultural building, which actually had a theater. It had a stage. And it had an auditorium. And it-- it-- there were Jewish plays. But we had a-- a-- a-- a marvelous little theater in the town, which-- and they used to call it the sort of small Vienna. They had all the Viennese operas and operettas staged in that theater. I don't recall cinemas. But I do recall the-- the main theater in-- in-- in the town. But we did have plays. [BACKGROUND NOISE]  INT: Anyway, I-- we're coming to the end of the tape. Did you ever go to the plays or theaters? KHM: Yes, I did. I remember seeing [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] a few Jewish plays. I-- I remember a-- I don't know if it called [NON-ENGLISH]. There are one or two Jewish plays that tend to sort of stick in my mind. I'm not sure if it was not based on at that-- at that-- at that time. And that was performed in-- in-- in the Jewish theater, yes, and [NON-ENGLISH]. Yes. That-- Those were the only Jewish plays that I can recall.  INT: What about the politics in the town and circles? Were you aware of what was happening? KHM: I was totally unaware of any politics whatsoever. I don't recall ever reading a single newspaper or being told anything about what's happening outside the town and especially in politics. 
INT: Right, we'll take a break now, please.  INT: Tape two, Kitty Hart-Moxon, on the 9th of June. We finished off the last tape about-- you were saying you didn't know anything about politics. Do you recall if your parents ever talked about things that you didn't quite understand? KHM: No, I never recall taking any-- any notice whatsoever of what was being discussed. And if anything was discussed, it certainly was never discussed in my presence.  INT: Did you have a radio in your home? KHM: Oh, yes.  INT: Mm-hmm. Did you listen-- KHM: No.  INT: --to the radio? KHM: No.  INT: What did you know about the situation of Europe-- of Europe at that time? KHM: Nothing at all. I didn't even know anything about Hitler having come to power in 1933. I think I recall just one-- one single event. And that is, I recall a-- a girl that was brought to Bielsko and attended my class. And she was a-- a-- a girl that I had never met before. And she came from Germany. And she couldn't speak Polish. And she, I remember, said, we had to leave Germany. We were thrown out of our home. And this is how I come to be in Bielsko. But that-- that now, looking back on it, of course, all Polish-born Jews that lived in Germany were expelled between 1933 and 1939. And this-- this was the-- the very first time that I heard someone say she'd been expelled from her home. 
INT: How old were-- were you when you felt the effects of the war or of any anti-Jewish-- KHM: Well--  INT: --measures? KHM: --I experienced the effects of the war before the outbreak of the war. I experienced it on about the 26th or the 28th of August. The war broke out on the 1st of September.  INT: Tell me about it. KHM: My mother and I were on holiday in the mountains-- a place called Krynica. That was the place where I took out a canoe on this very dangerous river and disappeared down the river. And police came searching for me because I hadn't got back. But in fact, I carried the canoe on my back and came back by train, [LAUGHTER] much to the everybody's [INAUDIBLE], I turned up. But my mother was absolutely frantic-- not only because I was missing, but because she had a-- a telephone call from my father to-- to say, we've got to return home at once. I don't know if he didn't say why. But we packed up and came home. And as we came into the house, I suddenly realized everything had been crated up. My father had practically emptied the whole of the contents of the flat. Everything was packed up, and my father saying, we've got to leave the town. We've got to go. And that was four days or three days before the outbreak of the war. And we were bundled. My mother and I were bundled into a train. By that time there was already a stampede out of the town. However, my father had arranged for my mother and-- and-- and myself to get onto this train to Lublin. And that is where I first experienced the effects of the war, before the actual outbreak of hostilities. Now the reason for this was, apparently-- which of course, I didn't know-- that already, machine guns were on different rooftops. And the German population already begun shooting down onto the Polish population of the town. And in fact, the invasion came very close to the city when it happened on the 1st of September, 1939. 
INT: When you got home from this holiday, where was your brother during this time? KHM: Well, he was on holiday as well. He was away with his friends. And he was summoned home. And he-- and eventually, he-- he-- he and my father arrived in Lublin, some two days after us, so that they all left.  INT: How did you travel to Lublin? KHM: Well, we actually boarded a train, and on one of the-- one of the very last trains. And my father still boarded a train. But most of the people who fled the town could no longer board the train. My uncles and my aunts, and they all had to set off on foot, because all mode of transport was requisitioned by the Polish Army. If people had cars or bicycles or whatever means of transport, it just wasn't available. The people had to move on foot. And many of the people from the town that set off on foot were eventually machine gunned by the invading forces. Because the planes came down very low, and they were machine gunning people as they were fleeing the town. But my father and my brother arrived safely in Lublin, still prior to the outbreak of-- of-- of the hostilities.  INT: Where do you go to in Lublin? KHM: Well, of course, I didn't know any of these arrangements whatsoever, but my father had it all organized. He's-- he packed up all our luggage and sent it off by train, which never arrived. It was eventually transpired that it had all been-- the train had been bombed. And all the-- everything was destroyed. And our-- none of our luggage arrived. We simply had what we carried. And my father had arranged accommodation in a very nice part of the city, in Lublin itself. And this is where we landed up, probably about the 28th or the 29th of August, 1939. My father decided that must be a safe place to be, because it was in the center of Poland. Because little did he know what that meant at that stage. 
INT: What was your accommodation? KHM: I don't recall it very much. It was a very pleasant apartment that-- we were with-- quite a lot of our friends had arrived as well. And there was quite a lot of community-- Jewish community that was already there in-- in-- that was the good part of the town. It wasn't the ghetto part of the town.  INT: How did your daily life change at that time? KHM: Well, my daily life changed radically. I mean, I thought that, well, it was almost the end of the school holiday. Soon I shall be going back to school. I kept on asking questions as, what's going on? I mean, who is fighting whom? And what's it got to do with us?  INT: What was the answer? KHM: Or what's it got to do with me?  INT: Can you recall what you were told? KHM: No. I don't recall any answers.  INT: Do you recall what effect it had upon you? KHM: All I wanted to do was to get back home to all my activities. And I didn't know that that was actually the end of-- of an era.  INT: Who lived with you in that accommodation in Lublin? KHM: Well, I think there were quite a lot of families from Bielsko that lived there, and families from Czechoslovakia. We became friendly with-- with the-- a family who-- the husband was a-- a very well-known pediatrician who had his own children's sanatorium. And we knew these people from before the war, and we met up with them there. And we were with this family for some time. And I became friendly with the son of this family, who I eventually saw killed in the street, when he walked with me in the ghetto. 
INT: How did you-- your mother cope with the food situation? KHM: Well, at that stage, there wasn't a problem. We-- we lived quite comfortably. And shopping was still pretty normal. It was pre-invasion. Because the Germans didn't occupy the city until, perhaps, two or three weeks after the outbreak of hostilities.  INT: Did you go to school? KHM: No. There were-- there was absolutely no way of attending any school whatsoever. There was total disruption of education already at that stage.  INT: What did you do, yourself, during the day, during that time? KHM: I don't recall doing very much. I-- my father wouldn't let me out of-- out of-- onto the town at that stage.  INT: How old were you then? KHM: Well, I wasn't 13. I wasn't 13 until the December. So I was still 12. Hmm.  INT: Tell me about this friend of yours who was shot. KHM: Well, this, of course, happened much later, in 1940.  INT: Right. All right. So how did things change in Lublin? KHM: What changed-- things changed absolutely radically once the city was occupied the-- by the German forces.  INT: All right. KHM: I was determined to-- to see these forces. You see, I was very curious. And my father tore his hair out, because I used to disappear and look at these forces. And they were marching through the town triumphantly, having conquered the city. And there were bands playing, and you know, there were the jackboots marching. And I used to peep from corners and just watch them. And at that time, I didn't realize the danger. Well, very soon, of course, all-- all manner of things happened. Decrees went up. 
INT: Like what? KHM: Like all-- the whole population had to register. That also applied to the Poles. So they knew exactly where people lived. And of course, the Jews also had to-- Jews had to register. And nobody, really, at that stage, understood what it meant to register. But you had to, because if you didn't register, you didn't get a ration card. You could only obtain food by having a ration card. You had to get special documents that you lived in a certain place. And if you moved from place to place, you had to get what was known as a [NON-ENGLISH] meldeschein That is a-- like a exit permit, from one address to another. So there-- there was really no option but to register. And of course, as soon as you were registered, they knew exactly where you were. And gradually, gradually things became more strict. And what we didn't know-- they were setting up a Jewish quarter with-- Lublin actually had a-- a Jewish quarter. But it wasn't a ghetto as such. But it did have a-- a-- a place where a lot of the Jews lived, and a lot of the Orthodox Jews. It was a very Orthodox community. And all these Jews, the Orthodox Jews, lived in a different part of the-- of the town. And of course, eventually-- not eventually. Actually, in 1940 already, all the Jews that were living outside the perimeter of this Jewish section of the town had to move into the Jewish section of the town. 
INT: What was that like for your family? KHM: And that was absolutely horrendous. Because it was almost like stepping back into the Middle Ages. There were sort of [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] tenement houses with courtyards and balcony-- sort of inside balconies, with little tiny apartments running off these balconies. And it became overcrowded almost within the first week of this decree.  INT: What was your family's accommodation? KHM: Well, our accommodation wasn't as what you would call an apartment. We had to share an apartment with other families. There were two families to an apartment, at that stage. But this was just the beginning.  INT: So how many people was that? KHM: Well, it could have been as many as 15 people to a small apartment. It could have been even more. Eventually, of course, when they started bringing people into the Lublin ghetto from outlying-- not just outlying villages and outlying towns-- but Lublin became the main center of deportation, in the first instance, just like Warsaw-- from countries like Holland, France, Belgium, Germany-- just about everywhere from Europe. And these people were just dumped in this small part of the town and let loose in the streets. And they had to find accommodation somewhere.  INT: Did you recognize these people as being those nationalities? KHM: Yes, of course. They all spoke different languages, and they could not speak the Polish language. Many of the indigenous population of this Lublin ghetto still spoke Yiddish. So maybe if some of the Jews, the foreign Jews, spoke Yiddish, they could communicate. But apart from that, there was a total language barrier. People were speaking French and German, and they were speaking Dutch and all kind of languages which, of course, the local population couldn't understand. And they couldn't communicate. And immediately there was complete chaos in-- in the ghetto. And there certainly wasn't enough accommodation. 
INT: Did your parents ever instruct you in-- to behave in a certain way during this time? KHM: No. My parents didn't instruct me as such. I learned very quickly. As I picked up the situation very quickly, indeed, of what was going on and what had to be done. And the first thing was the struggle for food and accommodation. Because by that time, of course, we had lost most of our possessions. My father's banks, of course, were non-existent. You couldn't just draw money anywhere. My father still probably had some jewelry or-- in fact, jewelry was the only currency that was negotiable in-- at that time. Or if you had any clothes that you could barter, but that-- that was about all your currency that you had. And people who came into the ghetto with their own currencies couldn't negotiate anything at all because it wasn't valid. Eventually, of course, the ghetto had its own currency. And unless you had ghetto currency, you couldn't exist anyway. You couldn't buy food. And your ghetto currency was invalid on the Aryan side.  INT: Describe the ghetto currency. KHM: I don't think I know what it looked like. It just was a currency printed by the Germans. I don't think I remember ever remember seeing it.  INT: What was-- KHM: But the only--  INT: --do you know what it was called? KHM: No, I don't know. I-- I think the only currency that my father used, or what I used, to get food was clothes and jewelry. Anything was negotiable. 
INT: Right. KHM: And the food, of course.  INT: What were the sleeping arrangements? KHM: The sleeping range was absolutely horrendous. You were lucky if there were two to a bed. And mostly, people slept on the floor. Well, eventually--  INT: Where did you sleep? KHM: Of course, the-- I beg your pardon?  INT: Where did you sleep? KHM: Well, I remember sleeping in-- in about, I would say, 50 to 60 different places in my time in the Lublin ghetto-- in cellars, in sewers, in lofts, in cupboards--  INT: Why? KHM: --under beds, on top of beds, on-- with other people. You name it.  INT: Why were-- why was-- why was this? KHM: Well, firstly, of course, because of overcrowding. There was just an indescribable overcrowding in the ghetto. Thousands of people were dumped, as I said, every day. Thousands of people disappeared every day as well, as deportation continued.  INT: Did you know where they went to? KHM: No. People supposedly were taken to work. It was said they were taken to build fortifications in the East. Because if you think about what happened-- which we didn't know at the time-- the Germans were eventually fighting the Russians in 1941. So rumors were that they were building these fortifications. Well, we didn't understand the situation at the time, because you couldn't listen to the radio. There were no newspapers. There were simply rumors, rumors from people who were brought into the ghetto. 
INT: Who actually got food for your family? KHM: Well, most of the food I bought for the family, because--  INT: How did you do that? KHM: What-- what was so very difficult in the ghetto was men couldn't venture out into the streets too well. Because if they did, they were picked up by patrolling German, Ukrainian, or Jewish police. So for a-- a-- men had to hide pretty well. Now, my mother did manage to obtain food, strangely enough, by her own skill of teaching English. There were still people that wanted to learn English, always in return for food. Of course, eventually-- eventually, when the ghetto was sealed, she actually managed to smuggle herself out of the ghetto. Well, first of all, she did it while the ghetto was still open. She went onto the Aryan side of the ghetto There was a period when the ghetto was open.  INT: What separated it? Was it a fence or-- KHM: Well, initially, no. Eventually, there was a wall. Initially, there was barbed wire. You could still go backwards, and you could still move out of the ghetto. In 1940 you could still move out. And in those days, she would go onto the Aryan side of the ghetto. And she taught English to a priest in a vicarage, that was housed opposite the Gestapo headquarters. And she did this two or three times a week, always in return for food. Now, this was in the early, early stages before-- before the ghetto was closed. She also managed to do it at the later stages by going down into the sewers and then up a manhole on-- on the Aryan side. Now as far-- as far as I was concerned, I used to go down. My father used to send me onto the Aryan side, also when it was no longer permitted to leave the ghetto. And I used to go down into the sewer, because I was small, and it was easier for a small person to go into the sewers and-- and get out onto the Aryan-- and it was easier for a child or a you know, a small person to do that or a young person to do that, than-- than for an adult. It was always believed that children would get away with it. Rather, adults probably wouldn't. And I used to go on to the Aryan side, barter whatever there was, whatever my father gave me, in return for food. Sometimes I was caught. Sometimes I was beaten up by the Poles. And sometimes it was taken off me. Sometimes I was caught by the Germans. But I always managed to get back to the ghetto, mostly with something. 
INT: How did you know about sewer-- KHM: Everybody knew about--  INT: And that? KHM: --all the hiding places by that time. We-- we knew all the hiding places that there were in the ghetto. Hiding--  INT: Who told you? KHM: Well, I saw people actually making these hiding places behind bookshelves, behind-- it was in lofts, in cellars, and digging tunnels. And as I said, I saw people crawling out from the manholes and down into the sewers. So yes, you got to know this. But I'm talking about the year 1940, 1941. Because my father decided there was only one way, and that was to escape from the ghetto.  INT: What did you do to do that? KHM: Well, I think it was the winter of-- it might have been winter, 1941-- might have been-- it might have been the winter '40, '41. I couldn't give you the exact date. It was winter. It might have been the first winter. Yes, it was. Yes, it was. Because 1941, the Germans were already fighting the Russians. My father decided that we've got to escape. Now, my brother had already escaped. A lot of young boys, young people, started leaving the ghetto, making their way to the East. And my brother went with a group of friends. And they actually reached the-- the Russian side on the town of Lwów, which was not occupied by the Germans at that time. My father didn't want to leave. He decided he wasn't going to leave my mother and myself. Although, a lot of men did leave. But he wasn't going to leave. So my father decided-- and we've still had my grandmother with us as well. My father decided-- he hired a horse and cart. Now, where he got it from, how he got the money, I don't know. And we piled onto this horse and cart-- my mother, my father, and myself, and a few bundle of clothes. And we traveled by horse and cart for about a week, because we couldn't go onto main roads. And most of the travel, I believe, was done at night. The mud was absolutely horrendous. And the horse couldn't pull a cart. And we had to dismount and push the cart. And we eventually reached a very small settlement on the River Bug. Now the River Bug is-- is-- is-- was actually the frontier between Poland and Russia, in-- in 1940. Poland extended much further than that. But of course, the Russians came in at outbreak of the war and occupied this part of Poland. So the-- the-- the frontier was the River Bug. And this is where we were heading for.
And we reached that place called Dorohusk, on the River Bug, in the winter. And it-- it was a horrendous journey. Because there were patrols on the road, and we couldn't, as I explained, we had to go through fields. But nonetheless, we-- we reached that place. And we-- we reached the house. And to our amazement, the-- the-- the-- where my father took us in-- it was occupied by probably about six or seven families, all trying to cross this river onto the Russian side. And some people got across. And my cousins-- my father's cousins, actually-- they managed to get across. And they had to leave all their luggage behind. But they got across. And we-- we had all their luggage. But my father decided, well, we must take their luggage, because there was things that we-- we'd probably need. And so we stayed in that village, and we were there probably two or three weeks. The reason for-- for this was that we were going to cross by horse and sleigh. A lot of people rode across this river. But my father decided we couldn't-- we were not going to do that. We were going to wait till the-- the river froze. Because they-- this river always froze in the winter. And so we did wait, and eventually this freezer-- this river froze. And in the meantime, we were all learning Russian. This is how I came to know that smattering of Russian. Everybody was learning the language. And we also learned to look out for patrols-- the German patrol, the Russian patrol, what time they patrol, what time one can cross the river. There was all-- we were taking it in turns to, you know, man this riverbank. And eventually, the time came for us to cross. And the river froze. And we were all piled up in that sleigh. And we started to cross the river. And we'd almost reached the other bank of the river, when shots rang out. And the shots came from all sides. And this chap, who-- the guide-- we all ducked in the sleigh, and we were not hit. But this chap wasn't going to take any more chances, and he-- we were almost on the Russian side when he decided to swing the sleigh around and brought us back to the German side. And so we didn't actually get across. Well, what transpired is that we were some 24 hours too late crossing this river. People who crossed 24 hours before were actually-- quite happily got across. But they already closed the frontier, because there were already skirmishes there between the-- the Russians and-- and-- and the Germans. And so we-- we were back on the German side. 
INT: Could we just go back to the Lublin ghetto when your father-- were there any-- how did you manage to get out? Did you have to have-- to have-- KHM: No, the ghetto-- the-- the ghetto was still open. Well, it-- it was still possible. Although, it wasn't permitted, you could still get out. It wasn't completely enclosed that you actually couldn't get out. I think they closed the ghetto not till 1942, when it was sealed. But at that stage, you could still get out.  INT: How was it sealed? KHM: I don't know--  INT: Oh. KHM: --because I wasn't there.  INT: Oh, right. KHM: Yeah. By the time, we'd got out. We-- we-- we had several attempts at getting out. And several times we had to turn back. But the-- the last time we got out, we didn't get back. And then it was sealed.  INT: Were there guards around, or was it barbed wire? KHM: It was always guarded. Yes.  INT: Always. KHM: There was barbed-- barbed wire. It was-- there was always guards everywhere. There were-- there were Ukrainian guards in-- in German uniforms. There were German Einsatzgruppen. There were armored vehicles all round. And there was Jewish police as well.  INT: Did you recognize the status of all these people? KHM: Yes, we knew who they were. Yes. Because the Jewish Council was set up, you see, in the town.  INT: Yes. KHM: Oh, yes.  INT: Yeah. What contact did you have with the Jewish Council? KHM: Well, we didn't have any contact with them whatsoever. No. At least I didn't. I don't know if my father did. 
INT: You mentioned your brother. How early did your brother go off to Russia? KHM: I think he-- he went a bit before we left. So it must have been late '39. Oh, yes.  INT: When you left the Lublin ghetto, you mentioned a grandmother. What happened to her? KHM: Well, she was with us.  INT: In-- KHM: She was still with us in Lublin.  INT: With us in-- KHM: Yes.  INT: --Lublin. KHM: Yes.  INT: And when you got out of Lublin? KHM: Yes, she was with us all the way there--  INT: Yes. KHM: --and all the way back. Yes.  INT: So you go back to the German border. KHM: That's right. We went back to the German border, and we still waited to see if we could get across. However, German patrols started coming into this place where all these refugees were waiting to cross. And they-- they took a lot of people away. Well, my father, have-- still had all these medals that he'd won in the first world war. And so he put up these medals up. And when the patrol came, he said, I'm an Austrian officer. I served in the Austrian Army. And I'm stranded here in this place. And this patrol saluted and promised to repatriate him. And of course, at that stage, we realized that we've got to get out, because if they come back and find out that wasn't quite so, they're taking all the other people away. They're-- they're going to take us away. So the-- the next thing is, my father got another horse and cart. And we had to go back to the ghetto. 
INT: Right. KHM: That was the first escape from the ghetto.  INT: Right. We're coming to the end of this tape, so we'll take a break now. KHM: Yeah, we can take a break.  INT: Tape three. Kitty Hart-Moxon, the ninth of June, 1998. You were telling me that you had to go back to the German border. What happened then? KHM: Well, we-- we realized we've got to leave this place, because the patrol was going to come back, and they were going to arrest us. So we made back-- we had to make our way back to the ghetto. At that time, we had all the possessions of my cousins, which was a godsend at the time. So we got back into the ghetto. We found conditions were absolutely horrendous. We'd only been away some time, but things became worse and worse and worse. And thousands of people were being brought in, and deportations were-- people were leaving the ghetto every-- every day. There were constant raids. The Einsatzgruppen, that's the action-- German action group, they-- they'd surround the ghetto. They'd burst into the ghetto at day and night, into the different accommodation. They'd-- they'd throw people down the stairs, out of windows. Lorries would pick people up and deport them away, and you wouldn't find them again. We never heard of them again. And these roundups continued day in, day out, all-- all the time on our return to the ghetto. Food became even more scarce. There were bodies lying in the street. People were starving by that time. There was very little food in the ghetto. Very few supplies were being allowed into the ghetto. 
INT: How did this affect your family? KHM: Well, it affected us immediately, because we couldn't find anywhere to-- to live. We came back into the ghetto and found that there simply wasn't any accommodation. We were lucky to find a-- a room with perhaps 15 more people in it. But of course there were constant deportations, so the ghetto began to empty, and then you found other accommodation when people were being deported. The point is you had to hide so you yourself weren't being deported.  INT: How did-- where did you hide? KHM: Well, there-- there were-- there were different ways of hiding. People, as I say, were hiding in the cellars. And when action troops moved into the flat or into the apartments, they'd smash everything up, dig people from underneath the beds or-- or-- looked into cupboards. But once-- once they'd left, and they didn't find you, you moved into a place that had already been emptied. And we've--  INT: What time of day would this be? KHM: It could have been any time of day. It could have been-- it-- it-- it was totally unpredictable. So there was always somebody on the lookout to see when the next raid was going to take place. And very often, we've-- two or three times-- we found ourselves already in the street being on-- on the verge of deportation. And we managed to get into a-- a-- an empty building. Once or twice, my father once again put on all his medals and said he served in the Austrian Army. And-- and there was some fluke of a-- a-- of an occasion where a-- a man actually, again, saluted my father and took him into an empty building. 
INT: Would you been standing with your father, with your mother and grandparents? KHM: Sometimes yes, sometimes no. I remember a time when I was actually hidden under a bed with-- with somebody else. And my father was probably in a different sort of building. And we didn't even know whether they found him or whether they didn't, not until after the raid. And of-- at-- at that time, there were a lot of sick people around as well, because there was already typhus, you know, in-- in-- in the town. So there was very little respite from these roundups. And this continued. And my father decided we-- we've got to make further attempts to leave the ghetto. And we had one or two attempts which failed, but eventually there was one attempt that actually succeeded, and we did get out of the ghetto. And that was-- I think it was about March, April, 1941. Something like that.  INT: The failed attempts, how-- how did you attempt to escape in-- on those occasions? KHM: Oh, by-- on foot.  INT: On foot. KHM: On foot. But we-- we realized we had to get back into the ghetto. There were patrols. There were Poles who were collaborating at that time. They were denouncing people. We-- we-- we were bedraggled by that time. We were very conspicuous, don't forget. We tried through sewers and on-- onto the Aryan side, and that, we realized, wasn't a proposition. Eventually, we were disguised as peasants and totally disguised. So we didn't actually look as though we were refugees or Jews or anything. 
INT: How did you do that? KHM: Oh, I don't-- I don't know where my father got all these different clothes from, but we wore handkerchiefs, just like the peasant people wear, and clothes just exactly how the peasant people look. And we didn't carry any suitcases. We each just had a bundle on our back, very small, inconspicuous. And we moved out at night. Now, there was a curfew at night, and you were not allowed to do that. If you were caught-- and we-- we disposed of our documents, as well. So we had no documents. We took off our Jewish star. Don't forget, everybody wore the yellow star or Jewish armbands. We took all this off, and we-- the-- incredibly disguised, so people didn't know we were Jews at that time. And we simply walked out one night, out of the ghetto, with my grandmother. And that was-- I reckon it was about 1941. Spring '41, anyway.  INT: Did you have any other extended family around at that time apart from your grandmother? KHM: No. No, there was nobody-- no, nobody-- nobody from our family. A lot of them got killed fleeing from Bielsko. They say my uncle and my aunt, that my-- my father's sister and her husband and-- and I think one of my cousins, they were killed on-- on the way by the-- by the-- by the planes that were flying low and machine gunning all the refugees as they were fleeing. 
INT: And in the guards, did they wear any particular color uniform? KHM: Well, the guards were the-- the SS Panzer divisions. You know, the-- the usual green uniforms with helmets, all with helmets and all with guns and with rifles and bayonets. But I would like to go back to one occasion in the ghetto where I walked with my friend's son in the ghetto. That was prior to our escape, the second escape. We were walking in the ghetto with this-- I was walking in the ghetto with a boy. Now, the rule was that you had to get off the pavement when you saw a-- a guard or-- guard or a green uniform patrolling. They were patrolling the pavements. And as you saw an approaching person, you were supposed to step off the pavement and go into the road. There wasn't any traffic at the time in any case, in the ghetto. And I stepped off the pavement, but my friend didn't step off. You see? And this particular guard came towards him, the patrol. He simply pulled out the gun and shot him through the head. And he killed him instantly. Now, oh, of course, by that time, I was already conditioned that I didn't actually see a actual murder. Now, this was the first murder that I had witnessed, although I've seen a lot of people beaten up, some thrown out through the windows. But this was the first time I actually saw somebody killed right outright. 
INT: How old was the boy? KHM: He was about a year older than I was. So he might have been 14, 15, 14, something like that.  INT: What did you do then? KHM: Well, I ran home, wherever that was at the time. I don't know.  INT: Did you know his parents? KHM: Yes, well, we were friendly with his parents, you see? His mother was a very good friend of my mother. She actually survived the war. And she-- she was here in Birmingham--  INT: Did-- KHM: --with us for a long time.  INT: Did she-- did you have to tell her that her son-- KHM: Yes, I had to tell her, yes. She-- he was the only son. Yes.  INT: Can you remember how you did it? KHM: No, I can't remember-- I-- I don't think I told her. I think my parents told her. By that time, she'd already lost her husband, because almost at-- at the beginning, like I explained, everybody had to register. They asked all doctors to register, particularly doctors, for some unknown reason. And he was a pediatrician. So of course, he registered, thinking that he might be needed for some sort of work. But what in fact happened, they imprisoned all the doctors, and he actually died in prison. We never knew what he died of, but they-- they killed him. So she lost her husband quite early on. And then she was really with us most of the time. But she wasn't with us when we escaped from the ghetto. 
INT: All right. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Is there anything more you'd like to tell me about the ghetto? KHM: Well, I think we've pretty well covered the ghetto and the shortage of food and the starvation and the-- the deportations. And the total chaos because of bringing in all these people from all over Europe.  INT: Could you give us an approximate size of the ghetto? How many roads did it include? KHM: No. I-- I went back to the ghetto last year. I went to visit the Lublin ghetto last year, because I wanted to know whether it is as I remembered it in my mind. And it was just like stepping back 50 years. It hadn't changed. In fact, it looked worse. Because the Poles had moved into the-- into the-- all these Jewish homes, have took over all the Jewish homes, anyway. But during communism, nothing was restored. And so things are just deteriorated from bad to worse and crumbling. And all these tenement houses are exactly as they were in those days. It's-- it's quite small. There was a-- a main street called the Lubartowska Street. And from that, there were perhaps half a dozen other streets, but the-- the Jews, apparently, were located even in a smaller section than that. So it-- it was very small. Comparing it with the Warsaw ghetto, it must have been a lot smaller. And conditions were totally different to the Warsaw ghetto. You see, in the Warsaw ghetto, children were actually allowed to go to school, when in Lublin, they were not. There was not-- you were not permitted to cook. You were not permitted to shop. You were not permitted to eat. If-- if a patrol came into a house, they'd-- they'd smash up all the food. Sometimes they'd set fire to-- to-- to a house, to the interior. And they'd certainly smash everything up. Smash all the books up, all the kitchens, everything, including the people. So conditions were different. And you had no contact with the other ghettos. You didn't quite know the situation in other ghettos. 
INT: Did you encounter any Gypsies in your ghetto? KHM: No. None whatsoever. No. I did not see any Gypsies. No.  INT: All right. Did they have any sort of underground or resistance in the ghetto? KHM: Well, there was, of course, a resistance, but it wasn't officially talked about. And so everybody knew, yes, there are people fighting who-- nobody knew who they were. I certainly didn't know who they were. No, not-- not when we've been talking about, in 1941. People were disorganized in the Lublin ghetto, because there was a continually shifting population.  INT: Did your parents ever talk about it, do you know? KHM: No, I don't remember.  INT: No. Right. So did you have any personal belongings while you were in the ghetto? KHM: None whatsoever. We-- the-- the belongings we had were my cousins'. They left all these things behind. And they became quite useful in bartering for food, of course. My father still had some jewelry, I think, as a-- you know, as a measure, drastic measure, in case something was needed. So-- and my mother, of course, still continued with her English lessons in the vicarage, and that was her main contact. And of course, this priest said, if ever you are in mortal danger, I will see what I can do for you. You must come. And so my father knew that somehow or other, if we ever needed false documents, because that was really the ultimate. The only way to survive was to get authentic false documents. Then they would have to be paid for. And at that stage, my father had arranged for some diamonds to be put into my back teeth. And these were my only possessions. And they were very much needed at some stage of the war. 
INT: All right. Were you or your family ever sick during this time? KHM: Oh, I think so. I think we had quite-- quite a lot of-- there-- there was a lot of sickness. But I do recall, at that stage, there-- there was the outbreak of typhus. And amazingly enough, my father managed to get some kind of immunizations or vaccinations, and we all vaccinated against typhus. And in effect, that, later on, saved both my mother's and my life. Because we both had typhus. And people who had some sort of immunization didn't quite have the fatal typhus, as-- as most people died of typhus, you see, in the camp. And we just managed-- just about managed to survive it. So it was thanks to this sort of immunization that we had early on in the ghetto.  INT: You've described some very bad periods in the ghetto. Can you recall any nice periods? KHM: No. I do not recall any nice periods in the ghetto. It was all horrendous time, trying to hide and trying to feed ourselves and trying to find somewhere to live. It was a-- a most awful time, in the ghetto. And we were terribly relieved when we actually walked out. Although we didn't know where we were going. My father had no idea. He had no map. All he knew, that he had to head south. Because east, the border was closed. North was the sea. West was Germany. And it was only south that you could run. And he thought if-- if we could walk far enough, we could get into Czechoslovakia, possibly Romania, and somehow or other reach a neutral territory. 
INT: Did you ever receive or did you ever observe any acts of kindness? KHM: Not at that stage.  INT: No. Right. [PAUSES FOR 4 SECONDS] Can we move on to what happened when you eventually escaped with your mother and father and grandmother from Lublin? KHM: Yes. Well, we-- we escaped from Lublin, and we walked and walked, and I didn't quite understand where my father was going, because he had to head south, and yet we could only walk during the night. And you walked through fields. And-- and we couldn't walk along main roads. Most of the main roads were patrolled, and there was a danger that we were going to be stopped. So he walked sort of from-- and they didn't want to go too close to farms either, in case somebody gave us away, although we were pretty well disguised. But anyway, we-- cutting a long story short, we did walk. I don't know how far, but we had stumbled on a small village. And it transpired it actually had a small Jewish farming community. And we were, by that time, exhausted. And I don't know how long we were walking. And it wasn't all that far, because we, you know, there were limited times when we could walk. But we decided to stay in that village, at least to recuperate. Well, it transpired that we actually stayed there 12 months in that village.  INT: Do you know what it was called? KHM: Now, the village was called Zabia Wola, the two words, Zabia Wola. The-- the village no longer exists, although part of-- part of the village has been renamed these days. I went to visit it last year. It was surrounded by quite large estates, Polish, aristocracy, who farmed this area. It was farm-- farming country. And the few Jews that lived there were also farmers. And there were not many refugees from the towns at that time. So it was pretty well a hideout. The Germans hadn't actually organized, shall we say, that particular community. They hadn't stumbled on it. It was quite hidden. So we lodged in-- in-- in a shop. That was the only accommodation we got there. They were very small, tiny, little houses with shopfronts, one or two little tiny shops. And we had a room at the back of the shop. Of course, we had to eat. We had to pay the rent. And that was the main problem. But my-- my-- my mother immediately set out to visit these estates. And she made contact on-- all on foot, again, disguised as a peasant woman. And at that stage, they were all peasants. And she walked into this estate owned by a Count Breza.
And he-- he was a-- he was a farmer, but he also had a lot of other refugees in his place. And those were Poles running away from the Russians again, Polish aristocracy. Now, my mother presented herself there, and she didn't at that time say she was Jewish. But she said she's a teacher of English. And she was welcomed with open arms. All these Poles wanted to learn English. And so immediately, my mother set upon teaching English to these Poles in return for food, mostly for flour, because we baked our own bread, flour and butter and milk, just the basics. When the count got to know my mother better, she introduced my father, and he employed my father in his library. He had a very important collection of ancient books, and he wanted this sorted out and also some translations, I think. And my father spoke several languages. So my father was employed there, all in return for food. And gradually, I came to the-- to-- to be there as well. And there were quite a lot of-- a number of small children there. And I taught them just the basics of reading and writing. So we were all employed there, and we were out of the village most of the-- out from the Jewish-- out of the-- all day. I also worked in the fields on the estate, harvesting, I think it was sugar beet at that time. Well, gradually, my mother walked to the other estates. There were about three large estates. And another one was on the other side of the village. It was all a long way away, and she had to do it all on foot. But she walked to another estate, and that was-- made contact. And that was a Baron von Thurnau. And he had-- actually had a German name, but he wasn't. He was a-- a Pole owner of this big estate. And the same situation prevailed there. They had refugees, housing refugees from the east, Poles. And she was teaching English. And none of the others knew she was Jewish, only the-- the count knew, and so these two owners. The other people didn't know. Because of course, we didn't know if they would have denounced us. So nobody knew that they were Jewish. We were just saying we were refugees. And we stayed there between 1941, say '40-- summer '41 to the summer of 1942. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Probably that was a mistake, because my father should have gone on. He was going to head south, and he should have carried on. But we found there was a community, and we were relatively safe at that time. Because what we didn't know and nobody knew, that we were surrounded already at that time by all the extermination centers that had been set up. And now that whole area became the extermination area for the whole of Europe. Because not far away was Belzec, and Sobibor, and Treblinka, and Majdanek. And we didn't know. We were so close, and no one had any idea. 
INT: During the period in Zabia-- KHM: Zabia Wola.  INT: --were you-- were you well-fed? Did you-- KHM: Yes. We were very well-fed, because my parents and-- and myself, we all earned food, that's all. Just the basics, and we could pay the rent, which we had to pay to the farming family. And there were quite a lot of youngsters. And we used to congregate at the edge of the forest with the youngsters, and we used to make music, play games, and mostly discuss resistance at that time. And all the youngsters were saying, well, if there's going to be a round up, which of course we didn't know, but we did, of course, it filtered out from the ghettos what was going on, if they were going to deport people to the ghettos. The youngsters were all going to go into the forest. And they were going-- and there were rumors that in the nearby forest were groups of partisans, which there were, of course, fighting, partisans, you know, Poles and Jews and Russians all fighting, guerrilla warfare. And the talk mostly was of joining these partisan groups in-- in the forest.  INT: Did you ever meet any of those partisans? KHM: No, we-- no, we never, never saw them. No.  INT: No. Did you know any names of leaders or anything? KHM: No, no, no, no. Some of--  INT: Right. KHM: Some-- some of the youngsters eventually joined the partisans. I know that. And one or two survived the war, in-- in the forests, fighting. 
INT: Were you considering that? KHM: I was considering it, yes. I-- this is what I wanted to do.  INT: What age-- what age were you now? KHM: I was-- by then I was-- what was I? Going on for 15. Yes. Yeah.  INT: Yes. When you were teaching the young children writing and arithmetic, what were you-- what language were you using? KHM: Oh, just Polish.  INT: Polish. KHM: Yes. Just Polish. They were all Poles. Yes, yes.  INT: All right. You mentioned the extermination camps. When did you become aware or fearful of what was going on around you? KHM: Well, we were fearful all the time, because we've witnessed what was going on in the ghetto, and we saw people being killed. And we didn't actually believe that people were being deported to work at fortifications, although there were groups of people working in factories, working making uniforms and on war work. So there were some people that were taken to factories, mostly young people.  INT: How did-- how did you know this? KHM: Well, because groups of people were-- from the ghetto were conscripted to work in certain factories that were set up by the Germans.  INT: And know that-- KHM: But-- but we didn't believe that they were working on fortifications. A lot of people were taken to dig trenches, and they were shoveling snow in the winter. But many people were never seen again. And of course my father didn't believe that there-- that there was such a thing as working in factories and working-- he just didn't believe it. But of course, he didn't know at that stage that people were actually being killed off. It was simply a hunch, a gut feeling that you had, by the behavior of-- the general behavior of-- of the action groups when they raided the ghetto. They were out to kill people. It was very obvious. 
INT: Yes. How did you come to leave Zabia Wola? KHM: Well, what happened there was that we were left in relative peace for possibly about six to eight months. And gradually they started to register people, just like we've seen previously in the ghetto. And-- and not only that, but they gradually-- gradually made demands on this small Jewish community, kind of ransom demands, blackmail. In other words, they used to come and say by noon tomorrow, we want a-- a dozen fur coats. [LAUGHS] Well, this small community probably didn't even own fur coats, although maybe the farmers did. And they had to run around and find goods to hand over to the Germans. Then they'd come with demands like we want a dozen wedding rings in-- at 5 o'clock this afternoon, or something like this. And so gradually, they came with all these different demands. And then they progressed and said, tomorrow we want 100 chickens or 10 cows. And now they started raiding the farms. So many pounds of butter-- every day, different demands, or five dozen eggs or things like that. And that went on. And gradually, this community realized they couldn't-- no longer provide. And always, there was the threat that if you don't, you're going to be deported. You're going-- you're-- you're-- the whole village will be emptied. And in fact, this is what we thought was going to happen one night. Because there came a ransom demand which could not be met, and that was, I think, July, August, 1942. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] And that night, when we knew that the elders of the village could no longer provide anything, there just was nothing left, we realized that we've got to get out that night. Because it was quite obvious that they-- they said, if you don't provide what we want, the whole village will be liquidated. They didn't say tomorrow, but they said they'd be liquidated. Well, there was no time to lose. All the young people ran into the forests. And I wanted to go to the forests, but in the end, my decision was to stay with my parents. And at dawn, we crept out. Oh, and my grandmother wouldn't go. She refused to move. And of course, there was this struggle. What are we going to do with my grandmother? She didn't want to go, we couldn't stay. And eventually, we had to leave her. 
INT: How old was she, do you know? KHM: I think she was 72 by that time, which was pretty well my age that I am now. But she decided she wasn't going to go anywhere, and we had to leave her.  INT: And the-- the elders of the village, were they the decision-makers that-- at that time? KHM: No. It wasn't discussed with anyone. My-- my father decided we've got to go. We're not going to take any notice what anybody else does, we're getting out of the village. And-- and we knew. We had contacts with these different estates, and we knew where the forest was. See, my mother knew the-- the-- the area pretty well by that time, because she'd been around, walking everything on foot. And so we went, and we got out at dawn in August 1942. And we got into the forest, just the three of us. And there was a clearing high up in the forest, a sort of a clearing. We could see the village nestling down below. And we actually watched the round up the morning of the liquidation.  INT: All right. We're coming to the end of the tape, so we'll take a break. KHM: Yeah. We'll take a break.  INT: Tape four, Kitty Hart-Moxon, the ninth of June, 1998. Kitty, we were-- you'd left with your mother and father the Zabia Wola. What happened then? KHM: Well, we got into the forest, and we watched the liquidation of the entire community. They surrounded the whole village with armored cars, but they brought in carts, small carts, and also lorries. And we watched people being beaten up and thrown onto the lorries and thrown onto the carts and driven away, and including my grandmother. Of course I didn't recognize my grandmother. We were quite a distance away. We heard a lot of screams and shooting. Shots were fired. And after about a couple of hours, the whole place was deserted. What we later learned, they were all taken immediately to Belzec, which was down the road, and that was where they were disposed of. The same day, they were killed, that-- that very day. 
INT: Approximately how many people, do you know? KHM: I don't know. I couldn't tell you. It was a-- a small Jewish farming communities. There were a few refugees there from-- that had escaped, like us. I don't know. The whole community was liquidated that particular day.  INT: And who were they who did it? KHM: And they-- and they-- they were not taken into a ghetto, they went straight to Belzec.  INT: Who were the people who destroyed the village? KHM: They were all the action groups. Might have been some Ukrainian units, I don't know. But-- because I didn't hear them speak. But they were all green uniforms and helmets.  INT: Right. So what happened then? KHM: Well, we were in this very-- the-- the forests around that area are extremely dense. And the Germans didn't really like going into the forest, because they didn't quite know what they were going to find. As I say, there might have been a lot of Partisans around. Now, my mother made her way to one of the estates through the forest, to Baron von Thurnau. Well, in the first instance, we were in-- in the forest for some days. And unfortunately, I got sick just at that point. And my father had to carry me. And of course, we didn't quite know what was the matter with me. I had-- I had a high fever. The-- the-- my mother made her way to the estate, and the-- the-- the count, Baron von Thurnau, sent his forester. He had a hunting lodge in that forest. Sent the-- sent the Forester and asked him to put us up in his hunting lodge, which this Forester came, collected us, and took us to this little hut. And he put us into a loft, a straw loft, with a ladder. We had to get up on-- on a ladder. And then he took the ladder away, so we were hidden right up on the top there. But at first, of course, we were in the forest for some days. We heard howling wolves, and we ate berries. You know, it was some time before we actually-- my mother actually managed to get to-- to this estate. You know, we had to wait for everything to quiet down in the village. I don't-- I can't recall, because I was sick. Well, eventually we were in that hunting lodge, and patrols came with dogs sniffing around this lodge. But they didn't find us, because we were up in the loft, and there wasn't a access to that. But nonetheless, the dogs were barking and fighting all around-- all around us. And the patrol went away.
Well, eventually the-- the forester came back and said we would have to leave, because they'll be coming back, because the dogs were making such a noise. And obviously, there was something in that lodge, because he wasn't there. It was like deserted. So my mother and I-- they explained that I was sick. So the forester said the baron said that my mother and I could come to the estate, and he'll lock us into a room and hide us until I recovered. But he couldn't-- he wouldn't, on no account, take my father, because it was too dangerous to keep a man, you see? It was just much more dangerous for men. So my father had already by that time had a contingency plan. He was going to go back to Lublin. And because it was a desperate situation, he was going to make his way to the priest who was opposite the Gestapo headquarters. So we split up at that point. And my mother and I went to the baron's estate, and we were locked in a room. And it transpired that I had jaundice, hepatitis, just at the most inopportune time. But the baron decided to keep me there. And in fact, there he got a doctor to diagnose it, and I had some treatment. I can't remember. I was put on a diet or something. And I-- I was locked in this room until I recovered. My father made his way to Lublin, and he-- he got there safely taking office his-- his uniform-- his armband or whatever. He didn't wear it anyway anymore. And he threw up all-- he threw away all his documents. He boarded a train and reached Lublin quite safely. And he was taken in by the priest, Father Krosofsky, in-- in the-- in the Catholic Church. And unknown to us, they had arranged for us to have false documents. And I was still on the estate with my mother. And then word came through from this vicarage, would my mother and I make our way back to Lublin to meet my father. And this is what happened. My mother and I, we-- we-- we-- they cleaned us up at-- in this estate, and we were put into proper clothes. And we boarded the train, and he gave us some money to board the train. And we actually reached the church and the vicarage in relative safety. When we got there, my father was hiding in the coal house, because the priest was afraid to have him anywhere else. So there was a huge amount of coal in that coal house, and my father was at the back of this, of this lump, of this great big mountain of coal.
And he was hiding at the back, sort of a dugout, a little dugout, and he was there. And that's where we met up with my father. Now, we stayed in this vicarage for a few days. We were also hidden there somewhere, I can't remember where. Because the-- the-- the-- we had to be told of the plan that my father had sort of arranged. First of all, he-- the-- the priest got authentic birth certificates for all three of us. I had the birth certificate of a person that actually died but was registered in the church. And therefore, there was this birth certificate. My mother was not-- no longer be related to me. And my father's identity, we never knew. We-- we were not supposed to know. And so we each had these birth certificates. And the priest managed to obtain all the other documentation on the strengths of these birth certificates. So we had all Aryan documents, and we had new identities, new names. And my name was Leokadia Dobrzynska. My mother's name was Mrs. [NON-ENGLISH]. I can't remember her Christian name. My father's name, we were not to be told. We had to be told our new [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] background, of course, where we were born and where we went to school. I had a different birth date, birth year. I think it was 1927. So there-- there were lots and lots of things to learn. And all this took place in the church and with-- with this priest who-- and my father, who gave us the whole breakdown. And we had to learn this. And that took a few days to get it into your head. Because you-- you were not allowed to make a mistake. And of course, we had different accents, don't forget, to the local population. So there were all kind of difficulties there, but we had to learn all this. And the plan was as follows. In those days, the Germans rounded up the Polish population. Not just the Jewish population, but also the Poles. They rounded them up in churches, in cinemas, or simply caught them in the streets. And those were people that were taken as forced laborers to work in German factories in Germany. Now, these raids took place at random. Nobody knew when and where, but quite often. And the plan was that my mother and I, when there was wind that there was going to be a-- a raid, we would try and make our way to the assembly center, where they were collecting all these people and transporting them to forced labor. In other words, we were going to be Polish forced laborers taken away, forcibly. My father-- of course, that wasn't feasible for my father. Because all these people had to-- eventually to undergo a medical examination. And as we know, all Jewish men were circumcised, but non-Jewish men, at that stage, were not circumcised.
