So Jews were identified-- Jewish men were identified instantly. So my father couldn't-- that was not a proposition for him. So the-- the priest found a farmer, a-- a peasant somewhere in Tarnów, and my father eventually lodged there and had some sort of occupation there. But my mother and I, we were to go and make our way to a-- an assembly center as soon as there was a roundup. While we were in that, also we hid in the coal house there with my father behind the coals during the night, because nothing was safe in the church. Eventually, the priest came and said there's a roundup. All the people from, I don't know, a cinema somewhere, had been caught and taken to a street called Krochmalna Street in Lublin, which was a-- a kind of railway siding. There were a lot of trains standing at the siding. And there was this great big platform. And the plan was my mother and I were going to smuggle ourselves in with these people. Now, it wasn't difficult to get in, because people are fighting to get-- get out of there. They were fighting their way out. And while a lot of people were fighting their way out, my mother and I fought our way in. And it wasn't difficult. And before we knew where we were, we were pushed into trains. Those were just ordinary passenger trains. And before we knew where we were, we were on our way to Germany with a-- with a-- with a transport of Poles being taken for forced labor in Germany, with our new identities. 
INT: Right. So at that stage, [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] when did you last see your father? KHM: I-- I said goodbye to my father in this coal house at the back of the vicarage. That was the last that I saw my father. We never saw him again after that, although he did survive for some months, and he was working somewhere in a small town outside Tarnów. Because we did actually have communication with him for several months. But of course, after we were picked up, we don't know if he wasn't picked up as well. But well, obviously, he was picked up at some stage soon after that.  INT: Can you recall anything he said to you on that occasion? KHM: No, he-- the-- the only thing I recall is just remember your new identity. All these things have to be remembered, every single detail. You must forget your old name. You must forget where you were born, your schools. You've got a total new identity, and this is all you've got to remember. And remember, your papers are in order. You just remember your new identity.  INT: Can you remember anything that you said to him? KHM: No. I cannot remember. Nope.  INT: Was your mother able to say goodbye to him? KHM: I don't know. I can't-- I-- I really don't know. I don't know.  INT: So what happened-- KHM: I don't recall very much.  INT: Yes. So what happened then? KHM: Well, it-- it was-- there-- there was so much tension there, you can imagine. You know, we were in hiding. We were-- had these new identities. We had to find a way of getting in-- in-- in with these forced laborers. There wasn't all that much time for any emotional goodbyes. We were very much alert to what was going on around you. There were roundups everywhere. They were rounding up Jews, they were rounding up Poles. So it was a very tense situation. We were in a carriage with a lot of Poles. And both my mother and I decided we must keep quiet as much as possible, must not speak, because our accents were different. We-- we didn't want any-- anyone to be suspicious. And we were incredibly tired anyway from all the events that had happened in the previous week, and we were just content to sleep. We were taken to-- of course, we were taken at first to a transit place called Dessau, where they were dividing people. Because this was a long train full of forced laborers, men, women, and they were sorting people out. Different factory people came and took groups of people. 
INT: How were you selected and sorted out? KHM: I don't-- I-- it was totally random. 50 people were counted and taken this way, 50 the other way, completely at random. 50 women were taken onto one side. And we found ourselves in a group of 50 women. We were taken to a place called Bitterfeld into a factory, the IG Farben Chemical Works, Aluminum Works, in Bitterfeld, which was a huge, huge concern, complex, industrial complex. And it had laborers from all over Europe, forced laborers, volunteers, all different nationalities.  INT: What country was this? KHM: Now, that was in Germany. It is a-- a town between Leipzig and Halle an der Saale, the small town of Bitterfeld, which was then in East Germany, until reunification.  INT: How long were you traveling? KHM: I-- I really don't know. Perhaps two days, something like this. I-- I don't recall very much of the journey.  INT: Were you traveling all the time, or did it stop? KHM: Well, it-- it stopped in a place where we were disinfected or-- or-- and sorted out. And then, of course, people were distributed to all these different-- different places. Incidentally, the-- the-- the mother of the boy that was killed was also on this transport, and she-- she was selected to a different factory. And she survived the war in that factory, hidden. But there were unfortunate circumstances in the-- the group of 50 that we found ourselves in. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Now when we came to be interviewed-- everybody was interviewed, all the papers were being checked, [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] it turns out my mother was one of the eldest. There were mostly young people. They-- they selected young girls. But my mother and I stuck together all the time, because we didn't want to be parted. So they allowed her to come with my group. Most of the girls were taken to work in the factories. The first thing you were interviewed is if you could speak a language. Well, I came forward and said I could speak German, so did my mother. So immediately, my mother was pulled out, because she could speak German, and she was told that she would be in charge of this group, and she wouldn't have to actually work in the factory. She would just keep the accommodation clean. And we-- we had a special hut for these 50-- 50 people. And she wouldn't have to actually do any physical work. All the other people were taken to work in the aluminum factory themselves, which was very nasty sort of work. They were shaking out sacks of aluminum dust, and it-- it wasn't very nice. Many of them contracted all kinds of lung diseases after that. Well, of course I could speak German, and I was pulled out because I could speak German. And I was told that I will be-- I was selected to work in an office. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Well, I was very up-- there was terrible apprehension at that stage for me to work in an office. I-- I couldn't actually write German as such, although I could speak it. My written German wasn't particularly good, but probably sufficient to what I had to do. And I was selected to register-- first of all, register people. Because as I explained, there were all different nationalities. There were Italians that were volunteers, the Yugoslavs or Croatians that were volunteers, and there were Poles that were forced laborers. And-- and there were some some other nationalities there. And all had different camps. They were all segregated. And I-- I was eventually given the task of sorting out and being in charge of issuing meal vouchers to all these different nationalities. And they all had different color meal vouchers, because they all had different food. The German-- the-- the-- the Poles had worse food than the Italians, and-- and the-- the Italians had better food than the Yugoslavs. And this is how they were distributing Russians and-- and food canteens. But because I was distributing these vouchers, I was actually given the voucher to eat in the German canteen. So I was the only person that had direct contact with the German civilian population working in-- in-- in that plant. And they were mostly women, because most of the men had been taken to-- to-- to-- into the forces, although my boss was a-- was a man, Mr. [? Meyer. ?] And he was extremely good to me. He actually taught me to type German in shorthand. And he, you know, he-- he was a marvelous person to work for. Because he didn't know at the time I was Jewish. 
INT: And was he Jewish? KHM: No, he was a German. He was a German-- a German manager of this administrative department within the IG Farben factory.  INT: Right. KHM: Yes, he was a German.  INT: What did you-- what did you wear? Did you wear your normal clothing? KHM: Well, I had very little clothes at that time, of course. Well, most of the people did, because they were all caught. They were all actually caught wherever they happen to be. So from that point of view, everything was safe. Nobody had any clothes, so we were not particularly conspicuous. The worst came when it was the girls were allowed to write home to say where they were. And that is where the stumbling blocks started, of course, because we didn't have anywhere to write to. At that stage, we didn't know where my father was. But we wrote to the priest to tell him where we were, and he had contact with my father. And eventually, we did establish contact with my father, which, with hindsight, was probably a big mistake. Because my father started writing, and we don't know some of the things that he wrote. It may have been censored. We don't know. Some things reached us, some things may not have reached us. He also started to send parcels, which was totally unnecessary, because we were very well provided for. And I think we were actually paid some nominal sum of money. And I-- I remember actually buying some clothes. So we were not short of anything at all. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Now, we were there, I explained it must have been September by that time. Probably September '42. What we didn't realize for a long time is that among the 50 of us was a Ukrainian woman. Now, we didn't quite know what she was doing there, because the Ukrainians were actually volunteers. And this-- these were all forced laborers, so what was the volunteer doing within our group? So we became slightly suspicious. Well, it turned out that she was actually planted there as a spy. Of course, we didn't know that at the time. What else we didn't know but suspected that there were-- we were not the only Jews within this group, that there were others. Of course, we didn't know whether they had documents or-- we-- our documents were authentic, so we didn't worry too much. 
INT: Where did you keep your documents? KHM: Well, it was all kept in this hut. We-- we had one hut for 50 women, and I-- I can't remember whether we had lockers or whatever. But-- but it was pretty reasonable accommodation for-- as forced labors go. I mean, yes, the labor for the women was most unpleasant, but they-- they were treated reasonably-- reasonably well. I was treated very well indeed, working in-- in the-- in the office. And what's more is every morning, coming into the office, I had to say heil Hitler, with big, lots of Polish swear words in my, you know, under breath. But also in-- in the canteen, everybody had to come in and say heil Hitler. So my-- I worked my identity pretty well. I also learned to pray in the same way as the Catholic girls prayed, which I was taught by the priest. So I wasn't all that conspicuous. We didn't look particularly Jewish. But of course, things did happen soon after, when we realized that there were too many Jews within a small group of 50 of us.  INT: How did that come about? KHM: Well, how exactly it came about, we shall never know. We do know that there was this Ukrainian woman that was planted there. We do think that all our documents-- they-- everybody had to hand in their documents, and they were scrutinized. And they wanted to know who was who. I-- I don't think it could have been all that difficult to sort out people's documents, you see? If they sorted out a birth certificate, they soon found out that this particular person had actually died. Possibly they could have found that out. So of course, immediately, they became suspicious. Possibly some of these documents had false stamps. We don't know. But I know all the documents were scrutinized. And we don't know if the other Jewish girls in our group actually had documents. Maybe they had no documents. Because after all, people were caught in the midst of walking the street. They may not have had documents on them. We don't know. It transpired that one day my mother was called in, Mrs. [NON-ENGLISH]. She was called in to the chief of the Bitterfeld works, the IG Farben. And he said to her, I have rumors that there are some Jewish women among your group. Would you keep your eyes and ears open and let me know of your suspicions. Well, we knew that, you know, it can't be far before something drastic was going to happen. And then one day, again, my mother was called in-- and then one day, one person went missing. And we couldn't understand this. One of the girls went missing. And it was one of the Jewish girls that went missing. Funny enough, she was the least conspicuous of the girls. And she was blond and blue-eyed, and nobody'd have known. Maybe there was something wrong with her documents. We can't-- don't know. Anyway, the-- the-- the next thing that happened is my mother was called in once more to the chief. And when she got into the office, the first thing that struck her was the skull and crossbones on the table, s hat from the Gestapo. And that is how the Gestapo was called in. Or maybe they had already had their suspicions, but that's the first time she saw them, because all these were civilian people. And the next thing was about 15 of us were called out and brought into this office. And when we looked back into the door, there were guards on the door, and with rifles and bayonets, and the doors were shut. And we knew then that obviously, they suspected all of us, and they had all our documents laid out on the table, all the-- all these people's documents. And before we knew where we were, were bundled into a Black Maria and driven off to the Gestapo headquarters in Bitterfeld. And there began all the interrogations. We were put inside. 
INT: When you say we, who was that? KHM: Well, the whole group, the whole group. I think there were about 15 they took in, but about two or three they released. I mean, obviously, they-- they were not Jewish. One girl that was missing, she, later transpired, was already on the Gestapo headquarters. She-- she had already been interrogated. So there were 13 of us. So with that-- within that group of 50, there were 13 Jewish girls. And it was just too many to survive. If it had perhaps been two or three, it-- we might have got away with it. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] The interrogations began. But first of all, we were segregated and put into solitary confinement, and you know, the usual thing. You were deprived of sleep and deprived of food. And you were interviewed, interrogated individually. And there was always the same questions. Where-- where did your documents come from? What is your name? What is your real name? We know your documents are false. Where did you have-- where did they come-- we must know where these documents came from. And that's one thing we were not going to divulge, no matter what.  INT: How did your mother cope under this? KHM: Well, my mother coped very well. And we were interrogated on and off for days. And one day, I was interrogated. And again, I said, look, I can't-- I don't know. This is my name. This is where I live. This is where I was born. These are my documents. I can't tell you any more. My name is Leokadia Dobrzynska. I kept on repeating the same thing over and over again. Once or twice, I was slapped in the face. Once or twice, I was given chocolate as an enticement, you know, inducement to say more. Eventually, after, oh, I think we were-- I think they transferred us. Then they transferred us to Halle, to the prison, the actual prison. And there was a whole commission of interrogators again. And one day, I was interrogated, and I was told, [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] well, you know, your mother's confessed. I said, well, she's confessed to what? I-- I don't know what you're talking about. I-- I don't know anything different. And then they came to my mother and said, well, your daughter has-- no. They didn't say that, your daughter said. We know that your-- I think she was supposed to be my aunt at that time. We know your niece has already said, confessed. We know that her documents are false. Give us your real name. And she did. She did. She actually fell into the trap, you see? She fell into the trap. And they-- the next thing is they came back to me and said, we-- they just called me by-- by my-- my name. He-- he-- he-- somebody burst into the room and said, we know your name, Kitty Felix. And I just passed out. That's all I-- that's all-- that's all I remember of that occasion. 
INT: Right. We'll take a break there, because we've come to the end of the tape. KHM: Yeah.  INT: Tape five, Kitty Hart-Moxon, ninth of June, 1998. You had just passed out, having been told that your cover was blown. Right. After regaining consciousness, [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] what is the first sense you experienced? KHM: Well, what happened is I was-- I was apparently dragged out of that room. Because everybody was interrogated individually. And I was thrown into a cell. And when I came to, there were all the other people there. There was my mother. There were all these Jewish girls. They were all there, all in one cell. And we all found ourselves together in the cell.  INT: What did you think had happened? KHM: Well, we-- we didn't know how they discovered it. We did know that-- that the Ukrainian woman was there, planted as a spy. And she, possibly with some other collaborators, found out who was Jewish, who was suspected of being Jewish. And they scrutinized all the documents, and somehow or other, they-- they happened to find out. We don't know-- actually, if we didn't have a contact with my father, whether that in some way also gave-- blow-- blew the cover. We don't know. This is something we-- we really don't know. What we-- what we-- what we did, my mother and I, decided, that on no account, because we didn't know at that stage whether my father was safe or not. He-- he might-- he might well have still been alive, you see? We might have even blown his cover, or the priest, for that matter, of course. Something might have happened to him if they found out where the documents came from. So we decided on no account, at no stage, were we going to stay where these documents came from. We just-- it had been handed to us, and that's it. We were not going to divulge how we obtained these documents. We also realized soon after that they're going to keep us alive until we keep-- until we blow the secret. So as long as we keep the secret, there was a chance for us to be alive. That we began to realize sooner or later. Now, the next thing was there was a kind of-- and we were-- we were in Halle in prison. And there was-- 
INT: Where was that-- where was that situated? KHM: Not all that far from Bitterfeld. We were taken in a Black Maria. And as we were taken away from the Gestapo headquarters, there were some of the people from the IG Farben Works outside the Gestapo headquarters. And the man who looked after me, who was the boss of the-- the manager, he had tears running down his face. And he tried to say goodbye to me, but he was pushed to one side. And these people actually saw us being loaded into the Black Maria and taken off to-- to Halle to-- to the prison. In-- there in prison, we were-- well, there were further interrogations, but primarily there were sort of statements and a kind of court, a semi-court, with interpreters. And they set out our charge-- the charges against us, big documents, which we eventually had to sign. Well, the charges were that we-- not-- not only were we Jewish, not only were-- did we possess false documents, but we entered Germany illegally. So there was three charges, all carrying the death penalty, all these charges. And so we had to actually sign these were the three. These were three things that we did, criminal offenses we did. And it-- it carried the death penalty. We were told it carried the death penalty. And they were sort of laughing and joking and saying, well, tomorrow we're going to have fun here. Tomorrow there's going to be dancing lessons here. Ha, ha, ha. That was all tomorrow it was going to happen. And we realized that tomorrow, in fact, they were going to kill us. Because we're told-- told us this carried the death penalty. So on-- the next morning came. We were-- we were all in that one cell together. And we were all-- some people were crying. Some people were very quiet. Some were praying. And my mother-- I was sitting on my mother's lap, and she was comforting me and telling me, don't worry, you know, we must die in dignity, and you know, and talking about the childhood. And-- and I didn't want to die. At that stage, I realized I was going to die. And I ran to the window, and I looked out at the sky, and I realized that was the last time I was going to see stars, you know. That sort of thing came-- came to me. Anyway, at dawn, we were all led out into the courtyard. It was quite-- still quite dark outside. And there-- there was a brick wall. And what else we had noticed, that they had put sawdust all along the brick wall. And of course, immediately, we thought that was to mop up our blood, you see? And-- and there were also guns lined up. And sure enough, we-- we were led out. And we were told to face the brick wall. And guards were at the side with-- with the-- with the rifles, ready to shoot. And somebody gave a command. And I tried to run to my mother to say goodbye, but I was sort of pulled by the collar and pulled back. And then there were shots. And some girls fell to the ground, but nobody was actually hit. What in fact they did, they shot into the air. And there was great big roar of laughter. And then came one of the commandants or whoever they were, a prison-- prison officers, also in uniform, and said, heh, you really thought we were going to shoot you? This is much too good a death for you. No. We're not going to shoot you. You're going to die slowly. You have-- your sentence has been commuted to hard labor for life. And we were led back into-- into-- into the-- to the prison. Well, we didn't know where we were going at that stage. 
INT: And who told you this? KHM: That was the-- one of the-- the prison-- one-- one of the guards. But he was in a flat cap, so he was an officer. Yes.  INT: Did you know his name? KHM: No, no, no. Of course not. No. No.  INT: So what happened next? KHM: Well, they-- they took us back in, into the prison. And then we-- they-- they started transferring us from one prison to another, from Halle to Leipzig. And from-- we were not there very long, a few days. Well, in Halle, in Halle, we were some time, because there were all these interrogations going on, and we were taken out walking around the courtyard, you know, and sort of around. The prison officer-- prison warder, actually, in the center, and all the prisoners had to walk around so many times a day for exercise. Eventually we landed up in Dresden, in a prison, in a huge, huge cell, which was right up on the-- on the-- on the top floor, and it had a glass dome. And you could actually see the whole city from there. And it was a-- it really was a huge cell. And gradually, that cell began to fill up. And there, I met the first Gypsies. They brought in some some-- some Gypsy women. There was all women, of course. They brought in Gypsy women. They brought in Russian women. And they brought in German prisoners, murderers, who had actually committed murder, also some German political prisoners, all the-- all the prisoners. It was a collecting cell, which we didn't know at the time. Well, we realized it was a collecting cell because there were all different inscriptions on the wall of-- of the cell. And some of the walls, some of the inscriptions said, see you in Auschwitz. So we already had an inkling that maybe this is where we're going. 
INT: Did you know what Auschwitz was? KHM: Well, this-- this is what I'm coming to. We-- we sort of-- my mother looked at me, and I looked at her, and I said, well, what did she mean, see you in Auschwitz? What is in Auschwitz? Now, my mother remembered Auschwitz, the Polish name, Oswiecim, as a small fishing village. It was kind of lowland, whereas Bielsko was high land. Auschwitz, which was some 30 miles or kilometers north, was pretty well lowland. It was bog land. And it had a lot of lakes and quite a fishing industry. People used to go fishing there. My mother said, I remember going fishing. People used to go fishing there. Well, what can be there? What we did-- what she did remember was that there was quite a elaborate siding. It was a kind of railway junction. That's the only thing she remembered, the-- she said, it was also a railway junction. I remember, if you-- if you had to go to a certain place, you usually passed Auschwitz junction. It was a large junction. But she says, she can't understand what there was. We had no idea what there was. And then there were other inscriptions on the wall, such as, we're on the way to hell. You-- you-- nobody has ever come back. And those were inscriptions on the wall of this cell. And we didn't realize this was a transit cell. They were collecting prisoners, you know, and sending them to Auschwitz. And then one day, a warder came back, called out our group, and said officially, well, your sentence has been commuted to hard labor for life in Auschwitz. And this is how we realized that we were actually being transported to Auschwitz, with-- with this whole collecting cell. There were-- there were so many people in that cell. It just had one-- it had a lavatory, actually, a-- a toilet in the cell. But it no longer had any beds or anything. People were just sitting on the floor. It was completely full, this-- this whole cell. And one night, the whole cell was emptied, and we were taken to a train in the town. 
INT: How were you taken to a train? KHM: Pardon?  INT: How were you transported to the train? KHM: I think in Black Marias. Yes. Because everything went in Black Marias. You never-- never saw-- looked-- could see out where you were. You were taken to a siding. And this train was a passenger train. And it was a fairly new train, designed specially to carry prisoners, not cattle trucks. They were-- they were-- they were coaches that were divided up into small cells. Now, that I know-- can describe to you absolutely distinctly, because it-- it was such a unique thing to see. There was a-- a-- a-- like a corridor, just like you have a-- a-- a coach that has a corridor, and then it has small compartments. Well, all the compartments had been taken out, and there were little cages. Now, some were bigger, some were smaller. Some were cages for two people. Some were cages for four people. And then there was just a gangway where the guard was moving backwards and forwards. And the cages-- and all the-- the windows were painted up white, so you couldn't see where you were. So you-- and before we-- we knew where we were, all the women were put in-- in these coaches. There were-- there were men as well. The whole prison, apparently, it was a whole transport taken to Auschwitz from different prisons, I take it. But we-- we were put into these little cells. And the cells were so that you couldn't sit down. There were little cells. I think the cells that we were in were actually cells for two, but we were four in one of these cells. And you could only stand. You couldn't sit. And well, my mother and I found ourselves with two big Russian women in one-- squeezed into one of these little cells. And it was padlocked. And so that's-- 
INT: Did you have any food? KHM: No. We have no food whatsoever. No food was issued at all as-- as we were loaded onto the trains. And none of the-- neither were there any lavatories. Sooner or later, of course, the two Russian women, everybody, needed to go to the lavatory. And they were still loading the trains. And no one could go to the lavatory. Eventually, they-- the-- the guards came in, and they saw a mess on the floor, and they started screaming and shouting and-- and beating people up and then taking people to the lavatories to-- to-- end-- the end of the coach. And this is how I was actually transported from Dresden into Auschwitz.  INT: Right. How long did it take? KHM: Well, I wouldn't know how long it took. We-- we were standing. I-- I really don't know. It might-- it certainly-- certainly a good many hours. And I thought the date was the 12th of April, 1943. Incidentally, the-- I was [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] at the Gestapo headquarters on the Friday the 13th of March, 1943. Now this transport to-- to Auschwitz, we arrived in Auschwitz on the 6th of April, 1943. This I actually discovered from the archives, that it wasn't the 12th, but it was the 6th of April. I was always under the impression that it was the 12th. But you-- you did lose the count of days and time and day of the week. You-- you had no idea. 
INT: Did you sleep on that journey? KHM: I don't think so. I think we could only stand. We took it in turns to sit, because as I said, it was a cell really intended for two, but there were four of us in a cell. And we were just-- there was just enough room to stand. And one person crouched down, the other had to stand. So we took it in turns. And there were the huge-- two huge Russian women, who started fighting. And-- you know, it wasn't-- it was a horrendous journey in-- into Auschwitz.  INT: Did-- did you talk? KHM: No.  INT: No KHM: I don't think so. Because there were Russian women. No, we didn't have much of a-- I don't think anybody had any conversation whatsoever at that stage.  INT: Right. Were you aware that anyone was sick on that journey? KHM: Well, we didn't see anybody else, because everybody was caged up. The whole-- the whole-- the-- the whole coach was all in coaches-- in-- in-- in little cages. And we don't know what the other coaches were like, of course. But certainly, the coach I was in was all little cages. So you had no contact with anybody else--  INT: Right. KHM: --until you actually-- until-- well, I don't know how many hours or when it was. I don't know how long the journey took. I don't know how far it is from Dresden to Auschwitz. KHM: Some two kilometers we were walked on. We-- we didn't go through the gate, 'Arbeit Macht Frei,' which was Auschwitz 1. We were led further on through a field. I don't know, because it was dark. And as we approached, there was a most peculiar red glow in the sky, in the distance. And I didn't see-- I didn't see any movement or anything, but what was very distinctive was the smell. There was a kind of sweet smell of roasting. And my mother turned to me, and she said, oh, what a funny place. Do you mean-- do you think they're roasting chickens here at this time of the morning? We didn't quite know what it was. Of course, we knew later those were the glow from the crematorium. 
INT: What could you hear? KHM: Nothing. It was-- it was still early dawn when we got there. Absolutely nothing, until we actually reached nearer to the gates. We didn't know what it was all about. That was the very first thing that greeted us. Well, what we didn't know, that, of course, 4 o'clock was such a time. We-- oh, whistles. Yes, you could hear whistles, lots of whistles blowing in the distance. And that meant that it was getting up time in the-- in the camp, and prisoners had to run to-- to stand outside their block for roll calls. But we didn't know that at the time.  INT: No. So you-- you're getting towards the gate of this place. Did you-- was there any name given to it? KHM: There was nothing at all. We came through the gate. KHM: Well, the-- the normal procedure was that when people were brought in--  INT: What happened to you? KHM: To me? No.  INT: No. KHM: We did not go through a process of selection. INT: No. Can we have your knowledge, what happened to you? KHM: Yes, my-- my knowledge of, we went straight through, into a-- a building called the sauna, which now, of course, we know it wasn't such a thing as a sauna. But it was-- it was called the sauna. It was like the bathhouse or whatever, disinfecting station, you could say.  INT: So what happened to you there? KHM: And there, they took all-- everything off you, just everything that you had off you, any of your possessions. You had to leave everything behind. And you had to go and be so-called disinfected. You went into a great big long [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] hall. In that hall were great big tubs of green fluid. And prisoner-- women prisoners-- were standing there with mops, I would say. And we were-- we were mopped down with this green fluid. And then you went further on, and you had to put your arms and your legs out, and they were-- there was a-- a kommando, or I should say a group of prisoners, working. And they-- they were called the [NON-ENGLISH]. Those were the hairdressers. And they were shaving hair of all the newcomers. So they were shaving you under the arm, pubic hair. And then, of course, they took-- cut your hair off. Sometimes they shaved your hair. Sometimes they just at randomly cut-- cut your hair with scissors, depending on who was cutting your hair.  INT: What happened to you? KHM: Well, I had my hair with scissors, actually. And it was the most holy mess, of course. In part, it was shaved, in part, it was short, in part, it was long. And so was my mother. We were both disinfected. And-- 
INT: Did anyone speak to you during this process? KHM: No. There was an awful lot of screaming and shouting and beating going on, and everybody was told to hurry up and urged on, and people with whips. There were prisoners--  INT: In what language? KHM: Well, the-- the-- the camp had its own language. And unless you could speak German and Polish and all the other languages mixed together, you couldn't understand. Anyway, it had its own vocabulary. So at first, you couldn't understand the commands anyway. That you progressed. As you progressed through the camp, you learned-- you learned the language.  INT: So you didn't understand what they were saying to you? KHM: A lot of it, I didn't understand, no. A lot of it, I didn't understand.  INT: Right. KHM: I mean, a lot of it was in German commands, which you didn't understand, you know. [GERMAN], and well, you know, we didn't quite know what he meant. You know, there were a lot of expressions which you-- you probably couldn't understand. You just had a gut feeling of what was required of you. And if you didn't do, you soon knew, because you were hit with whips or kicked or-- or whatever.  INT: Were you personally hit? KHM: Oh, yes. I was hit many times. Oh, yes. Yeah.  INT: Right. You are naked. You're being disinfected. KHM: Yes.  INT: What happened then? KHM: Well, the next thing was you were thrown-- well-- well, you-- you were taken to a heap of clothes. The clothes that we had been given were khaki uniforms, which were taken off Russian prisoners of war. 20,000 Russian prisoners of war were brought into Auschwitz prior to our arrival, and most of them were killed immediately. And-- and their uniforms were handed out. So I had a pair of breeches and-- and-- and a khaki shirt and a pair of clogs. That-- that was all. That-- that is all that was issued at that time. 
INT: Right. KHM: The same for my mother. We all looked alike. We all had the same clothes, the same clogs, and hair shaved. And believe you me, you could not recognize-- I couldn't recognize my mother. And when she looked at me, and I looked at her, we actually laughed. Because it was just too-- too funny, because we didn't recognize ourselves. Of course, there was no mirrors, but you pretty well knew what you looked like by looking at the next person.  INT: How long did this process take? KHM: Well, I don't think it took too long, because everything was done at full speed.  INT: Right. KHM: And I also think that not everybody was issued with the same clothes. I-- I also think that some of the German prisoners were issued with different clothes. Sooner or later, you were issued with the things like triangles and crosses on your back. And I think there was, like, a bit of a segregation.  INT: When did that happen? KHM: I think it happened after-- but after that, you were actually tattooed. What happened then is they were taken to another long hall, and there were women prisoners sitting behind long trestle tables. And there were-- everybody had-- was-- was numbered. So everybody was issued with a number, every single person, with a number.  INT: What was that like? KHM: Well, it was just like a pin prick on the forearm, on the left forearm. All tattoos were done on the left forearm. So my tattoo-- my mother was first, as a matter of fact. 39933. That was her number. Then there was a little triangle underneath, and this was-- denoted that-- that you were Jewish. I came next, 39934, again with a little triangle underneath. So we were-- we were tattooed at that stage. And then according to the tattoo, these were girls working what was known as the [NON-ENGLISH], sort of the office and the recording department. And I think that is where they began to sort of sort people out, which were the German prisoners, which were the Russian prisoners, and which were the Jewish prisoners. 
INT: What happened immediately after you were tattooed? KHM: Well, when everybody was tattooed, we were then allocated to a block. The next thing was you were actually in-- in what was known as on-- in quarantine for six weeks. It was nothing of the sort, of course. It was a slave labor market, as we know. But the-- the euphemism for this was quarantine. And you were sorted out. And the Jewish prisoners went on the Jewish blocks and the Russians on the Russian blocks. And the German immediately got privileged positions, and I think they got striped uniforms with their numbers written on and the triangles, red triangle for political and green and blue-- green and black triangle for criminal prisoners. So they-- they were sort of sorted out after being tattooed.  INT: Right. KHM: And of course, they didn't have a triangle. The non-Jews didn't have a triangle tattooed on their arms. But I was actually classed as political. At some stage there, in my record, it said political. Now, this-- this-- this became of-- of great importance. Because in most of the prisoners that came into Auschwitz were never really cataloged as such. But my transport, coming from prison, was actually cataloged and recorded.  INT: Right. We're coming to the end of the tape. We'll take a break now.  INT: Tape six, Kitty Hart-Moxon. The ninth of June, 1998. Kitty, you've arrived in Auschwitz. You've been through the delousing process, and you've now been tattooed. What happened immediately after that? KHM: Well, I was going to show you my tattoo. But of course I-- I can show you my scar on my left forearm, because I had my tattoo removed in the 1950s. But I have got it. It is intact. It is actually been mounted. And I've also got my mother's tattoo. And both of them are mounted together, which you can see later. 
INT: Right. KHM: Immediately after that, everybody was driven out and allocated to blocks. We were allocated to what were called the quarantine block. Now, you-- you-- you were-- you-- we arrived. Of course, we had no food. There was no food dish-- dished out after our tattoo. It was 24 hours, in fact, before we had anything at all, and then only, of course, the bread ration. We were taken to a-- a stone block, I think it was. Because Auschwitz had wooden blocks, and it also had stone blocks. And inside-- inside the blocks were sort of bunks, usually housing about eight people to one bunk. And the whole block housed around about 1,000 women. Now, you were not allowed to stay inside. You were supposed to be outside, what was known on the [NON-ENGLISH], the-- the-- the lawn. But of course, there wasn't a lawn. It was in-- it was in the mud. The whole place was bogged down with-- it was bog land, and it was muddy. It was always muddy. In the summer, when it was very hot, it was-- the mud had dried up and would get-- it cracks. You know, it was difficult to walk. But mostly, it was just pure mud. And we were-- we were just sitting around, literally, in that mud. That's where we had to stay all day. And we were what was known on the slave market. It was kind of initiation period to the camp. Presumably you sorted out the situation. It was such a bewildering place, people going backwards and forwards, the ruling prisoners running backwards and forwards in all different uniforms, in all different clothes, groups of prisoners working in-- in all different parts of the camp. And you were just sort of caught to-- to different work parties. And so if you could dodge these work parties-- that was the first thing you learned, is to dodge being caught for sort of heavy work. And that went on for about three or four weeks, before people were actually allocated to any proper work groups. Now, what-- 
INT: Had-- had any selection been made there? KHM: No, no. There was no-- no. There was no selection whatsoever. We-- we-- we went through without selection. We-- we were-- during that period, several things did happen. The girl who tattooed my mother said, gosh, you are really much older than most of the people in this camp. It's-- it's just a miracle that you were allowed through the gate. When-- when we explained that we were-- didn't pass through a-- a process of selection, she said, well, something's got to be done, because you cannot possibly go on and-- and survive, not one day, working outside. Leave it to me. We will try and see if we can get you to work in the hospital compound. So that-- but, she said, there's nothing we can do for the six weeks that's the quarantine. There's absolutely no access to quarantine. But people are sort of going to be sorted out. And at-- at-- as days passed, they were coming in and wanting to know could anyone sing, could anyone-- [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] were there some nurses, were there any doctors, were there somebody-- could-- is-- is somebody a carpenter? So women could do carpentry work. This is why it was so much easier for men, because many of the men had professions, and they could actually-- or they had trades, and they could-- or skills. They could actually work in some decent sort of work groups. But you see, most of these were teenage girls, and we didn't-- we didn't have any skills whatsoever. Now, then there was with us, for instance, two sisters. One was a very small girl, young girl, and she was-- she was only 12. But she was very small for her age, and she could pass for about eight. And of course, immediately, you know, there was this business, how did she manage to get into the camp? Because we came without selection. And some of the prisoners arranged for her to be taken away, and they sort of tried to protect her. So she was given a job as a-- a [NON-ENGLISH], a messenger girl. And she became one of the messenger girls one-- almost from the start. And as she got this job, she managed to get her sister out of this group as well, and her sister got a better job. And so one or two people managed to wrangle better sort of occupations. But I realized from the beginning that the best thing is to stay invisible, so when no one gets to know your face too well. Because I soon realized that if people get to know your face, that wasn't a very good idea. You'd be caught for certain duties, certain jobs that you probably will not survive. And my mother and I soon realized that people progressed in this place if they were prepared to take on some privileged positions. And my mother's instilled this on me from the start. There's one thing we were not going to do. We were not going to emulate the Germans, and we were not going to help them to carry out their dirty work. Because the-- the-- the-- the camp was run, it was quite obvious from the start, by prisoners themselves, who were much more brutal, because they wanted favors from their superiors. And my-- my mother said, that's one thing we must never do. Anyway, I think I ought to go back to our first night. 
INT: Yes. KHM: Yes, my first night.  INT: I'd like your reaction to the events of your first few hours. KHM: That's right. The first night sticks in my mind. Of course, the-- the next six weeks is a blur, because I was dodging, hiding, trying to find ways of getting food the first six weeks and trying to assess the place. And everybody said most prisoners die the first six weeks here. You know? This is-- this is when people die. The-- the-- the life span's three weeks. If you survive six weeks, you've got a better chance of surviving longer. The longer you stay alive, the better your prospects are. So I did realize I've got to weigh up the situation. And I had to weigh up situations before. You know, this-- I didn't come from home. I didn't come from the comfort of my home. I had-- I'd been accustomed to weighing up situations. So I knew there are certain things I have to learn very quickly.  INT: How old-- KHM: Anyway, going back to the first night--  INT: Yes. How old were you at this stage? KHM: Well, I was going on for 16, wasn't I?  INT: Yes. And what age was your mother, [INAUDIBLE]? KHM: Oh, dear. No, she was born 1890, so she was in her 50s. Yes. She was considered-- considered old--  INT: Yeah, yes. KHM: --to be in the camp. Someone over 30 was considered old, you see? So there were very, very few people of her age, women. There were one two political male prisoners that were older, but certainly not women. 
INT: So your first night. KHM: Yeah, my first night. The first night, we-- I say, we spent just like sardines, eight people on a bunk. And I was lying next to a-- it transpired, a Gypsy, who had been there a few days before. And she was a German Gypsy, actually true German Gypsy. And she'd-- she asked me if I speak German. Yes, yes. And I-- I spoke to her. And she-- she said to me, give me your hand. She said, give me your hand. She said, I can tell-- I can-- I can foretell the future. Give me your hand. And she said-- she looked at mine. She looked at my lifeline. And she said, well, I don't know, but you are going to come out of here. It's written here on your wrist. I know it. Never give up. Because this is what's going to happen. And-- and, you know, I laughed at that, really. It's-- it was a big joke, I think. Anyway, she-- and we settled down for the night. We-- we didn't have any food or anything. We settled down for the night. And then whistles blew the next morning, 4 o'clock, and I had no idea where I was, you know, woken up there.  INT: Did you know the time? KHM: No, no, no, no, we had no idea, you know. It was still dark when the whistles blew. But it was known that getting up time sometimes was 3:00, sometimes was 4:00 in the morning. The whistles blew, everybody was thrown out, thrown off. And I shook this woman I was sleeping next to, and I couldn't wake her. And then I-- I-- I kept on saying, come on, wake up. You've gotta get up. And she was cold. And I was terribly cold right through that night, because you only had one layer of clothes, don't forget, and there was nothing to cover yourself up with. And she was cold. And she'd died during the night. And I was lying next to her dead body. And she-- she was dead. And my mother was very close by. And I said to my mother, she's dead, you know. And oh, and I said, I know what we've got to do. Immediately, we've got to do something. And that is we've got to have everything that she's got, everything. If she's got any bread, because we've hadn't eaten. If she's got any clothes, we must take it, because it's not good to her. It will never do any good to her at all. She is dead. And my mother agreed. And we stripped her, took everything off her. And immediately, I gave my mother a second layer of clothes. And she had a few bread rations, which you had to keep on your body, because if you didn't, you didn't have it by the morning. And so eventually, it came to me that day, first night, this is the way to survive in this place. If anybody dies, you have everything off them. But you must never touch anything of anyone of a living prisoner. But when they're dead, that's fine. That's yours. And you must, to preserve life, this is what you must do. You must try and live off the dead. And I used this principle over the last-- the next 20 months of existence in-- in Auschwitz. 
INT: Right. Were you able after that to get the physical layout of the camp? Did you realize how big it was? KHM: Well, I-- yes. Well, no single prisoner had access to any part of the camp, only the one where you were taken. But I-- I do know that my camp I was taken to in the first instance was B1b, which was a sort of a quarantine part. And this was divided by a gate-- which was patrolled-- to the second section of the camp, which was B2b. Now, on one part of B1b was the hospital enclosure, which was-- which was also enclosed, which housed, possibly, about 12 or 15 huts. And almost next to the-- to-- to these huts was the electrified fence and the ramp. And this is where the railway lines came right into the camp, where all the people came into the camp, and where selections were carried out on the main ramp. This actually adjoined-- the hospital compound, directly adjoined the-- the-- the railway and the ramp where people were being sorted out, where people were brought in. So we had a direct view of all the people that were actually being brought into the camp. So this-- this was the women's enclosure, and it was-- the-- the women's enclosure was a lot smaller than the men's enclosure. The other side of the ramp was, again, the men's-- the men's camps. But camps stretched as far as the eye could see, and much of it I didn't see for a long, long time, until 1944, when I actually worked in the Kanada kommando, when I went around the perimeters of the camp, when I actually saw the-- the vastness of the place. And of course, there were so many sub-camps. There was Auschwitz 1, which was separated from Auschwitz 2, Birkenau, by some two kilometers. And-- and of course then there were outlying camps, [NON-ENGLISH], there were loads of them. There were about 40 different sub-camps which-- which I'd never been to. 
INT: So the-- the railway line went-- actually went into the camp? KHM: The-- the railway line--  INT: Even when you were there, first. KHM: Not when I was there first.  INT: No. KHM: We-- we actually-- I actually worked on this railway at one stage--  INT: So-- KHM: --carrying the cement, carrying stones. Yes.  INT: So that wasn't there from the beginning? KHM: No, it wasn't there on my arrival, no.  INT: Right. Was there anyone in charge of your barrack? KHM: Oh, there-- there was always a person in charge of the barrack. There was always one-- well, they're called a blockalteste.  INT: And who was that in yours? KHM: I couldn't tell you who-- that's on-- on-- in that particular block, I don't know. I can tell you one later on, in a late date. Because it was a very confusing time, the first six weeks--  INT: Right. KHM: --where you had to find your feet and dodge all-- all the people that were hunting you for work. So it was a very, very confusing time. And as I say, most prisoners died. Most people brought in died these first few weeks.  INT: Did any of your group die? KHM: Well, we were-- I-- yeah, a lot of them, died, yes. Quite-- yes, quite a lot of our own prisoners died, our own group that came, yes. Well, I don't know how many eventually survived when you talk about 1945. One or two died in-- of typhus. One or two died during-- were taken during selection. Some died, yes. 
INT: Right. KHM: During the period of quarantine as well.  INT: Yes. So what happened after the six weeks of quarantine? KHM: Well, after six weeks of quarantine, my mother was taken by the prisoners from the-- from the-- the girl prisoner, a-- a-- a Slovakian girl who worked on tattooing. She collected my mother, and she got her a-- a work place working in-- in the hospital compound. And most of the time, she was there. There was a period when she was back in the camp, because nothing was secure. One minute, you were working one place, next minute, you were thrown out, and you were working elsewhere. But most of the time, she was working in the hospital block. At first she was a-- as a helper, then she was working as a nurse. Then she was working as a-- a-- just sitting at the-- at-- at-- at the-- what they call [NON-ENGLISH]. That means that somebody had to sit at the entrance to make sure nobody got in and out. So she-- she had various duties.  INT: Did she come back to your barrack to sleep, or-- KHM: No, no, no, no. She was-- she was isolated there. There was no contact. In fact, it was out of bounds. And I was separated from my mother at that stage. And for me to-- to go there was a most incredibly difficult operation to actually get there. You had to bribe people, you had to dodge people, you had to make sure nobody sees you going in. You had to make sure nobody sees you coming out. It was very difficult to actually go and see her. 
INT: Did you see her? KHM: Oh, I did. Yes. I did manage to see her quite often. And there was a period where I actually hid there. [LAUGHS] I actually hid there during the day, and I went back for roll call outside my-- my block. Because you always had to appear at the roll call outside your block, where, you know, there was a roll call every morning. And sometimes the roll call lasted one hour, sometimes three hours, sometimes four hours. And I had a roll call that once that lasted 24 hours, where people just stood and-- and died on-- and were trodden into the mud.  INT: Did the barrack elder have to do a count at all? KHM: Yes, she did. Yes, she did. She-- in fact, she had a list, a so-called list of numbers. She just simply had a list of numbers. She didn't know the faces. And this is why it was so good to be invisible. Although she had a list of numbers, you see, she couldn't put a face to the number. She had 1,000 people on her block. And the-- the least people know-- knew you, the better, of the whole [INAUDIBLE].  INT: Did she have any sub people in charge? KHM: Yes, she did. She did. She had people that are cleaners and all various hangers on, yes. And distributing meals and fetching soap. Yes, she did. She had about three people working for her. And of course, all the commanders had kapos and under kapos. And then there was quite a hierarchy sprung up of all the ruling prisoners. 
INT: Right. Were you ever part of that regime? KHM: No, I was never part of it. I never, ever had any such duty whatsoever. I never allowed myself to be in that kind of category of prisoner. Never. I worked in probably about 20 different groups. Some were worse, some were better, some were absolutely terrible. I worked outside digging potatoes, carrying stones, digging trenches, carrying cement, being beaten, being-- dogs set-- set upon me. I-- very often, I managed to hide and just do nothing. As I say, go into my mother's block and hide under some sick person. But it was so dangerous to hide there, because they had constant selections, and you were always prone to be selected. So from that point, it was dangerous. But there were days that I hid there. I had realized the best thing to do is to do nothing if you can. There was a-- there was a time when a group of women were working in the Siemens factory, for instance. The-- that problem was that they all wear-- wore striped dresses and had red scarves, I think. And the problem was, if you didn't have these clothes, you couldn't actually belong to this group. But I decided I wasn't going to work in their factory and be beaten up, but what I needed was their uniform. So with bartering, you know, with-- at that-- at one stage, I was working at what was called a [NON-ENGLISH] as well, carrying bodies. So when you carried bodies, you stripped them of whatever they had. And with the stuff, you bartered and managed to obtain food and clothes. And sooner or later, I managed to obtain such a-- a uniform that these people from Siemens wore. And so-- but there was a night shift and a-- and a day shift. And I-- I had had a-- a plan that I was going to go to sleep with the day shift-- with-- with the night shift. So when the night shift came in, there was a role call, and I had to stand roll call in my block. As soon as the roll call was finished and whistles blew, I ran over to the Siemen's block. I walked in with the night shift, and they slept all day. So I stayed on their block all day. And then at night, when the-- when the night shift went out, again, there was a roll call. I went back on my roll call, and I slept in-- in my hut. So I didn't go on night shift, and I didn't go on day shift. But I-- I sort of belonged to them, but nobody knew. And I-- I did this for some months. There were occasions when I couldn't do it, of course. And-- and I sort of wore my-- my striped clothes and my other clothes underneath, and I changed over and put the others on top to go to roll call. There were all kind of chicanery that you had to indulge to-- to-- to be able to do that. The-- the same applied if you-- you see, when the whistles blew, there was a great commotion. All prisoners came in from work, from outside, and they came into the camp. And whistles blew. That meant they had to go and stand roll call. But during-- there was a commotion for-- it lasted for about half an hour. And during that half hour, you-- or perhaps even longer, I don't know. You could do all sorts of things. You could barter, go and see your friends, go and see your mother, if you could get in. Go and see if you could get into a lavatory. At one stage, there weren't any lavatories when I first came, so that didn't apply. But later on, when they built these lavatories, it was difficult to get into the lavatories. That's when you did it. That's when you tried to barter and do or perhaps try and wash or whatever. You did-- when the whistles blew, in between, while there was a commotion, the best time to do anything was while there was a commotion. As soon as things began to-- there was a curfew, and everything was orderly, you couldn't step out of line. So there were certain things you had to obey, and this is what you had to learn. And if you didn't obey certain rules, you were doomed. And if you obey other-- if you did obey the rules, you were also doomed. So you had to knew what-- you had to get to know what was the best thing to do and how to-- how to maneuver. And you couldn't live on food that was issued. You had to somehow organize-- organize was the universal word in Auschwitz. It meant to buy, to sell, to steal, to-- to barter. And that was just a universal word, and you had to learn it. And you had to learn it quickly. 
INT: Tell me about meal times and food. KHM: Whew, well, there was no such thing as meal times, of course. Very often bread rations-- bread ration, a bread ration was issued as you were walking in after roll call. As you were walking in, you got your bread ration. This was your evening meal.  INT: What time? KHM: Oh, well, this was after roll call. Whatever time roll call finished. That was your evening meal, and this was your breakfast. In the morning, they dished out sort of blue fluid, and that was your drink. That was dished out, actually, inside the hut-- sometimes inside, sometimes outside. Things varied.  INT: Who dished it out? KHM: Well, the-- the people who worked for the block leader. That-- that was all you had, your evening bread. Sometimes, what they used to call it [NON-ENGLISH]. It was a small piece of margarine or a small piece of sausage that was added to it-- [NON-ENGLISH] means addition-- to your bread ration, which was just a small crust of bread. But that was your meal for the evening and for morning, issued food. And-- and your soup midday, now, that dependent of where you were working. If you were working outside, they actually brought it to the out-- outside kommandos. They-- they used to bring-- bring the food out. If you worked in the camp, again, you-- sometimes some of the prisoners had to go and collect the great big drums of soup, and then they were dishing out ladles of soup. Everybody-- that was your whole food that you had. And believe you me, you couldn't live on it longer than perhaps three weeks. This is why so many people died the first three or six weeks in Auschwitz. Because it was a starvation diet, and your body couldn't sustain life. And therefore, you had to organize. But there were means of organizing. But you had to know how to go about it. And that is what you had to learn. And you had to learn quickly. 
INT: So what did you learn to do? KHM: Well, I learned to barter, first of all. First of all, I learned you had to take things off dead bodies if you possibly could. And you had to learn to barter. If you could get hold of-- didn't matter what. Everything was currency. A-- a-- a handkerchief was worth two bread rations. A-- a-- a-- I-- I don't know. I cannot give you examples. Just anything was a currency, except currency itself. Money didn't count. Jewelry, not really. Only to privileged prisoners. Ordinary prisoners needed a-- a pair of shoes. A pair of shoes, you couldn't buy. You would have had to find mountains of food to pay for a pair of shoes. If you had a pair of shoes to trade in, well, yes, you could buy other sort of food. Now, people that worked in a place like the sauna, where people came in, you see, they organized clothes. And from these clothes, they would buy food from people that worked where they were distributing food. This is why one single person couldn't very well survive on her own. You did need to live in a little tiny family of three or four. Because one person worked in one place, one in another, and so you smuggled stuff in, and you changed, and you bartered. I worked digging potatoes at one time, so I smuggled in a few potatoes. With a potato, I could buy something else from somebody else that worked elsewhere. This is how-- this is-- this is how the black market-- it was the biggest black market. But of course, the biggest black market was the Kanada market, which we'll come to later. But goods from the Kanada did filter into the camp. And this is how you could trade and eventually find ways of supplementing your diet. But prisoners who didn't simply died of starvation. 
INT: What about your health, sickness? Were you ever sick yourself? KHM: Small sicknesses or whatever, you didn't even notice. If you had a cold, if you had a cut or whatever, you wouldn't even know. You-- you'd totally disregard it, as your skin was black anyway, because, you know, you weren't-- you-- you couldn't go and wash. You couldn't take your clothes off. The-- the clothes that I wore, I wore for 12 months before there was a next delousing. So for 12 months, I never actually took my clothes off. And when I did, my skin was just gray. And it was covered in huge-- well, we used to call it [NON-ENGLISH], which is like red rashes and boils. Some of it went into boils, and some of it never healed, and you would just squeeze out the pus. You sort of rotted alive, in fact. You know, you-- there were no dressings or anything. You couldn't clean it up. Some people swore by washing in urine, because they said it was a good disinfectant. At-- at one stage, you-- you-- you actually use your bowl for your food, for your toilet, for your drink, all the same bowl, and you couldn't wash it out. So you know, conditions improved, shall we say, as time went on. Because they built one or two wash places, and they built one or two lavatories. But when I first came, there-- there was absolutely no access to water whatsoever. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] So that's more or less sums up-- oh, my health. Well, what happened next is I think it was-- let's think. I worked in-- I worked also in a place called the [NON-ENGLISH] kommando, which was just carrying shit. But it was within the enclosure of the camp, so it was better than working what they called [NON-ENGLISH], working far out in the fields, which you probably had to walk an hour or wherever to-- to-- to do this work. This was within the enclosure of the work. And it meant-- the-- now, I must just explain that the lavatories they built was like a long hut, and it had like an iron grid on the top and lots and lots of holes. I've actually got a picture of it if you ever-- we ever want to see it. And about, oh, 400 or 500 prisoners would go into there at one time and share a hole as a-- as a-- as a-- as a means of using a toilet. But of course, the stuff went into a pit. And eventually, this had to be emptied. And so there-- there was-- there formed a kommando called the [NON-ENGLISH] kommando. And you-- you carried, like, a yoke on the top here, with two buckets, one on each shoulder. And that-- and you-- you dug out the shit, put it in the buckets, and that was eventually emptied. So I-- I worked on the [NON-ENGLISH] kommando for some time. Well, it gave me access to lavatories, [LAUGHS] if nothing else. You know, that-- that-- that was a help. Because obviously, when you worked outside, and you had diarrhea, you-- you were just absolutely covered. And you-- there were no lavatories whatsoever. There was no means of going to any toilets. So that was a bonus, of course, to have access to at least-- There were three things you had to have access to, food, shit, and sleep, and everything else didn't really matter. If you could do these three things, you could live. And this you had to think of all day long and all night long as well. 
INT: I wanted to ask you about-- KHM: My health.  INT: --your bowl in [INAUDIBLE]. KHM: My what?  INT: Your-- the bowl that you used. KHM: The bowl, yes.  INT: You said you couldn't leave anything in the camp. Did you carry the bowl around? KHM: Yes, you carried the bowl. What you did is-- I had a red bowl. Most bowls were red, quite large red bowl. And you had it-- you had a-- a piece of string around your waist, and you had the bowl on your waist. Because if you didn't have your bowl, you can imagine. You couldn't get your soup. Nobody else would give you their bowl. If you didn't have a bowl, you didn't have your soup, and you didn't have your toilet.  INT: How big was the bowl? KHM: Well, quite large, quite a big bowl, yes. And you-- you were guarding your bowl very carefully.  INT: What was it made of? KHM: Oh, it was enamel. Just a red enamel, yes.  INT: Right. KHM: And you were lucky if you get a spoon. That you had to organize. You know, say, like everything else, you had to organize.  INT: Yes. KHM: Well, we were talking about my health. And I'll tell you what happened, one of the things that happened next. And I'm trying to recall when it happened. I would think my second year in Auschwitz.  INT: We're coming-- yes. We're coming to the end of this tape, so we'll take a break there. KHM: Yes. 
INT: Tape seven, Kitty Hart-Moxon, 9th of June, 1998. Is there any other work that you did before you went into Kanada in Auschwitz. KHM: Oh, yes, yes, yes. The next stage I think happened-- well, I-- I was also working in-- in a place called a Weberei, which is a spinning factory, because sometimes you couldn't escape until you were drafted into all kind of kommandos. And it was all virtually impossible to get out. But I will now talk about possibly the-- the latter part of 1943, coming towards the end of the year. I suddenly became ill. And as I said, you disregarded any ailments that you had. But at that's point I had a high fever, and I collapsed. Now nobody wanted to go into-- into the hospital compound, because there was always a danger of being selected to die, because selections were carried out there every day. But, of course, there was absolutely no-- no way I could stand up, or stand at roll call. And I was simply carted off onto the hospital compound. And all I remember is saying-- I think some of my friends took me, you know, because we were living in a little family together at that time. So they took me in. And I said, don't forget, my mother is on block-- I think she's on Block 12. Take me to Block 12, my mother. She's on Block 12. She was on the typhus block, you see. And that's where I landed up, on the typhus block, in my-- on my mother's block, fortunately. And of course, I had typhus, like everybody else. But like I've said to you before, we were immunized against typhus. And so luckily, I actually survived. But I was ill for three months. Now what happened was I was put on a-- a bunk, which was a bunk for two, but there were four of us. One had typhus. One had scarlet fever. One had diphtheria. And I think one had-- one-- I had-- all four of us had some other infectious disease. It was the infectious block. But we-- we didn't catch these other diseases, strangely enough. Although I-- I'd had never had diphtheria or scarlet fever, I didn't get it. But I had typhus. And-- well, of course, I had typhus, and there-- there-- there was no medication against typhus as such. And I totally lost consciousness. And I-- it is a well-known fact that you are delirious for weeks on end when you have typhus. And you have hallucinations, and you also-- you are completely deranged, which I was for apparently a long time. And I was screaming and shouting. And prisoners-- not prisoners-- women were tied to their bunks, because they wanted to get up and get out and-- and do all sorts of things which you were not allowed to do.
So I was tied to the bed. But I-- I was unconscious. I didn't recognize my mother. All-- all I was told afterwards is that I was screaming and shouting and-- and telling my mother how cruel she was, that she doesn't give me all the oranges and grapefruits that are lying at the bottom of my bed, and nobody wants to give it to me. All you wanted was drink, because in typhus you have got the most incredible black tongue or something, like a deposit on the tongue. And you can't close your mouth. And, of course, it affects all your organs inside. And all you want is drink. Anyway, I had this typhus. And I was delirious. And of course, there were selections on the-- on the block, selections for people to be taken to the crematoria. And my mother knew when there-- there-- there were going to be selections. And as soon as she realized there was going to be a selection, she would pick me up, put me underneath a straw mattress, put a straw mattress on top, and put a corpse on top-- a dead person on top. Because people died. The-- the-- the death toll was about 400 a day at that stage on-- on the infectious block. And hoping I would keep quiet. And-- and the selections were carrying out-- these selections were usual two or three times a week, and I was ill three months. Eventually, I recovered from typhus sufficiently. And I had complications of typhus. I had-- I had pneumonia, and I had inflammations in both my-- abscess in both my ears. You do get all kind of complication. All my hair fell out. By that time, my hair had grown quite a bit, but it all came out in clumps, and I was completely bald. And I was deaf-- completely deaf. And I-- and I had this pneumonia. And of course, there was no medication. But one of the prisoner doctors decided she's got to do something about my ears. So she pricked my ears with knitting needles to let the pus out, and then completely destroyed one of my eardrums. Anyway, I recovered from typhus. And I-- I was already sitting on the-- what was known as a heating channel off the bunk. Again, there were selections, but this time everybody had to get outside. And it was Mengele, who we know. Dr. Mengele, he carried out the selections. And I actually had to go through a process of selection immediately after typhus. And I was still crawling on my fours. And I-- I-- I recall that I had huge, huge knees, because all my-- the skin just hung loose, and I'd lost so much weight.
And-- and I just couldn't stand up. And my-- my mother said, now, you've got to run. It's no good just standing up. You've got to run. If he-- if he doesn't see you run, that's the end, you see. Because once you were selected, there was no way of saving people. It's very difficult to get people out of a selection once they were already ticked off and marked off and crosses made or whatever they used to do. Sometimes they used to just tick it off on the list, you see. And you were tattooed. You couldn't-- you couldn't get away. And I-- I stood up. And I actually ran in front of Mengele. And you know, he hesitated, because he turned me around twice. My body was all covered in sort of red blotches. But I ran. And-- and he just let me go. Because there were so many others that couldn't run. They were crawling. And so in comparison, I could-- I could already run. So I-- I passed this selection. That was the nearest I came to being selected. Because I passed several selections prior to that. But I-- I passed the selection when we had delousing sessions and things like that. But this was, as I said, the-- the-- the worst selection that I'd gone through. I began to recover. And-- but my memory didn't come back. You know, I was totally deranged still. I still didn't recognize my mother. And that went on for a long time. Apparently this is a long, long process before you get your-- your, you know, your senses back to normal. Well, eventually-- and-- and at that stage they were throwing people out back onto-- back to the camp. And my mother begged the block leader, and whoever they were, you know. Just needed another-- just let her recuperate another few days to see if she'd recover a little bit better. And eventually-- eventually we managed to persuade them to let me stay and actually work in the hospital by the time I recovered. Now that was almost towards the end of the 1943, I think. Yes, I think-- might have-- I-- I tell you what I do recall. I-- I remember my birthday in 1943, because my-- I was already, I think, working on another block. I was working on a diarrhea block-- the Durchfall block, in other words, yes? Well, there you were just carrying our bodies, because people died of diarrhea. They got like cholera type of diarrhea, and people died all the time. And my mother came to me. And she said, today is your birthday. Now I've got a present for you. And she brought me an onion-- a huge onion. And do you know, this was the most wonderful birthday present I thought I ever had. And, of course, I didn't even know it was my birthday. We didn't know dates. But she found out it was the first of-- first of December. And by that time, I was already on the staff, working first as a cleaner. And then I was working as a-- as a-- as a-- like as an orderly. And I was promoted as a nurse. But, of course, the nursing consisted only as carrying out the dead. So this is how I came to work in the hospital compound. And I worked there until about an incident that happened around about March. I worked in the hospital all that time. And, of course, food there was, you could say, in abundance, because the sick couldn't eat. They each got a bread ration, although that's all we had, was bread and soup. But nonetheless, it was-- there was enough bread. Soup, I must just say a word about the soup. My mother had access to all of the camp soup. And she would always maintain that if she ate a lot of the soup, something happened to her memory. She couldn't think straight. And she couldn't really converse properly. And a lot of my mother's work was talking to people, persuading them, you know. You must go on and things will get better. And trying to-- because you couldn't-- there were no medication, but just giving them moral support. And she found she couldn't think if she ate a lot of that soup. And much, much later, we-- we were discovered that poison was actually added to the camp soup, a kind of bromide. It was called Avo, or something, that some research had been done, that had been added to the soup. And that sort of made people subdued and obey and not rebel, and they couldn't think straight. And it was a slow poison. So we-- 
INT: When did you find out about that? KHM: That I found out well after the war.  INT: Oh, well after the war. KHM: Yes. But we knew there was something wrong with the soup, because it had the most peculiar effect on your mind, on your brain. You couldn't think properly. It slowed down all your movements in effect.  INT: Right. You mentioned Mengele earlier. KHM: Yes.  INT: Did you ever have any contact, or did you ever see any other infamous names? KHM: Oh, yes. While I worked-- while I worked in the hospital compound, we had all the-- all the SS doctors. The same SS doctors that carried out selections on the ramp were the same SS doctors that came into the hospital compound and also carried out selections inside.  INT: Any-- yeah. KHM: I can tell you names. Yes. I can tell you Dr. Konig, Dr. [NON-ENGLISH], [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] oh, Dr. Clauberg, and, of course, Mengele. There was about five of them. And quite a lot of the commandants came in as well. There was a Rapportführer Taube. There was commandant Hossler. Lots and lots of personnel came into the hospital compound. They practically left us alone, the-- the actual people working in-- in the hospital. We were not subjected to-- to the selections as such. And we were not also subjected to roll calls--  INT: Yes. KHM: --which was a big advantage.  INT: Did you ever have contact with any of the prisoners who had been subjected to medical experiments [INAUDIBLE]? KHM: No, there were no medical experiments carried out on our hospital blocks. All the experiments were carried out on the experimental block in Auschwitz 1. So therefore, we didn't actually have direct contact with the experimental station. The people that were working in Auschwitz 1 had contact. But what-- what happened one day is the-- the only contact I had was they were hunting, actually-- hunting people for blood, of all things. And that was almost prior to-- to me having typhus. And I was caught. There was like a mini-roundup, and I was caught and taken to the hospital compound. There was like a laboratory-- sort of laboratory. And they were taking blood. And they-- every-- everybody was actually had the-- had the blood taken off them-- rather a lot of blood, because we came out sort of dazed out of the place. So they took a lot of blood of us. And that was the only experimental-- medical experimental event that I actually experienced. It was then. There was a special block where all the experiments were carried out. And they didn't do them in Birkenau-- not-- not while I was there. Not on my two blocks, anyway. Well, not my mother's and not mine. 
INT: Right. Were you aware of the sequence of the Jewish calendar during your time in Auschwitz? KHM: Well, absolutely no idea of any sequence whatsoever. The-- you-- you didn't know the time of day, the day of the week, or the month of the year. You-- you knew it was hot and it was summer. And you knew it must have been winter, because it was snowing. You did know it was a Sunday, because groups didn't go out to-- to work on outside groups. They didn't take groups out. So we knew that was a Sunday.
