But you-- you-- the only way you knew there were dates is when newcomers came into the camp. And if they could tell you what day it was, well, that's how you caught up with the calendar. Sometimes some girls got to know it was Yom Kippur. And you knew because they were sitting praying, and somehow they got to know it-- it happened to be Yom Kippur. It was-- but by and large you didn't know. I-- I didn't know. I-- I had no idea of-- of, from day to day, I didn't know the time of day or the- or not the time of day either, by the way. You knew it must have been early in the morning and roll call time was pretty well your clock. And that is all you knew. And in the hospital you couldn't actually tell the-- the date whatsoever.  INT: Did you ever receive any mail? KHM: No, no, no. Nothing whatsoever. 
INT: Any parcels? KHM: No-- no-- no Jewish prisoners in Auschwitz received mail, not to my knowledge, unless there were some privileged prisoners working in Auschwitz 1, and in the political-- in the-- in the offices. But the ordinary, average Jewish prisoner didn't receive anything. And in any case, no-- no one knew I was in Auschwitz. None-- none of my relatives were alive by that time or wouldn't know-- my father didn't know where we were anyway, even if he'd still been alive.  INT: Did you ever have any cultural or educational activities? KHM: I'm afraid all-- all your time was spent with the three occupations-- shit, sleep, and eat. And if you could just concentrate your mind to that, everything else you had to forget.  INT: Right. KHM: Privileged prisoners, yes, they indulged in all kinds of parties, and cultural things. I think there was a time on one of the blocks where things were reasonably quiet for a period, where some girls tried to stage a play. But I don't-- I'm not-- not at that stage. In-- in the Kanada we-- we managed some cultural activities, of all places. That's-- that's where we managed it. But not-- not-- not at that time, and not in Auschwitz, 1943.  INT: Would you ever hear any music? KHM: Yes, we heard a lot of music. Because we had--  INT: How did that come about? KHM: At the gate of B compound was a piece of ground. And there was an orchestra which were compiled of women prisoners. This orchestra was led by a woman conductor named Alma Rosé. She was the niece of a very famous composer-- Gustav Mahler. And she was only really part Jewish. But nonetheless, she-- she was brought into Auschwitz. And she was given the job of organizing this orchestra. And, of course, this is how some of the girls managed to survive very well. Because they were part of the-- a part of the orchestra. Some could play instruments. Some were singers. Most of the pieces that-- and what they had to do is they had to play marches first thing in the morning when groups of prisoners were taken out through the camp to work. They also had to play when they were coming back in. So you heard the music twice a day-- mostly marches. The music was usually directed by people-- by-- by the SS, who said what they had to play. Occasionally, they were allowed to give concerts on a Sunday. And then all prisoners, because they were not going out to work on a Sunday, were allowed to listen to-- to the concert, with the orchestra. The male orchestra also existed. So there-- there were orchestras in-- in the camp, both in the-- in-- in the male section and the female section. 
INT: Who was-- KHM: My mother knew-- my mother knew her very well, Alma Rosé, because she had typhus, and she came onto my mother's block. And my mother actually nursed her during her typhus. And she-- she survived typhus, and she went back to conduct. And then something incredible happened. They were actually going to release her, because she was only part Jewish, or so many percent. I don't know. And she was very respected and very highly regarded. And it was decided they were going to set her free. Now there was a conspiracy among the German ruling prisoners who became very jealous of her. And it is said, but I don't-- haven't witnessed it-- it is said that they made a party for her prior to her release. And she was invited to this party. And the next thing is she was poisoned. Because the next thing that happened is my mother saw her being brought back to her block. And she actually died. So she was poisoned by her privileged class of prisoners. This is-- this is what we've been told. There were-- and-- and the most amazing thing after that is that her body wasn't taken to the crematorium, but she was given a-- a proper funeral-- the only person in Auschwitz that was given a funeral.  INT: Was she buried? KHM: She-- I believe so. I don't know. I believe so. But we-- we didn't witness it. But this is what's been said. Mm-hmm. 
INT: Who was responsible for any discipline amongst the inmates? KHM: The discipline among the inmates was all carried out by the inmates themselves. The-- the-- the ruling party of prisoners had the-- held the whole place in an iron grip. The whole place was disciplined by the ruling hierarchy of prisoners, who above them had, of course, the SS hierarchy. But the prisoner hierarchy carried out the-- many of the discipline themselves.  INT: Do you remember any names of these people? KHM: You mean of the kapos? Well, I know the name of my block leader, or-- or one of the blocks. And her name was [? Berta. ?] She was-- came from Hungary. Our camp leader, lager kapo, [INAUDIBLE] lager, she was the highest in-- in the-- at my time, her name was Orli-- O-R-L-I. Orli. And she was a German, true German political prisoner. She was a communist. And she'd been imprisoned for many long-- many years. So she was the-- she was the-- the lager kapo-- the Lagerführer really. I don't know who was the lager kapo. The lager kapos were normally the criminal category of prisoners, rather than the political ones.  INT: And were people punished at all? KHM: Oh, punishments were carried out all the time. Maybe I'll give you one example of my own punishment, which took place, I would say, probably in the summer of 1943. I was on the block of [? Berta, ?] by the way, at that time. I think it was Block 20. And for some unknown reason, my mother was also on that block. Because like I said to you, sometimes you were transferred for no reason at all. And my mother was what was known as the-- at that time, torwache. She was on watch, so people didn't get out at night, because there was a curfew. After roll call, everybody was shut-- shut in, and you could no longer get out of your hut. That was immediately after roll call. There was no more going around. So-- but what happened was that I had-- I worked in the field at that time, and smuggled in some potatoes. And I was determined to cook these potatoes. And, of course, we-- and now and again we managed to make a fire in-- in what was known as the heating channel. At one end it was like a stove, and you could heat this. And if you were lucky, and you had a pot or something, you'd put your potatoes in there and they'd cook. But of course, we didn't have anything to make fire. And I'd spotted the pile of wood somewhere outside the block. So I sneaked out, and I persuaded my mother to let me out.
And she was on guard. And she let me out. And I went out, and I crawled along the side-- well, there was a curfew on, you see. And there was no-- no one patrolling such as patrols within the camp. But there were searchlights all around the camp. And, you know, you were likely to be picked up by the searchlights. And I got to this pile of wood. And I-- I grabbed a pile of wood. And I actually managed to get this wood. And on my way back, I was picked out by a searchlight. And I could feel this, the searchlight was following me from-- from the watchtower somewhere. Because there were watchtowers. And there were the guards in the watchtowers. And I was picked out by one of the searchlights. So I dropped this wood and started running back to my block. And I-- I was followed. I was chased. And what I didn't know is I was chased by one of the SS women. I was actually chased by her. And she saw which block I got in, although she didn't see me. And-- and I immediately got onto my bunk and hid. But I was followed. And she said, who was outside? And, of course, I didn't own up. But then she said, if the person doesn't own up, the whole block is going to be punished. That was a thousand people inside, yeah? So eventually, I had to own up. So I did own up. I said, I-- I did. I went to try and to get some wood to make a fire. She said, all right. She'd went out, took my number, and went out. The next morning, there was a roll call, and my number was called out. And I-- And this SS woman, her name was Borman. In fact, I've got a picture of her somewhere, because I've got picture of all the SS women that were eventually captured in Belsen, because they all were transferred to Belsen. And she was one of the one that was captured in Belsen. Borman, a tiny little woman. And she said-- she pulled me out. No, she called out my number at roll call. Oh, first of all, I got a hiding off the-- the Blockalteste. And then my mother got a hiding for letting me out. It was only after some people intervened so that they let her go. And I was pulled out at roll call. And I was told I was going to get 25 lashes as a punishment. So I was taken to another part of the compound, which was called, for short-- SK-- Strafkompanie. That was just a-- a small enclosure where all people that were being-- that were punished, like a punishments enclosure, were being flogged. And this is where I got 25 lashes. But she didn't give it to me. It was one of the-- it was one of the SS, Rapportführer Taube, he gave me the 25 lashes. And of course, if you think one whip wasn't one lash. But one whip had about 20 prongs. So you, when you got the first lash, you already had 25 lashes. And you had to count. But I don't know how far I got. But I-- I-- I was unconscious by that time. And I was actually left for dead, because they'd-- they'd thought they killed me. But eventually-- eventually somebody came and collected me. They-- they were collecting heaps of bodies there. And I was already thrown on a pile of bodies. But I-- I was collected. And I don't know. My mother arranged for somebody to collect me, and my friends. And they brought me back. And-- and what's more, they brought me back on another block, because I couldn't go back onto the same block. Because she would have known that I had come back. So there was a lot of wrangling going on. And eventually I-- I-- I got onto a different block. And that-- that's-- there was a lot of punishment like that, a lot of punishment dished-- dished out for various-- If you were caught writing, that was one of the biggest crimes. If you were caught talking to a man who purposely called to the other side of the fence, or if you were caught talking to-- to the arrivals as they were passing on the ramp, that was strictly forbidden. We're not allowed to communicate whatsoever. 
INT: Were you ever witness to any torture? KHM: Well, in the Kanada. Probably in the Kanada. Talk about that--  INT: Yes. KHM: What we've seen.  INT: Yes. Yes. Is there anything else you wanted to say about the time before Kanada? Before we move-- KHM: Well, I'll just say a word about these selections.  INT: Yes. KHM: When selections were carried out within the camp, the-- the-- the women that were being selected to die were all taken into one enclosure, which was known as Block 25. Now the women were put in there, and the-- the windows were barred there. So you couldn't-- you-- you couldn't actually communicate-- you could no longer communicate with anyone that was taken onto Block 25.  INT: And you'd been in there? KHM: No, I've not been a Block 25.  INT: No. KHM: No, you couldn't-- never get anybody out of Block 25. It was almost impossible to get people out of there. By that time, they were all sealed in there. The-- the whole block had an enclosure with a-- with a-- with a-- it had a yard with a brick walls around it. And you really couldn't get out of there. And nobody could get in. There-- these people were no longer fed. And sometimes they were there three or four days. And then-- and, you know, as selections were carried out, and that filled up, then they brought in the lorries. And then they took people to the crematoria. So that's where they put them first, when people were being selected all around the camp. So maybe that concludes the selections. 
INT: Yeah. All right, thank you very much. We're nearing the end of that tape. KHM: Ah, yes. All right. KHM: What?  INT: Tape seven? CREW: No, eight  INT: Tape eight, Kitty Hart-Moxon, 9th of June. 1998. We were at about March '44 or the beginning of the year 1944. What happened to you then? KHM: Well, yes. I think we'll talk about March 1944. I was still working on the [NON-ENGLISH] block in-- in the hospital compound. And as I said, selections were carried out all the time. And one day, they decided on-- Mengele, rather, decided that this diarrhea block has to be emptied completely, of all prisoners. They all had to be selected to die. Now, many of them were already convalescent. And there were also two girls that were with me, came with me from prison, two sisters. They had typhus, and they had diarrhea. They were on this block. They were already convalescent and hiding there still before being thrown out, back onto the camp. And without any warning whatsoever came a roundup of this one block. Well, they may have rounded up others, but this, I'm talking about the-- the diarrhea block. And they decided they were going to empty the whole block, because there was an epidemic of diarrhea or typhus or whatever, and they had to fill it again. So they decided all the prisoners that are there, all that are sick and all that are convalescent, didn't matter who they were, they were all going to die. And I was already on the staff. And we had to empty the block. We had to take the people out and put them onto the lorries. The ones that couldn't-- couldn't get up onto the lorries, we had to take two-- two people to a person, hands and feet, and we had to actually throw them onto the lorries. And I actually had to help my two friends onto the lorries. And believe you me, that was a day I wanted to die. That was a day I really wanted to go with my friends. Because I just couldn't see a way-- At that time, I was still pretty well convalescing. I was-- I was still not 100% fit. I was-- I'd lost a lot of weight, and I couldn't think straight. And I really almost lost my will to fight, lost my will to live. Although it was a good sort of place to be, because I was undercover. But nonetheless, I was carrying out bodies all day long. And here were my friends that I now had to help to die. But my mother saw what was going on, and she grabbed me while this was going on. And the lorries drove off. And that was the end of the whole of the block. And there we were, perhaps a dozen of us who were sort of working on this block, including the prisoner doctors who were working there and the nurses and the two orderlies, and we were just left there with this empty block. And they hadn't brought any new people in. And we didn't know what was going to happen to us. And then, perhaps it was 24 hours later, one of the kapos came-- I don't know who she was-- with one of the German warders [NON-ENGLISH]. And she said, well girls, we are forming a new Kanada Kommando. Because there was already what was known as an old Kanada Kommando. They were girls working in Auschwitz I. 
INT: What did Kanada mean? KHM: Well, Kanada was just a nickname for a [NON-ENGLISH]. A [NON-ENGLISH] was effects. In other words, the-- you know, all the possessions of people. We didn't know what it meant at that stage. We knew the word Kanada means that there is some-- abundance of everything. What we didn't know, we-- we gathered, it's abundance of what possessions people brought that were brought into the camp. There's-- all their possessions had to be left behind at-- at the ramp, where they were unloaded. And this SS woman, the kapo, said, we're forming this new kommando. They're going-- there's going to be a day shift and a night shift. You can volunteer, or you can stay here. Now, we had some hours to decide. Don't ask me how that happened, but we did. And my mother said, look, you must go. We don't know what there is. We suspected it is near the crematoria. You know, we-- we didn't quite know what was-- what we were going to see. We knew there was going to be food there. And my mother said, look, I'm afraid this is the only way you are going to go on. If you can eat, just one day, of what you want, and if you can sleep, and if you can keep yourself clean, you must go. We don't know what's going to happen in the future. We might not survive here, we might not survive there. That is today. You must go. And so I did. I joined the Kanada-- I was allowed to join the Kanada Kommando. And the next thing that happened, the day after that, we were-- this was a whole group of 400 of us. Or about-- maybe it was just the night shift. Because I was on night shift. Perhaps the day shift was there already. Perhaps it was just the night shift they were organizing, I don't know. But there was a day shift and a night shift. And I-- I was on the night shift. So we-- we were marched out, just the group of us. And we were marched through the gates and passing fences and more fences and wires and enclosures, as far as the eye could see, which we hadn't seen before. Going right through different camps. And there was the-- what was known as a familienlager, and there was the-- the-- [NON-ENGLISH] lager which was familienlager, like a-- a-- people that came from Theresienstadt, they all brought them in together at first. Then there was the Gypsy camp there. And then there was a men's enclosure. There were all kind of camps that we passed. And eventually we came to this very small enclosure.
Now what we didn't know is we were-- up-- up to that point, the Kanada, the old Kanada, they used to work in the Kanada and come back to the camp at night. But what we were told, that we are going to be isolated. We will no longer be able to come back to the camp. So we were taken to this enclosure known as BIIg. That's what it was known as, BIIg, Kanada, BIIg. And that was where we were enclosed. And it was quite a small enclosure. I mean, as camps go, it was a small enclosure. It perhaps had about 30 huts or so, wooden huts. Now, first thing that greeted us was-- [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] well, there were a lot of poplar trees at the bottom of the camp, and we sort of passed some of these poplars. And then we passed a low brick building with very tall chimneys. And we passed two of these, and we saw another-- we-- we saw four of these brick buildings, with four tall chimneys. And then we passed a low building, an L-shaped building, which was the sauna, known as the sauna. And then we came to this enclosure of the camp. Now just beyond the sauna, there were a lot of trees. In fact, it was a little forest of birch trees. And that is the very first time I realized that the name Birkenau is actually derived from Birke is a birch tree, and [NON-ENGLISH] in Polish is-- is-- is-- is the birch trees. It's a birch-- birch wood. And the whole part was called Birkenau because of these birch trees. They're very distinctive trees with sort of white-gray rims, proper birch wood. We also saw some lawns, laid out lawns. Now, we were taken, again, to a sauna at first, then deloused. It's the normal procedure. And then-- oh, and-- and perhaps I'll just describe the camp. There was a-- a very small enclosure with two huts. And this is where the whole of the kommando was to be housed, in two huts, the first two huts. Well, the huts were wooden huts, and they had windows. And the one window on the side had-- had direct view of crematorium 4. So we had a direct view of the crematorium. We couldn't have-- we didn't have the view of crematorium 5, which was at the back of crematorium 4, but you couldn't actually see it. We didn't have a view of crematorium 3 and 2, which was the-- pretty well the other side of the-- of the-- on the left-hand side of the fence. But we had a direct view of the one crematorium and a direct view of the sauna, where people were being brought in and selected. You know, selections to the camp or selected-- selected to die. The next thing that hit us was there were some 30 huts. They had no windows. They had sort of slots at the top. They were, in fact, [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] horse barracks or something, designed for horses, not designed for people. But they housed goods at that time, of course. Our two huts had, actually, windows. I'm talking about the first two huts. The men were housed at the very end, on the opposite side. They also had two huts. There was a men Kanada kommando, of men, who were housed at the opposite end, so we had no contact with them whatsoever. At the bottom of-- as I say, there were two rows or three rows, I don't remember now, of huts. Right at the bottom, the first thing that struck us when we reached that small enclosure of the camp, there was the most enormous pile. By-- I mean enormous, I think it was as high as a two or three-story building. And that was heaped up with goods of all description. There were suitcases opened up and jumbled up. There were toys. There were photographs. There were men's clothes, women's clothes, shoes, everything was jumbled up in this enormous, huge pile, because all the huts were already full with goods. Now, these obviously were the possessions that people brought in. And everything was thrown onto this heap. And we were brought there to do the sorting out of all the goods. So this is the start of my Kanada Kommando time. And I was there March, April, '44 until middle of October of 1944. So about eight months. So we now start on the Kanada enclosure. I can tell you about the work, et cetera. 
INT: Right. Can you tell me about a typical day? KHM: Well, I was on night shift. So-- well, first of all, the first thing that happened was that the SS woman that was in charge of-- we had a kapo called Manci. She was a Hungarian-- a Slovakian or Hungarian girl. She was very good to us. And we also had a-- a-- a-- a-- the SS woman who was in charge. I don't remember her name. The personnel, incidentally, in-- SS personnel in that enclosure was incredibly small. There were only about four SS people in charge of that whole enclosure, one or two women. As I said, we had an SS woman who was in charge of us. And she decided that her group, that is, we, were to wear navy blue dresses with white spots. But of course we were not issued with these. So everybody just scrambled in this huge, huge, enormous pile of clothes to find something that was Navy blue with white spots. And this is what we did. And then we had to paint red crosses on our backs, because everybody had to-- all the Jewish prisoners had to have red crosses on their backs. And these were-- we kitted out in this. And I think we also had white handkerchiefs on our heads. I cannot recall what we wore on our heads anymore. And-- and we were divided into day shift and to night shift. So I was working-- all the eight months, I was working during the night. Now, during the day, we were allowed into the hut to sleep. And we normally slept till the early afternoon, so we pretty well could observe what went on for the rest of the day, because we were just sitting. But we were in a very, very small enclosure, just two huts and the fence surrounding it. So we had-- we-- we couldn't get out. We had no view other than from this very small enclosure. [PAUSES FOR 4 SECONDS] The work, there were different duties that people-- people did. You had girls that had to go to the big heap at the bottom, load-- jumble up in-- onto little hand carts, and bring it into the hut that you were working and throw it at the end of the hut, which again had a huge, huge heap, right up to the ceiling. Then you had other girls' duties, who would go to this big heap, and they would have to sort stuff that they were going-- they-- they had to find stuff that they were going to sort. So you were allocated exactly what you had to do. Now, at one time, I had to sort-- well, I think I sorted corsets at one time, because I found so much jewelry.
And at one time, I sorted men's jackets. So-- because you sorted different things. Now, what you had to do is you-- you had to go to this heap, find all men's jackets, bring them, or sometimes even the girls who who were doing the, you know, the ferrying to and fro, would bring you these jackets. And you then had to put these jackets on the table. Say I was sorting jackets. You put them on the table, and you had to sometimes slit seams, because you-- you knew that there was stuff inside. You had to empty all the pockets. There were photographs of people, of course. But very often, there were a lot of valuables there. And next to your table, you had a bucket. And into this bucket-- and sometimes you had a-- a blanket. You had to throw all the valuables in there that you found. When I was sorting corsets, again-- and I'll tell you about-- they had to sort jackets. You had to fold these jackets, sort them, and make a pile. I can't remember, perhaps it was a dozen. Then you had to bundle them up, and you'd put your number on there, what-- who-- who you-- who you-- were as the sorter. And this you had to hand in at another table. And there, again, sat a group of women, and they would tick off. Because you had a quota of how many bundles you had to hand in during a shift. Now, the-- the thing was, if you could do it really quickly, and you could find enough men's jackets or whatever, and you could do it quickly, you were finished quickly. And then we used to take it in turns and sleep in a big heap and very often observe what went on in the other half of the hut, because we were sort of-- it was a wooden hut, and you could see through the spokes what was going on in the other half of the hut. So very often, we used to observe. Because it was a SS hut at the other end. You know, the SS enclosure. And we used to watch what they were up to. Because they used to sort out the jewelry there and things like that. And they used to-- mostly used to sit and drink. Now, all the food was in a separate hut, and then you had girls who worked in this food, sorting all the food. The food was rotting away there, you can imagine. And it depended where people were bought from. I-- I remember transports, say, from Italy or Greece. And they were just full of this canvas bottles of-- of wine.
Because they used to bring a lot of wine in. And they were just lying there, and then the SS used to get drunk on-- on these wines. Now, we had no access, so we couldn't actually go there and take any food. But again, what happened is we lived in little families. In fact, one of the girls that was with me in Kanada, [? Batsheva ?] is in Israel now, lives in Israel. Now, you probably know [? Batsheva. ?] Well, [? Batsheva ?] and I lived together on one bunk for eight months in the Kanada. And I'm not sure she didn't work with me on the night shift, because the idea was everybody did something else. So we could organize, and food wasn't dished out. So you had to help yourself to-- to-- again, to the food. And you had to steal it. You were not allowed to have it. So it was very useful to, again, work in different places. Some girls worked in the sauna, so you-- you had access to water. And so this is how you lived there in this Kanada on the night shift. When it came to-- I'll explain to you-- when it came to sorting underwear and corsets, we usually came upon the most incredible [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] amount of diamonds and jewelry and dollars and whatever. And of course we didn't want to hand it in. Yes, we had to hand in-- there was an awful lot of amount that had to be handed in. Because we couldn't keep it. There was nowhere to put anything. If we were caught with the most minute piece, you would have been-- it was a death sentence. So it wasn't-- it was much too dangerous to have anything that you could keep. In-- in the hut you couldn't keep anything. There were-- in fact, there were constant searches and inspections of the beds to see if anybody's hiding anything. But nonetheless, at the end of our hut, there was, like, a little wooden hut, and that was a toilet. And many of us, with our bare hands, buried a lot of stuff somewhere there, behind the toilets, when-- when nobody could watch. Because we-- we-- we were very visible as well. We were visible from the crematorium side, you see? So it-- it was a very dangerous procedure. What else we did, we did know, of course, at that stage already, that there was an underground movement. There was a resistance movement somewhere. We didn't know who these people were. We didn't know how to contact them. Although one girl, I knew a girl called Roza Robota, who was one of the four girls that was eventually hanged for supplying explosives. Now, somewhere or other, we made contact with this girl, because she, at that time, already worked in the munition factory. And we did manage to-- to have contact with this girl. And we did manage to pass to her quite a lot of the jewelry, things that were inconspicuous, like small diamonds. You know, things that you could actually handle easily, not great big watches or anything like that. And part of this wealth found itself to the resistance. I wasn't the only one. Of course we all were at it. And part of this-- with-- with this wealth, with this jewelry, some of the Ukrainian SS were bribed. And some of the men eventually managed to obtain arms. The men, that is. We didn't have any arms in-- in our enclosure. But some of the men did. And some of the men of the Sonderkommando, you see, who worked inside the crematorium-- we had no contact with the Sonderkommando men. But now and again, we had contact with the Kanada males. Because they used to bring stuff in from the ramp. You know, they used to come in with the loads of stuff, bringing it in. So if we could possibly have-- What I mean, contact, you couldn't actually speak to them, as such. At one stage, there was one occasion where I had found a rather a large amount of jewelry, which I had to dispose of very quickly. And there was a group of men, and they were having their lunch brought to them. But they were guarded by one of the SS guards. And I-- I went up to one of the guards and said, can I just give some soup to-- to one-- one of the men. And I don't know. He said something or something. And while he turned round, I dropped a load of jewelery into the soup, which was a great big container of soup. And of course I couldn't tell the man what was there. But by just, you know, sort of shaking my head, et cetera, the man realized what was there. And it was a lot of jewelry that I managed to pass on to the men. And part of this jewelry eventually-- eventually contributed to the blowing up of the crematoria, I'm glad to say-- indirectly, of course, not directly. 
INT: Right. You-- you mentioned that one of the girls died during that time. How did that come about? KHM: Many, many things happened during that time. First of all, I explained to you that I was on night shift. So I didn't really know what went on during the night, except that we had direct view of Crematorium 4, and smoke was billowing out of Crematorium 4 all night, and so were the flames. But during the day, during the day, we could observe everything that went on after we slept, you know, after our night shift, after our sleep, sort of 3:00 or 4:00 in the afternoon. We were usually left in peace to just-- we had a little lawn there in the front, and we-- in fact, it was a very hot summer, and we were allowed to use water and splash ourselves, and we actually had, like I said to you, we-- there was actually where we made a bit of music. At the same time, there were hundreds and hundreds of people sitting in full view of us at the other side of the enclosure, outside the crematorium. Men, women, and children sitting underneath the trees in the woods. What they didn't know is they were actually queuing, waiting their turn to go into Crematorium 4. We didn't see Crematorium 5, but Crematorium 4. We also saw people pass by, both from Crematorium 3 and Crematorium 4 when they were being-- when-- when they were carrying out selections. They went into-- some-- some of the people they selected there and then, when they didn't carry out selections on the ramp. Sometimes selections were carried out just outside the crematorium, sometimes even inside the crematorium. They'd pull people out, when there were one or two young people. We saw them go into the sauna, where sometimes they got a-- a change of clothes and were taken into the camp, and others were put waiting in the woods. What they didn't know is they were waiting to queue into the crematorium. What these people thought is they were waiting to go to be disinfected. They saw us lying around on the lawn, sitting there, well-dressed. We had no communications with these people. Although we were within shouting distance, we could not communicate with these people, under sentence of death. There was no way we could speak to any of these people. Occasionally, one could say, where from. And on one occasion, it was a transport from the Lódz ghetto, one of my group, one of the girls, came from the Lódz ghetto, and she recognized her father. And she saw him go into the crematorium. And this is when she committed suicide. So this is one of the girls that died. But there were horrendous things outside the crematorium. 
INT: How did she commit suicide? KHM: Actually, she went on the fence, as simple as that. If you touched the electric fence, you were dead anyway, because it was high tension. What I didn't tell you is when we finished making the bundles, all these bundles were taken into the sauna. And there they had huge drums, sort of, I don't know, delousing drums. And all these bundles were put into these drums, and they were disinfected. And then they had another group of girls that were loading all this stuff onto lorries. And all this-- all these belongings were then shipped into Germany. And these big containers, I saw last year when I went back into Auschwitz. They were still there. The whole place was out-of-bounds. The whole sauna was closed. But now they are opening it up, and they are restoring part of the building. And I managed to get in before it was restored, and it was just as if you'd stepped back 50 years, and it was all there, intact. You could still see the pipes and the hot water system going to these big autoclaves, where they were disinfecting the clothes, by steam, I think. Now we come to some horrendous scenes that we actually witnessed.  INT: Yes, can you tell us about those? KHM: Now, as I said to you, we had all-- most of the afternoons to see what was going on. Now, most of us could no longer take it in. Because all day and all night, there was the stench of burning flesh and the stench of huge smoke billowing into the camp that make the whole place look dark. And at the same time, you had red flames coming out of four chimneys. And we only had, really, a view of the one chimney. But we had a direct view of the person who was placing the gas into the gas chamber. So we actually saw an SS man, person, personnel, part of what they're known, the disinfectors-- that's what they were called-- picking up tins of Zyklon B, going up on the ladder, opening up a flap in the roof, and emptying the tin into this chamber, which we had never been inside. Emptying the tin. And he wore a gas mask. And then came straight down, and he usually disposed of the can just outside. The hole place was littered with the Zyklon B cans. And soon after that, soon after that, you could hear a muffled sound of people screaming. And that lasted, depending on how many people there were, how much gas they were. That was all worked out by the SS doctors, how much gas was needed to kill a certain amount, a certain number of people. So once this died down, soon after that noise died down, we could see the smoke billowing out of the chimneys. And soon after that, we actually saw the men of the Sonderkommando who had to empty the gas chamber and burn the corpses. And pretty well soon after that, you actually watched the ash being emptied at the back of Crematorium 4. There was a sort of a pond. And the-- a lot of the ash was emptied there, into the pond. And at the same time, you had groups of people sitting, picnicking in the woods, waiting to go in. Now, they may not have been aware of the sound the way we were. The sounds were muffled. But nonetheless, you could hear a lot of screaming. Now, we don't know what these people thought, or what they didn't think. They, at that stage, had no idea they were going to die. 
INT: Do you have a name for any of these people who actually did some activity towards-- what was that name? KHM: Activity--  INT: To do with the Zyklon B. KHM: The Zyklon B. No. All the SS doctors that I mentioned to you, they were all connected with-- with working out the-- the dosages. The man who was in charge of the crematorium enclosure was Oberscharfuhrer Moll, M-O-L-L. And as I said, there were only about four personnel who worked in this enclosure. One was Gottfried Weise. And we know about-- we shall hear about him when he comes to the trial. Which we didn't-- I didn't know his name. We nicknamed him Schleppy, the blender. A man called Wunsch, and a man called [NON-ENGLISH].  INT: Right. KHM: And there might have been one or two others.  INT: Yes. We'll take a break there--  INT: Tape nine, Kitty Hart-Moxon, the sixth-- sorry-- ninth of June, 1998. KHM: [INAUDIBLE].  INT: You were talking about-- on the last tape, you were talking about your observation of activities around Crematorium 4. And you were mentioning some names that you-- KHM: Yes. Like I've explained before, the-- the SS personnel was very-- a very small number. I would say there were about four or five of them. There was a man called Wunsch. There was a man called [NON-ENGLISH], and there was a man called, what we nicknamed him as [? Schleppy, ?] which means the Blinde, because he had a glass eye. His real name was Gottfried Weise, which I didn't know at the time. These men all patrolled, sort of, where these people were sitting, waiting to die. And we witnessed individual murders there which were gratuitous, I would say, because these people were all waiting to die. However, there were-- these-- there-- there was especially that man, Weise or Blinde, who was known as the William Tell. Because his specialty was placing cans on children's shoulders. Also prisoners shoulders but, I'm talking about outside Crematorium 4. He-- he would place them on children's shoulders and onto their heads and then shoot at them. And invariably, he would kill them. So he would indulge in this sort of game outside-- just on the outside of the crematorium. I also saw children running out of the queue. Sometimes they were queueing in-- into the crematoria. Sometimes they were sitting, picnicking. But there were also children running around. I once saw a child picking a flower. And one of these SS officers-- now, I don't know which one it was, because I really didn't always-- could recognize them. but the one of the four, he picked up this child by its feet, and he smashed its head against the crematorium wall. Now, I don't know which-- it was one of the four of them. Now, that was the sort of individual gratuitous murder that went on. Now, one day-- well, it happened on many occasions. But I-- I recall one day, there was a child that begged for water. And somebody threw a bottle of water, and it came from our enclosure. And of course, that was strictly against the rules. But whoever this SS officer was-- and it turned out it was actually the Blinde, but I didn't see-- I didn't actually see his face. He picked up this child, and he threw it in the air, and he then speared it on his bayonet. So he killed it in front of its mother. Now, it was totally unnecessary, because these people were going to die in the next 10, 15 minutes. These were sort of murders that these people committed on the people that were going to die. Now, no one owned up from my group. Eventually, eventually a-- a-- a peasant had to own up. Because he said, if no one owns up, every fifth person is going to get shot. And in fact, he did kill some of them. Well, I don't know whether he killed, but they were actually taken out and killed. I don't know if it was the person that actually threw the water. Because again, as I say, I was on night shift, and a lot of these things happened during the day. And there-- there were-- there-- there were all kind of murders perpetrated in front of Crematorium 4 that-- that I saw while I was actually waiting to go on my night shift. 
INT: Did you ever have any opportunities of-- of responding to this at all? KHM: No. The-- the-- the-- the way to respond to any of this was to pretend you didn't see it. Because if you acknowledged that you saw it, and they-- the-- the SS people realized you saw it, you would have been next. So it-- the-- the thing to do is to just disappear from sight. So you were totally invisible. You didn't see it. And you tried to make yourself believe that you didn't actually see it, because you didn't really want to believe it. If you had taken in everything you saw, you-- you-- you would have committed suicide the next day or the same day. Because you-- you couldn't-- your brain just couldn't take it in. So the answer is you pretended you didn't see it.  INT: Did you see your mother during this time at all? KHM: Now, what happened there is that my mother was-- was still working in the main camp, B-- B2b in the hospital, and I had no contact. But now and again, we were permitted to take some sort of third-rate clothing back into the sauna, in the main camp, for the newcomers. And one or two people were allowed to accompany this-- this cart. And I was very friendly with the kapo. She was a very good kapo to us, actually. She helped us along, very much so. And she always made sure that I could go, because I have my mother there, you know. And it was a rare event, so she made sure that I could go. Now, in order to go back-- to go back, we used to carry stuff. We were not allowed to carry anything. Of course, you had this cart with the clothes that you took in. But we ourselves were not allowed to carry anything. We were not allowed to possess anything. But we used to-- well, I used to-- put on about four or five different layers of clothes and hang stuff at the back of my-- right over the back, with safety pins. Sometimes, I used to carry tins under my arms and tins between my legs and smuggle stuff into the camp. And that way, I would go and visit my mother. And on one visit, I found that she actually had typhus. And luckily, I managed to bring in enough stuff to pay for her to be looked after. And so if-- if I hadn't been able to bring stuff in, she wouldn't have been looked after. And if she hadn't been looked after, you know, she would have been selected to die. So she was hidden during her typhus. But on one-- on one or two occasions, our SS woman, warder, decided she was not going to take us along the normal route going in between the different camps to the main camp. She said, I'm going to take you to the outer perimeter, through the outer perimeter of the camp. Because the-- the camp was surrounded by different perimeters. And that was out of bounds to prisoners. But-- but she said, if you do see anything, you must look down. You must not look where you're going. And it was only a small group of us with this cart. And we went to the outskirts, to the-- to the outer perimeter, through the little wood, where all these people were sitting, waiting to die. And somewhere there at the back was a white house. Now, we knew there was a white house, because you used to sort of think we saw it from the distance. We never knew what there was. But in fact, what there was, while we were passing, there were men lined up to go inside that white house. And they didn't know what was going to happen to them. Each one was shot in the back of the neck. We could hear very, very faint thuds, like a shot. And then further on, as we walked on, we actually saw pits. And we saw the men of the Sonderkommando burning bodies in pits. Because by that time, I think it was May, June, 1944, and they were bringing in all the Hungarian people, you know? And there were, I don't know. They brought in half a million people. And they simply couldn't cope with disposing of all these people. And they were actually burning-- burning bodies. And I'm not sure if they were not burning people alive in-- in the pits. And they were-- they were carrying a lot of brushwood and making these great big piles, and burning people in the pits. So this is what we were not supposed to see. And this is what we actually saw on several occasions, going back into the camp. 
INT: What was that like for you? KHM: We were not allowed to see it. We were not allowed to look at it. We were just-- we were told to look down. But we did actually see. We did manage to see some-- some of the things that went on there. We managed to-- to witness. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Now, this-- I think we were talking about summer of 1944. Now, when it came to around about August '44, rumors began to circulate that they were going to dispose of the whole camp. They were going to evacuate the whole of Auschwitz. Rumors began to circulate that the Russian front was closing in. We didn't know where the front was at that time. We had absolutely no contact whatsoever. But rumors circulated nonetheless. And we knew there was an underground movement. And-- and we knew that they were already evacuating people from Auschwitz. And transports were not coming as frequently as they used to. And we just wondered what was going to happen to us, because we couldn't believe that they were going to evacuate us who had actually seen what went on. Because during that time, we could have easily seen a million people die, which is a difficult thing to take in. But day in, day out, there-- 10 trains arrived, full of people. And most of them were taken straight to the gas chambers. And in fact, there came an order for the total liquidation of the camp. This has already filtered out by September. Now, what happened next was totally unexpected. We were still sorting clothes, because there was this-- this enormous backlog of stuff with all these people from Hungary that brought an enormous wealth with them in jewelry and clothes and furs and you name it. And it was all had to be-- still had to be sorted. So we knew there was still quite a lot of work for us, but transports were no longer arriving. They were bringing in people from different camps now. They were bringing in people from Majdanek, disposing of them in-- in the crematoria. So they were liquidating some of the camps. And they were also liquidating some of Auschwitz, we-- we realized. The next thing that happened was on a day-- the date I do know, which we got to know it later, was the sixth of October. And suddenly there was what we thought was a bombing. Because the rumor was they were going to bomb the place and raze it to the ground. And we thought we were going to be bombed. And there was a enormous explosion. But what, in fact, we saw was a chimney of Crematorium 4-- floor-- of Crematorium-- the chimney of the Crematorium 4 topple to the ground. And then we saw a lot of men running around, shooting. There was a lot of shooting going on. And we were told to lay down on our fronts, spread-eagled. Lay down. And this is what we did. We lay on-- face down, because they were shooting all around us. And we-- we run into our hut. One of the SS men, actually ran into a hut, and somebody got killed. And it transpired later that that was actually the Gottfried Weise, who was later facing trial. He ran into the hut and shot one person during the uprising. Quite a lot of people got killed during this uprising. The men in-- what happened was the men in the crematoria decided they could no longer wait for any official uprising. They also felt that their plan was unsafe, that it might have leaked out. Because they were going to evacuate all-- they were evacuating all privileged prisoners. 
INT: These are inmates? KHM: Yes, they were inmates. They started to evacuate a lot of the influential inmates. And so they thought that possibly, possibly it had leaked out that there was going to be a-- a resistance or an uprising. And the men in the crematoria were not going to wait any longer. And they took up arms, and they suddenly burst out and blew up the crematoria themself. They-- they blew up the Crematorium 4. They also blew up, I think, Crematorium 2 and 3, well, we didn't see it, because we had only view of Crematorium 4. And the kapo-- they had a German kapo. They picked up the German kapo and threw him into the fire, the-- the-- the actual kapo. And there was this enormous explosion everywhere. And the-- the next thing happened, the whole place was absolutely-- they raced in on motorcycles, and there was shooting everywhere. It was like a-- a miniature war going on. People escaped, cut wires. But eventually, of course, it all died down. A lot of people got killed. And they all got caught. And following this, there was an enormous inquiry, because they wanted to know where, naturally, all these things came from, where the ammunition came from, you know, how it was obtained, and how explosions were-- were obtained from. And this is where we had a huge roll call. And what happened during this roll call was quite incredible, soon after this roll call, actually. Unknown to me, quite unknown to me, because I had-- didn't have a lot of contact with my mother. My mother realized that there was a lot of evacuation going on in Auschwitz. With the stuff that I had brought in for her, she managed to pay, actually pay, for her number and my number to be included in the list to be evacuated. And what else she managed to do, she'd managed to get us onto a list of privileged prisoners. Because she thought, well, maybe privileged prisoners will be evacuated to some place rather than be killed. And unknown to me, she-- she did this with the stuff that I managed to bring. I managed to bring some jewelry, and-- and this-- this is what helped to-- to do this. And she'd-- one day, she was waiting for one of the lager commandants, on his name was Hossler. Now, he-- she-- he-- she knew that he wasn't quite as bad as the others. And she went up to him, which was totally forbidden for an ordinary prisoner. She went up to him, and in her very best German, she said, I am prisoner 39933. I beg to speak to the commandant. And he listened. And he had oddly, a Lagerführer, a-- you know, the-- the prisoner kapo with him. And she said, I have been here for nearly two years, and my daughter is working in Kanada, and she's been there eight months. And I am now going on transport, being evacuated. Would it be possible for her to come and join me in that transport? And he just wrote this number down, but he didn't say anything to her. And during the inquiry that followed the uprising, my number was called. And I thought, oh dear, that's it. Because there was an inquiry, and I helped with all this jewelery, and I had things hidden here and there, and they found out. But no. They called, 39934, you're being transferred to B2b. And this is how I came to be transferred back to the camp. And it's the-- the-- the SS woman said, you're being-- you're going on transport with your mother. And that-- that's-- that's the conclusion of Kanada. And I was just transferred. 
INT: Right. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] You mentioned people that you were with. Were we-- were you particularly close to any one person? KHM: Well, I was very close to-- to [NON-ENGLISH]. Her name was [NON-ENGLISH] in those days.  INT: Yes. KHM: And she wasn't evacuated with me. She was, in fact, there, and she-- because they no longer had transports arriving, they were-- started to burn all the stuff that was that there. It was-- they started to destroy it. And they'd brought in all documents from-- from the records offices. And all records were being destroyed. This is why there are so few records now in-- in-- in the museum. Because following this [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] uprising, the-- the-- the crematoria were destroyed, and they could no longer use the crematoria. In fact, it was a most incredible event. Because it put all the crematoria out of action. And they could-- no longer could use the gas chambers and the crematoria. But of course, by that time, those October transports were no longer arriving. But they formed this, what they call a burning kommando. So all the girls that were left behind there were then in a burning kommando, and so was [NON-ENGLISH]. And she was-- she-- she was actually engaged burning all the records after I was transferred. And she was there almost to the end, and she was then on the death march from Auschwitz. So she wasn't evacuated. I don't know what happened to the others. 
INT: Was there ever any contact between men and women? KHM: No-- no-- none official-- no official contact. The contact was under sentence of death, if you had contact with a man-- with an ordinary prisoner. Some of the privileged prisoners did. There were clandestine sort of contacts, no official contacts. No, it was strictly forbidden to speak to a man. And even much, much more punishable was if you wrote a note. If you-- we were not allowed to write to anyone or contact anyone. No-- there was no contact.  INT: You mentioned that there were-- you saw children. Did you ever see any babies? KHM: Yes. We saw a lot of babies being killed there, yes. They were smashing--  INT: You saw them being killed? KHM: Yes. They were being killed outside the crematorium. Yes. Yes.  INT: Can you name any of the perpetrators? KHM: Well, there were all the four, four who were actually in charge there. There was Moll, who was the [NON-ENGLISH] while I was there. There might have been others. But I-- I know there was one called-- of course they-- they might have had one or two on the men's section as well. But I knew there was Wunsch, and there was-- and now, this Wunsch, funny enough, he fell in love with one of the girls in my group. She was a very beautiful Hungarian girl. And because he was very fond of this girl, he actually saved her sister. He actually saved her. I don't know how he did it. He got her out of the gas chamber or somewhere. And because there was this liaison between him and her, which was to our advantage, we had quite a lot of concessions. And he was no longer as brutal as he used to be. But then, of course, there were the others. There was this Weise, who perpetrated a lot of murders. He was eventually committed on five counts, but, I mean, he committed dozens of murders. 
INT: All right. So when you-- was your mother surprised to see you when you got back to-- KHM: Yes, she was. Because she didn't really-- she didn't think that this commandant actually would-- would do that, that he'd pull me out of there. She didn't know. She-- she was waiting to go on this transport. Nobody knew where this transport was going, but because it was-- it-- there was only a transport of 100 names or 100 numbers, really. There were no names. 100 numbers. And she realized that some-- some of the people, some of the numbers that were on this list were block leaders. There weren't any kapos, but they were all people that had privileged positions in the camp, good jobs. And how they-- and why they were selected to go on this transport was some unknown. But she surmised it-- it was better than being on transport with people who are perhaps in not a very good condition. And this is-- this is what really happened. What-- what happened was that one chief of some factory came and wanted 100 women who were in a reasonable condition to work in one factory. And this is how we 100 came to be evacuated on this one list.  INT: How soon after the uprising were you evacuated? KHM: Well, the uprising was on the 6th of October. And there followed this enormous, as I said, inquiry, and whatever. And there was-- I was still working there until-- probably till about the third week in October, something like this. And I-- I was evacuated on the 11th of November, 1944. That's when we were led to the trains, on the 11th of November. 
INT: What sort train? KHM: It was a cattle truck. In-- in a cattle truck. The same cattle trucks that brought people in to die were the same cattle trucks that took me out.  INT: What-- what did you take with you? KHM: Well, unfortunately, I didn't take enough. Because normally, if transports were leaving, they were, again, going through the sauna and being issued with new clothes. But it so happened, because this was some special transport of only 100 privileged prisoners, we were not searched, which, of course, I didn't know. But I-- I did have-- I-- I had-- the-- the diamonds that I had in my teeth, I actually had extracted while I was in Kanada. By some fluke, I managed to get to a dental station. I had these out. Because I-- I knew I might have needed them. But I-- they were in teeth, and no one would have thought there was anything but teeth. And I also had a gold sovereign, which I had sewn into the bottom of my coat. And I-- I actually went out. As-- as I was-- as I came in-- back from Kanada, I had a coat, and I had a dress, and I had some shoes, but-- but nothing-- nothing else. But of course, if I had known that, I could have-- I could have brought out a lot more. But at that stage, I thought, we might have to be searched. [BACKGROUND NOISE] So no, the answer is we didn't. Are you all right? 
INT: I think we'll take a break there.  INT: Right. Tape 10, Kitty Hart-Moxon, the 9th of June, 1998. This is, in fact, a repeat of tape eight. Kitty, you're still in Auschwitz, in 1990-- 1944. And in March of 1944, what was happening? KHM: Well, at about March 1944, I was still working in the hospital on the diarrhea block-- [NON-ENGLISH] block. And there were a lot of prisoners there that were already-- or a lot of girls there that were already convalescing. Not all of them were ill. There were two girls that were from my transport from prison who were already convalescent. They were sick, but we were hiding them in this block, because there was a possibility everyone was going to be thrown back out into the main camp. And completely out of the blue, there was a selection. And it was said that Mengele had ordered for the whole block to be cleared, because there were a lot of people that were having diarrheas and a lot of infectious diseases all around the camp. And he had to fill up the block with new-- newcomers. And he wanted to dispose of everybody that was in the block. And because they didn't sort them out, whoever was already convalescent, the order was that they were-- all had to be loaded onto lorries, and that they were going to dispose of the whole of the block. Maybe they disposed of other blocks as well. But I'm just speaking about the block that I was working on. And before we knew where we were, lorries appeared, surrounded the block. And we were already on-- on the staff. So it was really people who were on the staff that-- including-- including the prison doctors and the nurses and the helpers. We had to actually help all the people out of the block. And many of them were too sick. They had to be loaded onto lorries. There were two people-- one person taking the arms, and one person taking legs. And they had to be thrown onto the lorries. And in fact, I had to help my friends onto the lorries. And they was screaming and didn't want to go. And of course, I personally, just didn't want to live at that point. I-- I was still very weak, and I hadn't completely recovered from typhus. And I really lost my will to live. And when I saw that I had to actually load my friends to die, to-- to me, that was the last straw. And I-- I was struggling, and tried to climb onto the lorries. Well, it so happened that some people saw this, including my mother, and they were watching all that was going on.
And she rushed behind me. And-- and somebody grabbed me and took me away. And I struggled. But the-- the lorries had gone, and all the people were gone. And that whole block was emptied. This block was empty for a few days. And we didn't know what was going to happen with it. They hadn't brought any other people in. They said they were going to disinfect the block before they were bringing somebody else in. In the meantime, a kapo arrived with one of the German wardens, one of the Aufseherinnen. That's the German-- the SS women. And she said, we are forming a new Kanada Kommando, because there was an old Kanada, working in Auschwitz I. Those were girls that used to come back into the camp at night, and they-- they were taken to work in Auschwitz I. But she said, we are now forming a new Kanada Kommando. There'll be a night shift and a day shift. So there's going to be about 400 of us-- 400 people. And if you want to, you can volunteer to-- to work in this Kanada Kommando. Because we didn't quite know what it meant. We did know Kanada meant a-- a world of abundance, because that is where all the goods were brought in. Everything was sorted out that people were-- brought in from all over Europe. All their belongings were just dumped on the ramp and taken to this-- to some of the sections of the Kanada, where things were sorted out. And we did realize, of course, there was access to food. There was access to clothes, and access to things that we hadn't seen for years. And we'd-- we had a few hours to think about this. And my mother said, look. You have to go, because you-- you cannot recover your strength here. You'll be thrown out. You might be thrown out back onto-- to the main camp now that the block is empty. You got the opportunity. You must go. If you have the choice of doing it, you must go. And so I said, yes, all right. I will join this Kanada Kommando. And so early-- early-- it were perhaps end of March, early April, 200 of us were marched out from the main enclosure of the camp, taken through different sections of the camp between fences, and more fences, and more enclosures, all different sections of the camp, until we came to the very end, more or less, of the camp. And there we found a-- a very small enclosure, which-- the-- the thing that we didn't actually know is that-- which they didn't tell us-- that we were going to be isolated in that particular part of the camp.
We were not going to be permitted to come back at night. We were to be housed there, and we were going to stay there and-- and not return back to the camp. I think if I'd known that, that perhaps I would not have volunteered to-- to go. Because then I'd realized that I'll be parted, and I won't be able to go and see my mother. However, there-- there was no choice by that time. We came in-- into the Kanada enclosure. Well, what we found there was a-- a relatively small enclosure, probably about 30 wooden huts, which were, I believe, originally stables for horses. They were wooden huts with no windows, but they-- they had kind of skylights at-- at the top, except two huts that actually had windows. And those were the huts for the-- our group of 400. And we were enclosed. These two huts were enclosed with a wire, with a-- a electrified fence. Although, we were within the-- a camp, we were still enclosed within this camp and in a separate little enclosure. And this-- I believe the-- the men were also enclosed at the opposite end of this-- this small camp. Now, we passed-- on the one side, we passed two brick buildings with tall chimneys, which I later learned was Crematorium 2 and 3. And on-- on the other side, we passed, again, two crematoria, which Crematoria 4 and 5-- low brick buildings with high, tall chimneys. In the center was a-- what was known as a lagerstrasse, which just was a-- a-- a paved-- like a-- a street. And there was the-- the-- the sauna, a sort of a L-shaped building. And this, we were told, was the sauna. On the outside of the fence was a-- like a little wood, birch wood-- all birch trees. And this is how I realized that this is actually the-- the name derived for Birkenau is from this small wood. Because it were all birch trees. And in Polish, Brzezinka This is the name for a birch tree in Polish. So Brzezinka was the part of Birkenau-- was called Brzezinka. And in German, they called it Birkenau. The-- the actual camp itself, I think I said had about 30 huts. Most of the huts contained clothes and belongings that were to be sorted out from people that were brought from all over Europe. Right at the bottom of this small enclosure, of that 30 huts, were the most gigantic heap of all manner of stuff jumbled up. There were open suitcases. There were prams. There were dolls. There were toys. There were shoes. There were men's clothes, women's clothes, all jumbled up. The pile was as high as a two or three-story building.
And of course, the same happened-- was in most of the huts. They were full of these clothes that had been-- and belongings-- that were to be sorted out. What happened next was the-- the SS woman that was in charge of our group decided that our group was to be kitted out in navy blue dresses with white spots. But of course, they were not issued. And so there was a great big scramble. We had to scramble in all these huts and-- and this great big pile at the bottom, to look for these sort of clothes-- dresses-- blue dresses with white spots. And I think we also had some head scarves. And we had to paint red crosses. And I remember we used to paint them on with lipsticks that were found in the pile somewhere. So we had to paint. Everybody had to have a white cross-- a-- a red cross painted on the back. And of course, we didn't have any red paint, and we-- we used lipsticks that we found. We were then divided into a day shift and a night shift. And I was actually allocated on the-- to a night shift. Now, there were different-- but-- different girls had different duties there, during the day and during the night. Some girls were sorting out from the big pile and bringing cartloads of clothes into these huts. And then of course, the things-- stuff was sorted out. There were other girls that were working, sorting out food. So one of the first things we did is formed a sort of a small family, because it was important for one person to work in one place, one in another. Because we didn't actually have access to any of these things, although we were handling these things. But even food, there was no access. So you did need always to have one person working in one place so you could exchange food, clothes, shoes, whatever you needed. We-- we were four of us on-- all sleeping on one bunk. And one girl I slept with I knew as [? Ida. ?] Now, her name is [? Batsheva, ?] and she actually lives in Israel. And she and I shared a bunk for eight months in-- in the Kanada Kommando. And now my personal work on night shift was as follows. At one time, I sorted men's jackets. I also sorted, at one time, corsets-- women's corsets. This was-- well, what you had to do is you had to go-- either you had someone bringing you these jackets to sort out, or you had to go into the large pile and find men's jackets, bring them onto a trestle table, where you-- you had-- you laid out these jackets, and you had to open up the seams to see if there was any valuables inside.
And believe you me, when I was sorting out corsets, I used to find great big pouches of jewelry that were on, money that was sewn in. And all this had to be taken out and put into a bucket or-- or a blanket that you had next to your trestles, you know, next to your table. And you had to sort the-- sort out-- say, you sorted jackets. So you'd put them in bundles of 12. And they all had to be neatly folded and then parceled up. And you had put a number on-- your own number-- who did the sorting. And you then handed this in. And again, there were girls working, ticking off how-- how many parcels you did. Because you had to do a certain quota during your shift. Well, what we used to do-- as soon as you did your quota, [BACKGROUND NOISE] we would-- you all right? We would hand in everything that we'd managed to do-- the quota. [BACKGROUND NOISE] You all right? And then we would go and lie in-- in a pile of clothes, if we could, just to hide. Because there wasn't such a-- such a time that you could do nothing. And when you'd done your quota, you-- you thought you'd probably done your duty. But as I said, there were other girls that were-- some were sorting shoes, and some were sorting food. And this-- this is how we managed to-- to organize things so that everybody had enough to eat and-- yeah, and enough clothes. Now our hut-- I think I explained. It had windows. And our view from the hut, from my hut, was directly to Crematorium 4. I could actually watch what was going on outside Crematorium 4, not inside. But I could also watch how gas was being put into the gas chamber. Because I was on night shift, I slept sort of most of the day. But from perhaps early afternoon, we were free to roam until it was time to work. Well, I don't know what time the shift started, because no one knew a time. From what I saw, I witnessed the murder in Crematorium 4. First of all, I, on many, many occasions, saw one person, an S-- one of the SS personnel. Sometimes they wore white sort of overalls. And sometimes they were-- they wore just a green you know, army overall-- army uniforms. But they always wore a gas mask. And you-- you saw this person go up a ladder outside Crematorium 4, with a canister of gas, and throw it in through an opening in-- at the top. And he'd throw in this canister of whatever it was-- not the canister itself-- just the contents. And-- and then-- and with a gas mask-- and then he would come straight down and remove the ladder. And sometimes-- or mostly-- discard the-- the empty tin of gas. Now, outside Crematorium 4, there was a paved area. But the-- past the paved area, there-- there was like a-- a-- a meadow. And flowers were growing there. And the-- and the wood-- and there were people sitting in the woods there, actually picnicking. But of course, what they didn't know is they were waiting and queuing to go into the gas chamber. It was the next lot to go into the gas chamber, which of course, they had absolutely no conception. Because they saw us. We were pretty well dressed. During the afternoon we even sunbathed on the lawn there. And it was a very hot summer-- 1944-- and we were splashing ourselves with the water. And we-- we-- I actually found a-- a mouth organ. We did-- did-- we'd played music. Well, at the same time, we saw and watched what was going on outside the crematorium. And people were led in into this building. And soon after that, you heard screams-- first muffled screams, and then quite-- sometimes quite loud screams. And then it was probably deathly silence. Soon after that, you saw smoke coming out of the chimneys. And you also saw flames coming out of the chimneys. And a while later, you saw a group of men working. Or while you there, they're entering one entrance or another. And they came out with cartloads of ash, which was deposited at the rear of Crematorium 4. There was a kind of a pond, and they used to dump it into the pond. 
INT: Who were these men? KHM: Well, the-- the men working there were the Sonderkommando. But we had no actual direct contact with the Sonderkommando. We couldn't speak to them. But those were the men working inside the crematorium.  INT: Were they inmates? KHM: They were inmates working in-- inside the crematorium, yes.  INT: Did you ever have an opportunity to talk to those people in the woods? KHM: There-- there was-- there was an opportunity. It would have been. But it was strictly forbidden. We were not allowed to communicate. Anyone that attempted to communicate was immediately taken out and-- and immediately taken into the gas chamber. So it was under-- you were under sentence of death if you even tried to communicate. It was absolutely not possible to communicate unless you wanted to commit suicide. So there was no way for us. And it would not have helped. There was no way that you could have said to these people, you're now going to die. Well, they wouldn't have believed it anyway. You know, it was just something that you couldn't take in. Or it's something we couldn't take in. We-- we saw it day and night. We saw the smoking chimneys day and night. And sometimes the air was black with smoke and soot. But yet, you tried to believe that it wasn't really happening. Because you couldn't take it in. Your brain simply would-- didn't register this.  INT: Did the people who were so-called picnicking in the woods, was it their own food that they brought with them, do you think? KHM: I think so. I think this-- this was still the-- because you see, what happened is, that when people were thrown out of their trucks, mostly, they were-- they had to leave all their luggage behind. But if they had sort of hand luggage and things, they didn't sometimes do selections there. They didn't sort them out. Sometimes they did. Sometimes they didn't. Sometimes they selected people in the woods. Sometimes they selected them outside the crematorium. Sometimes they selected them inside the crematorium. And sometimes they still carried out selections in the saunas. So it was very difficult to know where people were actually selected. I think most of them that were already sitting around in the woods, they were never selected. They were just people who were going into the gas chamber. And they had small belongings with them. And perhaps they had-- if they had small babies, they had a bottle with-- with milk or water or whatever. But then-- but you could actually see them still eating or drinking or whatever. So, no, they were not issued with anything. Is this what you think? No, no. There-- there was all stuff that they just happened to have with them at that particular moment. Now, I witnessed a lot of murders-- 
INT: Yes. KHM: --being perpetrated outside the Crematorium 4.  INT: Tell me about them. KHM: What I think I must explain-- that there were very few SS personnel actually in charge of this section of the camp. But on-- and in overall charge was a man called Moll, at that time, when I was there. There were other commandants, et cetera. But all-- the person I remember was a man called Moll-- M-O- double L.
