Schleppy ?] or the [NON-ENGLISH], in German. I never knew his true name. It-- his true name was Gottfried Weise, which I didn't-- hadn't known for many, many years, until much, much later. And well, there might have been just one or two others. They were not really in charge of prisoners as such. They were in charge of the goods. And there was no need for. And I had-- so there-- there, at that point, there was no need for guards, because the whole place, was in any case, electrified. No one could get out anywhere, even if they tried. So they didn't actually have any guards there. And they didn't want panics, and the dogs, and things like that. They wanted people to go peacefully to their deaths. They didn't want any shootings and suchlike. But there were a lot of gratuitous murders perpetrated by these four people who were sort of patrolling and just looking on as people were waiting to go into the gas chamber. 
INT: Did you witness those? KHM: Yes, I-- I witnessed a lot of murders there. Unfortunately, I couldn't actually put a name or a face to these murderers. Because there were four of them. And very often you couldn't tell one from another. But-- although, the-- the blind one, this one had the glass eye. So he was quite conspicuous. And there-- there were a few very nasty instances of-- of gratuitous murder. Because these people were waiting to die anyway. But I-- I recall an-- an occasion where one child pick-- wanted to pick flowers, had got out of a queue, and was starting to pick flowers-- a small child. And one of these four people, SS officers, picked up this child by its legs and smashed it against the wall of the crematorium in front of its mother. And there was another occasion where one child begged for water. And again, well-- well some water was thrown from my side of the camp. Somebody threw a bottle of water to this child, which was actually a disaster at that time. But he saw this. Sometimes we thought they hadn't seen it, because they weren't actually patrolling there. But he did see it, and he picked this child up, he threw it in the air, and then he speared it on his bayonet. And he did other things outside this crematorium. He used to pick out small boys or children and place tins or cans on both their shoulders and-- and-- on the child's shoulders and his head, and shot at them. And that's how he became known as the William Tell of Auschwitz. Because he's practicing shooting tins of prisoners. He also did this to prisoners. So there were quite a lot of murders perpetrated. But we were not allowed to see this. I mean, we watched this. But you-- you never had to admit that you actually saw this. So you-- you tried to disappear when you-- when you saw a murder like this committed. Sometimes we saw it from inside the hut. And we-- we tried to get away as soon as we saw a murder like that. Because if they had realized that we've seen it, I-- I think they would have not liked us to be a witness to-- to that sort of murder. 
INT: Did you ever talk about it amongst yourselves? KHM: We tried not to talk about it. We tried not-- we tried to push it out our minds. We always-- we decided we-- we cannot take this in. It wasn't actually happening, you know. You didn't-- we pretended it wasn't happening. Although, you know, there-- there were screams, and there were bodies all around you, and murder all around you. But you just tried not to see it.  INT: Did anyone ever try to resist what was happening? KHM: There were one or two occasions when people realized what was happening. And there was one very nasty occasion, which I didn't actually witness. But there was one person who realized what was going to happen. And she screamed out, you're not going to go in to kill me. We-- I'm not going to be gassed-- or something. And she actually pulled a revolver from one of the SS officers and shot him. And his name was Schillinger. I-- I do remember this particular SS officer. And he got killed by this-- by this person. And then there was a lot of shooting. And they let dogs loose And it-- it really was a-- a very mess-- messy way for people to die. And I think this is why they tried to keep it all calm and quiet. And there was a great big pretense. And nobody knew exactly what was going to happen until it was too late. By the time people were-- people realized once they were shut in the gas chamber. Because there were-- loudspeakers used blare to say-- because we could hear this-- please, tie your shoes together and put your clothes neatly on one side. You're now going to be disinfected under the showers. And of course, we knew what happened. Gas came out instead of the-- instead of water. 
INT: Mm-hmm. Did you know the-- that the person pouring in something from cans on top of the crematoria were-- did you know that it was gas that they were pouring? KHM: Yes, we knew. We-- we saw the-- the canisters of gas. The canisters of gas were all around the camp. All around this enclosure you could find--  INT: You knew what that was. KHM: --canisters of gas. Well, it said on it-- Zyklon B gas. It actually said--  INT: Right. KHM: --so. And there was a-- the skull and crossbones on it. In-- in fact, if you-- if you came too close to one of these canisters, there-- there was a smell of gas. There-- there were occasions when the gas chambers were opened a little bit too soon. And you could smell gas, you-- you know, in-- in the surrounding area.  INT: What were the cans that the-- the William Tell person put on them? KHM: Oh, any cans.  INT: Any cans. KHM: Any cans, yes. He used to shoot--  INT: Yeah. KHM: --any cans of-- of-- that's right-- used to shoot them off prisoners' heads and off their shoulders. But he shot until he killed them. So there was-- there was always a-- a death followed the-- the-- the shoot-- the-- the-- the game. It was kind of sport to-- to-- to this particular person. What I really must mention is about the jewelry that there was-- the incredible amount of jewelry and gold when-- I'm not only talking about gold that was taken from the corpses, which of course, anyway we know. But I didn't work inside the crematoria. I didn't see that. But I can tell you about the gold that I found while sorting the clothes, which very often, at end of a shift, could have been a bucketful. And one-- one person had a bucketful. And at the-- the end of the shift, it was carried in blankets. Now, we were very conscious that we must not hand in. But on the other hand, we couldn't keep it. There was just no way that we could hide it anywhere, because they were inspecting our living quarters almost on a daily basis. And so you couldn't hide anything. And anything found on you was classed as sabotage. And we know what death penalty-- that carries the death penalty. So nobody took a chance to have anything on them or in their bunks. But what we did do is at the end of our hut, there was a-- a very small, little tiny hut, which was a lavatory. And within this lavatory, we buried-- well, I buried. I don't know-- some of my friends as well. I buried a lot of the stuff that I found-- was buried there. This was eventually, of course, excavated by the Poles who-- after liberation. But this is where we buried an awful lot of stuff all there. All around the area there were places where some of these valuables were buried. Now the other thing-- we realized that there was a-- a resistance movement somewhere. Now, we didn't know where they were, who they were. But we knew there was a-- a resistance movement. Oh, I did have contact with a girl whom I knew quite well, a girl called Roza Robota. And she was one of the four girls that helped to-- to provide the explosives for the-- for the uprising. And I knew this girl. And I knew that she worked in a aluminum factory where they were producing explosives. And I knew that somehow or other I have got to get some of these valuables to her, because she had access to explosives. And this is eventually what happened. We did manage to get some stuff through to these girls, and they bought some ammunition-- bribed some of the SS people. And eventually, some of the guns and ammunition found itself back to the enclosure, to the-- to-- to the so-called underground. And in that sense, we actually helped to-- to-- in the uprising indirectly. 
INT: Right. KHM: But what-- what I meant to say-- there were one-- there was one occasion where I found an enormous amount of jewelry. Now, money was of no value. What was this for? KHM: Well, there was one occasion where I found an enormous amount of jewelry. That was during when the Hungarian transports began to arrive, between May and July, or-- July, yes. And you were not allowed to talk to the men. But there was an occasion when-- when the men passed by. And they were just dishing out their soup. And I spoke to one of the-- I approached one of the-- the SS guard, just to say something about the-- I'm just going to just give them some soup or some-- some such thing. INT: So what was this going to be used for? KHM: Well, it was going-- well, it was going to-- it was useful. Because what had happened, the-- the-- about end of July, August, rumors began to circulate that they were going to liquidate the whole of the camp. They were going to bomb the whole place, the whole complex, especially the Kanada complex, and they were going to raze the place to the ground. You see, transports were not arriving all-- all that much more by that time. Although were-- they were still liquidating some of the camps. The Lódz ghetto, they were liquidating. In fact, I was going to tell you about a girl that lived with me who recognized her father going into the gas chamber, and she committed suicide. And I also-- I also remember the-- the-- a transport from Theresienstadt that came in just during that time. And they all went straight to the gas chamber. Because we-- the only thing we could sometimes find out is where these people came from.  INT: How did you know they came from-- KHM: Well, I think-- I think when there-- when there was no one around, you could just sort of shout where from, so that we knew where these people came from. And the-- the-- one lot of transport came from Theresienstadt, and also one transport came from Majdanek.  INT: Did you know those places? KHM: Did I know the places? No, no. Well, we knew what Theresienstadt was. We knew it was the-- the model camp. And we did realize then that they were liquidating the-- the model camp. And of course, we tried to think why they were doing this. But it was obvious, you see? The-- the-- the Russian front was nearing, and so they began to liquidate. They liquidated Lódz, and they liquidated Majdanek, which, again, was near Lublin, you know, far east. So we realized they were beginning to liquidate these people, and they were bringing them straight into the gas chamber. Now, so this is why the men prepared an uprising. Now, we heard rumors about an uprising, because they-- they were saying that they're not going to wait until the whole of the complex was going to be bombed and razed to the ground. They were going-- there was going to be this uprising. Unfortunately, it couldn't be organized, because there were so many informers. And many of the prominent political prisoners had-- they started to evacuate. So they-- the-- the authorities realized that there was a danger of an uprising. Then they began to disperse people also already from Auschwitz. And so the men in-- in the-- in the crematoria were not going to wait.
Because they were being liquidated. Every so often, the Sonderkommando was being replaced. There was not just one Sonderkommando that worked there right through. Every so often, they replaced these people. So they-- the-- the last Sonderkommando, I don't know what number it was, decided that they were not going to wait. They were going to have this uprising. And we didn't know. We knew there was something in the offing. But we thought they were going-- what-- the next thing that was going to happen, we were going to be bombed. You see, transports were no longer arriving from Hungary. They had liquidated the whole of the Hungarian Jews. Trickle of prisoners kept on coming in from different camps. We still had a lot of work to do there. We realized, as long as there's these pile and pile to sort out, we are still relatively safe, because they wanted all these effects. They wanted all these goods. But we realized that our time is possibly up, and they were not going to evacuate us. This is what we felt. Now, we knew this underground movement, but we didn't know who they were or what they were going to do. And one day, which in fact, I knew later, it was the sixth of October, 1944, there was an almighty explosion. And the very first thing I remember is the chimney of Crematorium 4 toppling to the ground. Of course, our first thought was that we were being bombed and razed to the ground. And we all lay down flat, spread-eagled on the floor. There were commands came from all sides, lie down flat on the floor. And we were we all lying flat, waiting to be bombed, I presume. But what in fact happened is the men in the crematoria, the Sonderkommando, staged a revolt and blew up some of the crematoria. I think they also blew up Crematorium 2, but we couldn't see it. We could see Crematorium 4, you see? But they definitely blew up Crematorium 4, and at a stroke, put all the crematoria out of action. So no way could they continue with the process of killing people, you know, the-- the way it-- it progressed. But-- and there was a skirmish. They brought a-- a whole armored division in, and there was a lot of shooting. One man-- well, I think it was this man, Weise, he got into our hut. Because some of the male prisoners ran all around the huts to hide. And one was shot in our hut. Well, I don't know who shot-- who exactly it was. But it was one of the four, anyway, one of the four SS officers. But a lot of other SS personnel came in with machine guns, and they came in, roaring in on motorcycles. And they rounded everybody up. Some men got away. They cut the electrified fence. But they-- they caught everybody. And a-- a lot of people got killed. But some of the SS people got killed as well. So that was the uprising, on the sixth of October. 
INT: What-- what contact did you have you with your mother during your time in Kanada? KHM: Now, I really want to tell you about this, so if we need to go a little bit back in time. As I explained before, we were isolated there, and we couldn't actually just go back into the camp, or we were not brought into the camp at night. However, every so often, there was a-- a cart with sort of junk clothes, taken in to the main camp as distribution for the newcomers that were coming in, I take it. We don't know. Anyway, that was the pretext that we were going into the main camp. I-- my kapo, Nanci, she was a-- a very-- Manci. She was a-- a very good kapo. As it-- obviously, she didn't have to beat us up, of course. You know, there was an abundance of everything there. And she arranged-- mostly, she arranged for me to go with a cart back to the camp, because she knew I had my mother there. And one day, I got back, and I found my mother had typhus. Well, luckily, I could bring in-- I-- I used to smuggle in a lot of stuff. Now, it wasn't-- it wasn't permitted to bring any-- you-- you couldn't carry anything. Because you-- at the gate, going into the main camp, you were-- there was an inspection, and you were searched. Your arms and your legs out, and they searched you to see if you brought anything in. But nonetheless, I had all-- I-- I usually wore about four or five layers of clothes. And I had various things pinned on my back, because I knew this is where they were not searching. And I also had tins tucked inside. And once I-- I got through with a tin between my legs, a tin of meat, would you believe it? I smuggled that in. And because I could-- I brought things in, my mother was able to-- or I was able to arrange for people to look after my mother, which they did. They nursed her. 
INT: Who were those people? KHM: They were the nurses on-- on-- well, the nurses and the-- the prisoner doctors. I could pay for that. And they looked after her, because there were selections to the gas chamber every other day from the typhus block. And so they looked after her and hid her while she had typhus. So eventually, she recovered. But on one occasion, or was it perhaps more than one occasion, our SS woman decided she didn't want to take us the shortcut. She said she was going to take us right around the outer perimeter-- perimeter of the camp, right around the other side, which was out of bounds. And it was certainly out of bounds for prisoners, because there were things that we were not allowed to see. And she told us, when you pass through the outside, you must look down. You must not move your head to the right or to the left. You look down as you pass. But nonetheless, we-- we did see certain things. One-- one of the places we passed was-- was known the white house. The white house had walls actually splattered blood. Now, outside this white house was a queue of men, queuing in to go in. They didn't know. They thought they were going in to be registered or whatever. But in fact, every time one person went in, they were shot in-- in-- in the neck. And you could just hear a very, very faint thud of a bullet going into their necks. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Then we passed pits. And you see, in July or August, or July certainly, or June, July, the crematoria could no longer cope with the amount of people that they were bringing in and the bodies to burn. And even the gas chambers couldn't cope with the number of people. And so they had dug pits. And we passed some of the pits. And at the edge of the pits, there were people that were being thrown into the pits. And there were also bodies in the pits, and they were burning bodies in the pits there. So this is something we were not supposed to see. But as I say, this-- these are very few people that actually saw this on the way from the Kanada. The Kanada enclosure was, by the way, called B2g, a very small enclosure. And I have actually got a plan of it, which I can show later. 
INT: So-- KHM: And we come to the--  INT: What happened after the-- KHM: Uprising.  INT: --uprising? Were there any killings? KHM: There were a lot of killings after the uprising. Mostly killing-- mostly they-- they interrogated people, and they found-- certainly they killed off all the people in the Sonderkommando that participated, because they knew which-- where-- where the revolt originated. So yes, they killed a lot of people there. They-- they also-- of course, there was a battle, and people died in the battle. They found the four girls that were responsible for supplying the-- the-- the munition-- not munitions, the-- the grenades or powder or whatever. The four girls had actually worked in that munition plant. And these four girls were found. They were tortured, and they were hung in full view of the camp. Now, I was no longer there, because they were hung in January, I believe. And of course, I-- by that time, I was evacuated.  INT: Do you know the names of any of those girls? KHM: Well, I knew-- I-- I knew Roza Robota I knew. And then there was another one called Ella Gartner I knew. I-- I didn't know the other two. But I knew two of them, yes.  INT: How did you-- when did you hear that they were hanged? KHM: Oh, I didn't hear that until after the war.  INT: Right. KHM: Yes. I-- I-- we thought that they were, you know, shot immediately. But apparently, they were not. They were taken. They were questioned. They were interrogated, and they were tortured for a long time before they actually hung them. Now, what-- what happened then, there was a-- there-- there was an enormous interrogation. There was a-- a roll call, and, you know, people were questioned, and all kinds of things happened. But in the meantime, my mother did things that I didn't know. For starters, I said I-- I-- she'd recovered from typhus. And I smuggled in a lot of precious things which I shouldn't have had on me, and certainly all the clothes which were-- were tradable and were sort of currency, towels and soap, which you-- which you never saw, toothbrushes and things like that. And with that, my-- my mother managed to-- she found out that there was a-- a general evacuation from Auschwitz, and people were evacuated all over the place. But she found out that there was a transport of 100 women, all privileged prisoners on a list. There were only 100 of these, and they were all put on a separate list. And because she had all this to-- to give and to pay for, she managed to include her number and my number on this list.
And this is how I came to be on the list to be evacuated with some privileged prisoners. There were some block leaders there, women that-- some-- I don't think there are any kapos, but there were-- there certainly were some block leaders and people that had prominent positions in the camp. I didn't know this. What else my mother did while I was in Kanada, and that one day, she-- she decided she was going to speak to Commandant Hossler, because he was one of the more amenable commandants. He'd-- he'd not been actually seen to kill anybody or actually beat anyone up himself. Now, it wasn't possible, normally, for an ordinary prisoner to approach a commandant. You always had to speak via a prominent prisoner. So you had to speak to-- to a kapo or Lagerführer and say prisoner number so-and-so wishes to speak to so-and-so. Well, it-- it was practically unheard of to address a commandant, but my mother waited her-- waited and eventually had a opportunity. She saw the commandant there with the Lagerführer [NON-ENGLISH], and as I've said to you before, when she was ruling the camp, things began to improve. And my mother went up to the-- directly to the commandant. And she said, prisoner number 3993 wishes to-- begs to speak to the commandant, in her perfect German. And he was a bit sort of startled. But he said, yes? And she explained that she'd been nearly two years now in the camp. And of course, he realized she was quite elderly there. And he was quite amazed at that. And she showed him the number, 39933, it was quite an old number. It was an old prisoner by that time. And she explained that my daughter has been working in Kanada for eight months now, and I am on a list to go on transport. Would it be possible for me-- for-- for my daughter to be transferred, so that we could go on transport together? Well, he didn't say anything, apparently. Now, next thing that happened, during a roll call, soon after this interrogation, while there was a roll call in Kanada, my name-- my number was called. And my first thought was, oh, yes. That's it. They have discovered my connection with the jewelry and the passing on to other people. Somebody must have confessed where it came from. But it wasn't that. The SS woman said, prisoner number 39934, you're going to be transferred to camp B2b. You're going on transport with your mother. And I was transferred back to the main camp. Now, I do know what went on in Kanada after I left, because [NON-ENGLISH], who was with me, told me after-- you know, several times, what happened. She was there till the end and eventually was on the death march when they evacuated Auschwitz, just prior to liberation. And she was there. And they formed a burning kommando. The Kanada kommando became a burning commando. And they began to burn all the records from Auschwitz 1, which was the political [NON-ENGLISH], or the political department, where all the records were kept, and all, no manner of papers. And all these records were brought to the Kanada enclosure, and they were burning all the records and burning all the goods that were still in those-- in-- inside the-- the huts. So they started razing the place to the ground and burning all the things. And-- and they also exploded or sprung some of the-- the rest of the crematoria, which to this day are the way they had been sprung, some by the SS, some, of course, during the uprising. 
INT: Did the Germans ever request any sort of special things from Kanada? KHM: Yes. Oh, yes.  INT: In what way? KHM: Well, it wasn't that they requested. Of course, they had-- they themselves were not allowed to touch it. That was the rule. But of course, there was an awful lot of looting going on. And even the commandants couldn't actually come and take what they wanted. But of course, you can imagine the great wealth that was brought in there.  INT: Who was the commandant of Auschwitz at the time? KHM: Well, the-- the overall-- well, the overall commandant, it could have been Hoss. Or Hoss was transferred, and he came back into Auschwitz in September. I think the-- the commandant of this particular-- of B1, B2 was Kramer, who was eventually, of course, in Belsen, and he was hung. Because all-- all the commandants of Auschwitz and the women were transferred to Belsen during that time. But-- the-- the-- the one commandant was in charge of that area was called Moll. And there was an awful lot of looting going on, which-- which we witnessed. Because I think I have mentioned to you before that at the end of our hut, there was also a great big heap of clothes. Because originally, the clothes were brought into the hut and all the heaped up at the end of the hut, and we then had to sort out. And often, when we'd finished our quota, we used to go and hide in-- in this heap. And there was a dividing wall between us and the next part of the hut. And it so happened, the hut that I was in, there-- there was what was called a [NON-ENGLISH], which is a-- a duty room. I don't believe every hut had the duty room. It's possible, because we've never been inside it. But there was a duty room, just at the back of this heap of clothes. And there were quite sort of open gaps in-- in the construction of this dividing wall. And we could watch what they were actually doing. And believe you me, most evening-- because I was on night shift-- most evenings and nights, they were sorting out jewelry there. That's-- there-- and it looks to me as though that is where a lot of looting took place. They were actually sorted out. There-- it was all-- they had tables there, and they were sorting out the diamonds and the jewelry and the gold. Now, I don't know whether it was possible for them to-- to keep it or what, but most certainly, there-- there was that's where this black market was, and stacks and stacks of notes. We used to use notes as toilet paper, because that's all it was good for. It really wasn't of any use to us, and we didn't want to hand it in, so we literally used it as lavatory paper. But they had stacks of currencies there on the table. And of course, they were always drunk. They had huge carafes. When the Greeks came, when the Italian transports came, they had huge carafes of wine. And they-- they were always drunk, these-- these SS people there. There were a lot of drinking going on. So we actually watched what they were doing. But of course, they were also watching us, which we didn't know. And at one time, I was actually watched, and-- and somebody saw me write a note, which I-- I wrote a note to my mother, which I sent with one-- one of the carts, and I was caught writing once. So we were-- there was like a two-way-- two-way watch going on in-- inside the hut. So there was a-- and some of the commandants had their trustees that would come in and ask for various things for the commandants. So yes, there was a lot of private looting going on as well. 
INT: When were you aware that the transports were lessening? KHM: Well, we-- I would say already about August 1944. There were not as many transports. The-- the-- the worst period was between May and July. Transports began to thin out around about August, September. In September were the rumors of the-- of the liquidation of the camp. And of course, in October, we blew up the crematorium. And then a lot of transports came from other concentration camps and also, of course, from the main camp. You see, a lot of people thought they were actually being evacuated from Auschwitz. And what we found, that some of these people, the trains never left. They shunted them back to the Kanada, and they actually surprised them and put them all into the gas chambers, people that never thought they were going to be gassed. This is why my mother included our number on a prominent list, because she felt that this might perhaps be a-- a transport that was going to go out of Auschwitz, which of course, and-- and that's what happened.  INT: How-- how-- how did you go out then? How did you get back to your mother? KHM: Well, as I said, I was just simply transferred, and somebody just took me back to-- to-- and I was dumped back on my mother. And my mother was on the-- in the-- by that time, she was put what was like a transit block, where all these 100 people that were going to be evacuated were all put together. And of course, what I didn't know is that we were-- were-- normally, if transports were evacuated, they all went into a sauna where they were stripped of everything they had, and they were issued with different clothes. But it so happened, because this was a transport of prominent sort of prisoners, we were among them, that we were never searched, which was rather a-- a shame. Because if I had known, I could have smuggled in a lot of stuff which would have been invaluable in the-- in the-- in the next few months. 
INT: Who were the other prominent people on this 100? KHM: Well, on the 100, I-- well, some I-- I don't know the names, some of them. There was a-- a-- a mother and a daughter, a mother and two daughters, and a-- and a cousin and a niece of the mother. And they were all the family of Selinger. And they came from the town of Sosnowiec. The-- the one daughter was Henya, and she was actually a block leader. She was first a [NON-ENGLISH], a-- a messenger girl, and she then got a block. She-- she in fact, had the block, I think where the musicians were. So she had a good block. You know, she didn't have to beat anybody up. I mean, they-- they were all relatively prominent people on-- on her block. Her mother didn't do anything, because she was hidden, and same with her sister and her cousin. She saved all of them by herself having this prominent position. And then there were one or two girls that worked in the sauna. And who else? There was one or two girls that worked in the kitchens. And-- and I don't remember any kapos, actually. There were one or two other block leaders. So they were all people that had sort of some position of where they managed to get onto this list. Because they said that the-- the list was compiled because a chief of one of the factories came to look for 100 girls that were in relatively good condition. And so this is how we came to be included on-- on this list. And believe me, I came into Auschwitz in a much worse condition than I actually left it. Because during the eight months that I worked in Kanada, I at least managed to put on weight. Because after my typhus, I was what was known as a Muselmann. I was literally on the death's door. And I just couldn't recover on-- on the camp food, anyway. And the same with my mental attitude and my-- you know, I had lost my will to fight. And in Kanada, I just managed to regain my physical strength, although mentally, it-- it was the most horrific time. But physically, I actually put on weight, and my health improved. And the same with my mother's, because I managed to bring in other things than just living on camp soup. So we both left Auschwitz pretty well in a better condition than we actually came in-- 
INT: Right. KHM: --which was a paradox.  INT: We'll take a-- we'll take a break there. KHM: Yeah.  INT: Tape 12, Kitty Hart-Moxon, the 9th of June 1989-- 1998. Sorry. You finished on the last tape saying that you were about to leave Auschwitz in a better condition healthwise than when you entered. Right. What happened then? KHM: Well, one of the reasons I entered Auschwitz in a worse condition is because I had been, of course, on the run since 1939 until 1943, you could say, apart from the very short period in Germany. I was pretty well on a starvation diet. And I'd been on the run. And I'd been sick, and I'd been in pretty bad condition, and so was my mother. But the last eight months in Auschwitz I had recovered, having worked in the Kanada. The next thing that happened, we were led to some cattle trucks. We were pushed into the trucks and sealed with a hundred people. I believe there was another 200 that joined in another truck, which were not on our list. The whole group-- the whole transport-- there may have been others. There might have been men in-- in-- in a different truck. I don't know. We were taken-- well, of course, we didn't know where we were evacuated to. We had no idea.  INT: What had you been told? KHM: Nothing. Nothing whatever. Nobody told you anything. And you couldn't get any information from anybody. And nobody dared ask too many questions. The whole point was, which way did the engine face? Did it face to the crematoria, or did it face out of the gate? And that is where we all held our breath. We didn't know, because we were shut in in the truck. And it wasn't until it actually moved that we realized that it was actually gaining speed, gathering speed. And we gathered that we've actually gone through the gates of Auschwitz. But we didn't see it, because we were sealed in in the cattle trucks. We were in these trucks. And there was-- there was a lot of relief. People were crying and singing. We were actually singing the-- there was a special prisoner song. And I don't remember the words. And [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] my mother and I, we were both a little apprehensive, because there were all these prominent prisoners, and some of them we didn't know, and some of the faces we did know and didn't like. And we didn't quite know, you know, whether we were going to be attacked, or whatever. Anyway, we decided between us, my mother and I, we were going to say something. And we said, look, girls. We are all in this together now. You might have had good positions. We don't know what the next place is going to bring. We're all equal now. So let's just be friends, and let's just say that we help each other, and not kill each other, and look after one another. And lo and behold, all these prominent prisoners, they all joined hands. And we all sang and promised that we're going to look after one another. 
INT: Who suggested this? Who said this? Your mother? KHM: Well, my mother suggested it at first, you see. And-- and it-- it had all came out. And I-- and we, again, immediately formed into little families. We're going to stay together. So-and-so is going to stay together. We all chose groups that are going to stick together, because that was almost the first thing you did. Because even two of us couldn't be on our own. You had to be more than that. And we were together with the Selinger family. So that there was Henya and her sister Renya, and her mother and my mother, and myself and-- and the cousins. And we sort of stuck together, like sort of one little family. And from then on we were together until liberation, this-- this close group of-- of five of us.  INT: So how long were you in the train? KHM: I don't know how long we were in the train. We-- I don't think we-- we traveled very far, because we-- the next place where they tipped us out was Gross-Rosen. We'd never heard of Gross-Rosen. But it-- it-- it-- it was a-- a camp in the-- the-- within the Reich, within Germany. We didn't know where it was. We-- we had gathered it wasn't all that far away, because we hadn't traveled a huge distance. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Eventually, we found out we were in a region between the Oder and the Neisse. And so this-- and not very far from the Czech frontier, the Sudeten mountain range, or what they called the Eulengebirge. So we knew we were in the eastern part of Germany, which was Germany. Of course, now this territory is Poland. But pre-1945 this belonged to Germany. We were tipped out of there. And the next thing that happened is our group of 100 were led to one side. There was a group of 200, like I explained to you-- 
INT: OK. KHM: --which didn't-- wasn't on our list. And this group was then transferred, which we didn't know for a long time. That transpired much, much later. Almost after-- sometime after the war-- they were transferred to the Schindler camp in Brunnlitz. By that time Schindler's camp was already in Czechoslovakia, because he'd relocated from where he was in Cracow. And 200 of these women were taken to his camp. So somewhere along the line we were together with these 200 Schindler women. But we were not on their list, and so they didn't take us. We-- we were a 100 group that was specifically chosen to work in a-- another factory in-- in a place called Reichenbach, not all that far away from Gross-Rosen. I don't remember how we got there. I think we-- we-- they took us there on lorries or trucks. When we got to this place, it-- it was a small camp situated on the outskirts of the town. But the actual factory was somewhere in the town. It was known as the Telefunken factory pre-war. But at that stage it was already part of the Phillips concern, and it-- it was an electronics plant. Of course, we never really got to know exactly what we were-- what's being produced there. But we-- we-- we think it was parts for aircraft, making valves. And in that I mean actually producing the vacuum that-- that produces-- produces valve for some aircraft. And we were taken to this small camp, and sorted out. And not everybody was doing the same sort of work. My mother and Mrs. Selinger-- [INAUDIBLE] that's Henya's mother-- they were simply sorting screws. That's all they were doing. They had trays of screws, and they're sitting there sorting screws. I-- I had a very peculiar sort of job to do. I was sitting next to a civilian German woman. And she wasn't allowed to speak to me, because there was no communication between the civilians-- [INAUDIBLE] the civilians working there and the prisoners. The whole place was patrolled by the SS women, and there was no contact. Well, I was just shown what I had to do. And what I really had to do was to take cylinders of I think it was carbon dioxide. I don't know what it was. But cold cylinders. If some of that stuff went on your skin, it burned. So it was cold like ice, but it burnt like fire. And these cylinders had to be changed in this particular machinery or whatever it was. And this German woman was sitting at the control panel, and looking and watching her dials. And every so often she would tell me I have to go and change the-- these cylinders. So this was pretty well my occupation. What I didn't realize, it was quite a dangerous procedure. Because this-- this thing was-- not only was it explosive or whatever, but it-- it-- if it overflowed, it burnt your skin. And this is what happened to me. I-- it had actually burnt me through my clothes one day. One of these cylinders overflowed, and I had a huge burn on my hand. And this civilian German woman mentioned this to the patrolling SS woman. And she was told off, to mind her own business. So that's, I think, where she realized, you know, what is happening. And there is no such thing as anyone looking after you, or whatever. And from that time on she used to leave me a sandwich every day. [INAUDIBLE] there was like a control panel with all these dials and a shelf underneath. And every day there was a sandwich there for me that this German woman would leave. But I couldn't communicate with her. I couldn't even say thank you or anything else. I just managed to take it and put it somewhere in my pocket. And when I got back to the camp we used to share it out with my mother. Because food became incredibly scarce there, because it wasn't like you could organize in Auschwitz, you see. There-- there was food coming in from outside. Not only was Auschwitz [INAUDIBLE] the greatest black market in Europe. But it was self-sufficient. You know, people were working on farms and fields, cattle, fisheries. And so things were smuggled into the camp. There you had a very small camp on the outskirts of this town. And it was strictly a ration of food coming in. And there was no way of obtaining any extra. So there we began to experience the starvation again, because the rations became smaller. 
INT: Do you recall what your meals were? KHM: There were no such thing as meals. It was your-- your bread and-- and your midday soup. Those were your meals.  INT: Who-- who dished it out to you? KHM: It was brought in. It was usually brought in, in-- into the camp. And midday soup was brought in and dished out, ladled out, pretty well as it was in the camp. But about-- [INAUDIBLE] interesting what was the-- what was there, that, for instance, if we wanted to go to the toilet, and you were working all day, you couldn't actually go. But every so often the SS woman would come and say, [GERMAN]. That means everybody, all everywhere in the holes, they all had to come. Everybody had to go to the loos together. So she used to collect all these workers. We were-- we were sort of a group of 100. She used to collect us. And we all had to go to these loos. Well, you can imagine what happened. They probably had two loos or something, and about a hundred people. But we-- we were actually delighted with this, because it gave us a-- a chance-- in those loos there was actually water-- running water. And sometimes there was hot water. And I did explain. My mother had sort of dishes where she was sorting out screws. And she used to smuggle a dish or two into the toilet. And we used to have a bath in a little dish like that, and actually wash ourselves, because there was no running water back in the camp. And so this is what I recall from-- from there. And the other thing I recall at-- the camp was on the outskirts of the town. And it took about a good hour to walk to the factory. And we-- we were accompanied by usually a guard at the back, and-- and perhaps some SS women at the side. Now we-- we used to pass-- we used to actually pass through the town quite early in the morning. And you could smell bread being baked in the bakeries. And, you know, you could just about see people getting up. It was quite early in the morning. And we had one or two girls that were just incredible. They-- they were able to snatch the SS women's food out of their hoppers-- out of their rucksacks that they had. And-- and so-- so we had to-- well, I don't know whether they allowed themselves to be raided. I don't know. But she always managed to get some food from the SS on-- on the way to work. 
INT: Did any civilians [INAUDIBLE] going through the town ever show you any kindness? KHM: No. No we never saw any civilians. We left very early in the morning. The bakers, well, you could-- well, the bakeries were working, obviously, because we-- we could smell, you know, fresh bread being baked, and curtains being sort of opened. It was very early in the morning. So I think we must have-- we-- we passed a empty town. And the same at night. We never saw anybody. Whether they took us from to the outskirts, somewhere where there were no people, I don't know. We never encountered any civilians in the town itself, although we were-- we-- we definitely passed the town. Streets in the town.  INT: Any idea of what hours you worked-- how many hours you worked? KHM: Well, usually you worked a 12-hour shift. And then, of course, you had the walk to the camp and back, as I said, to-- to-- to the factory in the morning, and back in the evening.  INT: Sleeping facilities [INAUDIBLE]? KHM: Sleeping-- well, what happened there, as I said, the 100 of us had-- we had just one hut. And we had two to a bunk. Now sometimes it-- it was very cold winter [INAUDIBLE]. We're now talking about going on for winter in 1944. Probably even January 1945. And there was no heating at all in-- in-- you know, in this hut. And it was so terribly cold. But there was a way of actually making a fire there. It was like a stove. But of course we had nothing to make fire with. So gradually we chopped up the-- the slats on the beds, and eventually [INAUDIBLE] we chopped up so many slats and made fire every night that in the end there was like a straw mattress that had a hollow in the middle. There was just one slot at each end. And one day there was a inspection. And they realized that we were chopping up these boards. And everybody was punished. I-- I can't remember the exact punishment. But I don't know-- they withheld food and things like that. That was the punishment. But they also decided to punish-- to take-- making-- make an example of one person. And they decided-- they pulled out-- they'd pull out my friend Henya, of all people. She was a very beautiful girl. And they decided to shave her hair as a punishment. And she actually lost all her hair then, because she'd had-- she already had quite long hair, and they shaved her head. That-- that was sort of the punishment for chopping up the boards. But by that time, as I say, it was January. But we were not ill-treated. We were left in relative peace once we got back to the-- to the actual camp. And in the factory we were harassed, but not beaten. So it was a-- a relatively quiet period of November, December, January 1945, except for the starvation. But what we didn't know is that the whole area had something like a hundred camps all around. [INAUDIBLE] ammunition-- the whole of the ammunition industry seemed to have been relocated into this region between the Oder and the Neisse, with Gross-Rosen being the base camp, and then all these camps all around. And prisoners were working in-- in different camps, in different factories. And they were also working in subterranean plants within the Eulengebirge, the-- the Sudeten range-- mountain range. And they were-- they-- they had high-tech electronics plants there. I believe they were making parts for the V-2 bombs. And also they were constructing the new Hitler headquarters. That's what most of the men were working, in-- in a quarry inside the mountains of-- of-- of the Sudeten range. But we didn't know that at the time. 
INT: Did you ever have any opportunity to sabotage the work that you were doing? KHM: We did a lot of sabotaging. We didn't do any good work whatsoever. I mean, I had to replace these cylinders. There was no way of not replacing the cylinders. But there were other work that were sabotaged all the time. People managed to sabotage. And then the next place we worked, we-- we didn't do anything-- anything of any-- any usefulness whatsoever. But the-- the thing that I, of course, didn't know, until I read Albert Speer's book, by Gitta Sereny, where Speer describes that particular factory, or she actually got it out of him, because he always maintained he didn't know what happened to the forced laborers, or the-- the slave labor. Now he said that there was a group of people who were working in the Reichenbach factory-- that was factory where I was working-- who were specially trained to do some very intricate electronic work. Now it certainly wasn't my group, and possibly there was another group. Because there was another plant like this in a place called Porta Westfalica, which was also part of Philips-- of a Philips factory. And he wanted this particular group that worked in Reichenbach to be relocated to Porta Westfalica. [INAUDIBLE] we didn't know all this. This is what I read. And I-- I then [INAUDIBLE] and then realized that it was my group, that-- because I didn't know how I-- eventually I found myself in Porta Westfalica. Why Porta Westfalica? But it transpired that there was this group of such people that were specially trained in these electronics-- women with very nimble fingers, apparently-- that were trained to do this particular work at-- at Philips. And because this was a subsidiary of Philips, they wanted these particular-- he, Speer, on his specific orders wanted this group to be transported to Porta. But of course, other things happened in the meantime. 
INT: [INAUDIBLE] how long were you in Reichenbach? KHM: Well, we were in Reichenbach the whole of winter, the whole of-- the latter part of '44, the beginning of '45, when rumors begun to circulate that the Russian front is not very far away. And indeed, early February, 1945, we could already hear, in a far distance, we could hear artillery fire. And we knew that the front couldn't be all that far away. And that became much closer. But what happened next was that there was a general roll call. We didn't-- we were not taken out to the factory on about the 17th of February. And on the 18th of February there was a order of what was known as a Marschbefehl. That means the order to march out. And in fact what I didn't know at that time, it was the first phase of evacuation from this area. They were evacuating this whole area. They were evacuating it in phases. And that was the 18th of February. And I believe on the 17th of February, that the day before a part of the Russian forces had already occupied Gross-Rosen, which was even a little further west. But we were slightly southeast. And they hadn't. So they drove us out the morning of the-- the 18th, which was just 24 hours prior to-- to liberation.  INT: So what happened to-- to your group at that time? KHM: Well, my group was taken out. And we were just told that this Marschbefehl-- we didn't know what it meant, of course. And we were lined up to be-- to be marched out. We-- we didn't know. We-- we thought we were all-- 
INT: All of you? KHM: Well, there were a hundred of us. But we soon learned that we were joined by hundreds and thousands of others from all the surrounding areas. But we didn't know that there were all these surrounding camps. But we found ourselves in a column that might have been between 5,000 and 10,000. I wouldn't know. But certainly most of the women working in all these different factories, they were all evacuated more or less at the same time.  INT: Were they women, or men and women? KHM: Only women. No, we-- we never saw the men. The men were evacuated separately. No, it was just a column of women that was marched out-- a long, long column of women.  INT: Any children? KHM: Oh, no, no. No, no children. No, those were all-- all--  INT: --any idea-- KHM: Mostly-- oh, mostly people that had been evacuated from Auschwitz to all these different factories that were working in-- in this area. And there were different death marches with different routes. But my route took-- well, I think. Because what happened was as we were marching out we realized that the main roads were absolutely choked with military convoys.  INT: Who were the military? KHM: German military convoys. Now we don't know whether they were retreating, or whether they were going to the front. I don't know. But they were military convoys. And the-- the-- some roads were choked with German refugees that were running away from the Russians. So they didn't want to mix all these people up. And then, of course, there were the slave, the prisoners who were being evacuated. So we were normally taken through the country, through villages and outlying pathways and hardly ever passed any towns. All we passed were isolated farms. But we were heading south nonetheless because eventually we reached a mountain range. And that mountain range was south. And it was possibly south, southwest, not southeast, because the Russians were approaching, and from the-- from the east. So I-- it was towards the southwest that we were taken. 
INT: Were you on foot all the time? KHM: We were-- we were on foot. And the columns stretched as far as your-- your eye could possibly see. And our guards were no longer the SS. They were mostly the Wehrmacht, the soldiers, what I term today elderly. But they were certainly not young people. There were those that could no longer fight on the front. And there-- there were some women [INAUDIBLE] as well. But mostly they were men-- soldiers with-- with rifles. And they-- they were leading the columns. And-- the-- the-- the column. And the-- the column got longer and longer, because it sort of stretched out. But we soon realized that people that were starting to lag behind were either clubbed to death or they were shot. Anyone that was stumbled or stayed behind or got out of line were shot. And we realized that one has got to, A, stay together, and B, stay to the front if you wanted to stay alive.  INT: How many of the hundred were there? KHM: We were all 100. We were the complete 100. And we tried to stick together within this enormous group of people. Because--  INT: How do you think you succeeded in this? KHM: Well, we-- we were pretty successful in sticking together. And we found that when someone lagged behind from the 100, some of us would pull them up and drag them along. And if-- if somebody stumbled, they were pulled up, and they were carried. And at one stage I was carried. And we had to carry my mother at one stage. So the 100, we do-- looked after one another during the death march. And not-- not only that, but no food was issued at all during the death march. Well, we were not issued with any food. 
INT: I see. KHM: That's right. Now the ones that couldn't in any way obtain food began-- became too weak. They started lagging behind. And once they lagged behind they got killed. Some of them tried to run away. But of course there was nowhere to run at that stage. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] At night there were-- there were different places where we stayed at night. Sometimes it was just on the open ground, just wherever, when it began to get dark we just staying on the open ground. Occasionally we were not far from some villages or barns. And then some of us got into the barns. Some of us raided farms-- milked the cows in the cow sheds, and found eggs, and even got as far as some of the farmers, and got some food from them. Or carts would pass by, and we'd throw ourselves onto carts and take some food. But those that didn't didn't survive the death march. It was either you took a chance, which was very precarious. Mostly we waited until the guards were asleep, because the guards became just as tired as we were. And we kept an eye on the guards. So when the guards were asleep, and whoever could-- could check, some of us would move away from the column and go into the neighborhood to see if we could find some food. Also fields, or whatever there was in the fields. We would find little cabbages in the fields, and raw potatoes, or whatever there was. We would try and obtain food. Now one night we slept in a coal mine. And you couldn't actually get anything in a coal mine. We all came out absolutely covered in-- in black dust. One night I had in a-- in a cow-- [INAUDIBLE] what was it? In a pig sty. That's it. One night I-- I remember was a pig sty. And I actually fell into a heap of manure, because it totally dark. And I came out covered in this heap of-- in this manure the next day. We eventually got to the foot of the mountains. And we had to climb the-- the-- the mountain range called the Eulengebirge. And it-- it was a climb quite high, the summit. I don't know now. But it would possibly be well over a thousand meters high. And we climbed up a path onto the summit. And when we got to the top it was the most incredible sight that met our eyes. It was early dawn when we got to the top. I always thought when we got to the summit we would just descend the other side. And it was, you know, top of a mountain.
But in fact it wasn't. It was a completely flat plateau. And it was early morning. And we were in the-- most of our Auschwitz group were pretty well in the front. We kept to the front, although it wasn't easy. But we did try to keep to the front. And what we saw were refugees-- German refugees-- sleeping, many of them still sleeping in carts. And they had kind of hoods over their-- you know, almost like the gypsy caravans. And they had like cattle with them as well-- cows. And when I saw these cows, the first thing that happened, I just ran for a cow, and started milking this cow. And we all had little beakers [INAUDIBLE] milking this cow. And the next thing was I was flung. I was actually kicked. And my mother came running. She said, [INAUDIBLE] what are you doing? This is not a cow. This is a bull. You can't milk a bull. Because I didn't know. At that stage you might know. Anyway, I picked myself up and milked the next cow that I could find. And by that time there was an absolute commotion, because people were coming up the hill. All this column was coming up the hill. And we got entangled with all these refugees, which they tried to avoid. I think the guards didn't know it was on the top there, you see. They were horrified when they found all these civilian people there, and we got mixed up with these civilians. And we started raiding their carts. We threw ourselves onto the carts, and pulled out sausages. And on one cart I dislodged a bucket. [INAUDIBLE] I didn't know what was inside of it. It was [INAUDIBLE] like an-- metal bucket was hanging [INAUDIBLE] from the top. And I-- I grabbed this bucket. And my mother said, what is it? What is it? I says, I don't know. It looks like lard. Oh, she said, marvelous. We're going to smear our bodies with this lard, because it was so intense cold, you know, and we didn't have any gloves. And by that time many of us were already barefoot, because our feet were sore, and many of us were just had the rags on-- on their feet, you know, after days of walking. And we smeared our feet with the-- with these lard-- with this bucket of lard. And it saved a lot of us from-- from frostbite. And so this-- this was the situation there. And, of course, the guards tried to lash out. They couldn't shoot, because we were mixed up, you see, with all the civilians. But they were just clubbing with their-- with their rifle butts. But nonetheless eventually they got us together, and we began the descent into Czechoslovakia. 
INT: All right. We'll take a break there.  INT: Tape 13, Kitty Hart-Moxon, 9th of June, 1998. You literally climbed up the mountain and you-- KHM: --raided the-- the German refugees--  INT: --refugee camp. KHM: And then, yes, it was a sort of camp. That's right. They were just waking up. And we descended-- we started to descend the other side. Of course, this was the border between Czechoslovakia and Germany. And we descended into Czechoslovakia. Now again we passed--  INT: Still in the column. KHM: --sorry?  INT: Still in the column of-- KHM: Well--  INT: --ex-prisoners, or-- KHM: Still-- still in a column of many, many people. A lot of them had by that time died of starvation, of having lagged behind, of weakness, or simply being clubbed to death trying to obtain food. Now that nearly happened to me, because as carts were passing by-- you see, farmers were still working on their farms. Not all people were running away. So there were farmers on, especially in-- on the Czech side, there were farmers working in the fields. And they would pass us by with their load of potatoes or beet or whatever. And as soon as we saw something resembling a horse and cart, many of us threw ourselves onto the cart, especially the Auschwitz group. We knew what to do. We knew we had to-- some of us had to get onto the cart and get some stuff. I threw myself on one of the carts. And I was clubbed with a rifle butt on my head. And I was actually knocked out. And I fell to the ground. And it-- it was part of the-- the Auschwitz group, and my mother who actually dragged me along. And I believe they-- they carried me for some time, because I'd lost consciousness. And eventually I came to. And I-- I continued walking. Eventually it transpired I actually had a fractured skull. But I wasn't actually shot. I was absolutely clubbed with a-- with a rifle butt on-- on my head. I've still got a-- a dent in my head. But nonetheless who didn't take a chance and try and get some food, all these people that didn't try didn't survive the death march. There-- there the difficulty was on the death march that the guards were not going to carry their own belongings. They each had like a blanket and a rucksack and goodness knows what. And they used to pass this down the line and expect us to carry this for them. All they would carry was their guns and the rifles. And everything else had to be carried for them. [? Of course, ?] nobody wanted to carry it, so you passed it down the line.
And, of course, when it came to the end of the line, those people were eventually shot, because they-- they couldn't carry it, you see? So that was another difficulty. This is why it was so important to [INAUDIBLE] stay to the front of the column. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] This went on for I don't know how many days. We got into Czechoslovakia. And we eventually reached a-- a-- a place called Parschnitz, which was a camp somewhere on the outskirts of a town of Trautenau. Parschnitz I've never found on the map, so maybe this was just a name of the camp. But I know there was a town called Trutnov-- Trautenau. This is-- this is how far we got. I don't think it was a huge, huge distance, but it was a very difficult ascent to the top, and a descent. And it wasn't just a road. It was a climb up and a climb down, and it was quite a difficult walk without proper footwear, and without food, and of course the extreme cold. At-- at one stage we passed, before we got on the mountain range, we passed a very, very beautiful part of the country called the Sachsische Schweiz, sort of the Saxony, the Swiss Saxony, with beautiful scenery. And-- and my-- my mother used to say, now don't think about your feet and don't think about your hunger. Just look at the beautiful scenery. That'll take your mind off everything else. And so this is sort of stuck to my mind. We used to try and look at the scenery and not think about what was happening to us. But eventually we landed in this camp of Parschnitz. I-- I think we were there on-- on only a very short time, because the-- well, first of all they were already evacuating the place as well. They-- they already had the-- the Marschbefehl, the, you know, the order to evacuate. And secondly, of course there was this group of 100 who we were supposedly to be transported elsewhere, you know. So they-- they called out this group of 100. And we were still all intact, still all alive. And they loaded a group-- quite a large group of people, possibly-- possibly about 3,000, something like this. And this time we were loaded onto open coal trucks. They were open. They were sort of to-- to somewhere here. And they-- they were obviously coal trucks, because they were all full of coal dust. We were piled into these trucks. And the first thing that happened is each truck had a guard. And the guard had a piece of chalk. And he marked out his area. And that was going to be his place where he was going to sleep.
And he announced, anyone stepping over the line will be shot. Well, they didn't just joke. That was a fact, that if you stepped over you were killed. So everybody tried to keep away. And we were-- we were all huddled together in the-- in about one or two thirds of this truck. And he had this whole section all to himself. Now we were in this particular train which went from Czechoslovakia and eventually landed up in a place called Porta Westfalica, which was the northeast-- northwest of Germany, not all that far from the Dutch frontier. And we were in the coal trucks eight-- either eight days and seven nights, or seven days and eight nights. One or the other. And many times during this journey we'd passed places that we'd seen two or three days previously. So the train had stopped, and then it turned around, and then it went round. And then it started. And then you stopped again. And then there were air raids. Part of the tracks were bombed. We-- we experienced several air raids, because they thought we were a military convoys, that-- bombed by the Allies by that time. They thought we were a military convoy, and they-- they bombed the train. When-- when there was an air raid, the guards would jump off and go into the ditches. But we were not allowed to leave the train. But we were not actually hit. Only the track-- the actual track was hit. So when the track was hit, the train was shunted back. And then it stood for hours. And-- and now the other thing that happened is of course we-- we didn't get any food. In Parschnitz, in that camp, I managed to trade in my one gold sovereign. And I still had my diamonds and my-- those [? teeth ?] that I had. And for that, I managed to obtain a loaf, which was the most incredible, precious thing that you could possibly imagine, a whole loaf in Parschnitz. And Henya, my friend, she did the same. She managed that. I don't know what she had. She also had to pay for it. And we-- we each-- we were in this whole group, one family. You must remember, all your family were looking after one another. But outside your family you couldn't help people and feed everybody. So our group, the others also, some of the Auschwitz girls also managed to get something, because of course they were very experienced prisoners. They'd been-- most of [? had been ?] in Auschwitz a long time. We sat on this bread, believe you me, for eight days. My mother and I sat on our loaf.
And every so often we would sit on it and just nibble a little piece of bread. This is-- this is all. We had no drink. It rained and it snowed, and we collected rain and snow in our mugs. [INAUDIBLE] we still had little mugs with us. And so this-- this is what we drank. Now at one stage, when the train stopped, it stopped near a heap of rotten cabbages. And despite all the screams and shouts of the guards, some of-- some of us climbed down and got some of these rotten cabbages. But everybody that had these cabbages had the most awful diarrheas. You can imagine what that was like in the open truck for seven nights, or eight days. In fact, if you had to go, you-- you had to be held by two people. And you sat on the edge of this truck. And you had to let it go over to the side, because they wouldn't let us dismount and-- and go and-- and do anything, although the guards went off, you see. When the train stopped, the guards got off. But they wouldn't let us get off. Anyway, we were-- we-- we-- let's say we were in-- in this-- in this train all these days. I mean, we were-- it was raining. It was snowing. It was absolutely freezing cold. We were all huddled together, because by that time many of us had suffered frostbite. And many were too weak to-- to stand up. And eventually we got to a place called Porta Westfalica. But how I know, because there was actually a proper station. And it said Porta Westfalica. Otherwise we wouldn't have even known where we were. But it said Porta Westfalica. And the next thing was there were shouts, alles raus. All out. Well, there were a lot of people that could not longer get out. We-- we lost a lot of the convoy. A lot of the girls had died. But all our Auschwitz group were still alive, and we still were able to stand up and-- and march off. So all 100 of us survived the eight days on the train-- in the-- in the open coal trucks. And we-- we were tipped out and marched off to another camp. Now that camp, again, it was a very small little camp. And it was run by Dutch prisoners, all from Holland. They were all women. It was a women's camp. And all the women, these Dutch girls, were working in-- in ammunition plants. And they were supposedly have been the girls trained with this very intricate electronics maneuver that they had to do in this electronic plants. And we were supposed to have been the experts having brought all the way from Reichenbach to-- to join this group to work in this factory. Because it was relocated by that time. You see, they started evacuating. They still wanted to produce whatever they were producing-- I don't know-- in this electronic factory. And so we were sorted out in this camp. And by that time my mother didn't have to work anymore. She wasn't selected for work, fortunately. She-- because-- 
INT: Who selected people for work? KHM: Well, they had kapos, and then ruling parties in the camp, just like everywhere else. So--  INT: Still Germans? The Wehrmacht, or-- KHM: No. They were-- the-- the-- the camp was run by the SS, and it-- it was run by Dutch prisoners. But it was a work camp, as compared to a concentration camp. So it was run on the-- on the-- on the lines of a work camp. It was-- I-- I don't even know the-- it was a subcamp of-- it might have been Neuengamme. I don't know. Possibly. Because it was-- and that was the nearest base camp, I think. We were told we were going to work in this munition plants, we'd-- plant-- which was a Philips factory requisitioned by the Germans from Philips in Holland, I take it, and relocated inside a forest. Now I believe the forest was called the Teutoburger Wald, but I couldn't swear to it. It was somewhere near the Teutoburger Wald. Somewhere there there is a very well-known monument. And somewhere not far from there was this munition plant, which we believed the-- the Allies never knew of its existence, because it was inside the mountain. It was underground. And we were taken 11 shafts underground. And right underground was-- was this munition plant going full [INAUDIBLE], making what we didn't quite know, parts for aircraft we gather, or-- again, the same sort of electronics factory, as similar to what we worked in Reichenbach. 
INT: Can you explain the 11 shafts? KHM: Well, all I can tell you is it was a kind of makeshift lift. And it-- it was, what I recall, it wasn't a proper lift. It was-- [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] it was almost like ropes on-- on the platform which took you down. And it took you so many floors down. And I believe ours was the 11th-- 11th shaft down the hole that we worked.  INT: From the surface. KHM: From the surface, yes. And the-- the-- the air was incredibly, well, it-- it wasn't well ventilated. And people became drowsy. And the-- the-- the German civilian population, the civilians working there, only worked very short shifts. They-- they worked like five, six-hour shifts. And they had to come back to the surface. But we were there 12 hours in these holes. Now the work wasn't particularly arduous. And I couldn't tell you what we were doing. We were not doing anything that of any use. That is where a lot of sabotage went on, because we didn't actually do anything. And whatever we were told to do, we didn't do it the way we were told to do it. So that is where there was plenty of opportunity to sabotage. But the girls who were supposedly these experts, they could no longer at that stage carry out their work, because their supplies were no longer arriving, and, you know, the parts that they needed. So all they were doing is sitting at sewing machines and sewing uniforms for the-- for-- for-- for the-- for the army. Well, we were still sort of fiddling around and doing intricate things, but sabotaging at the same time-- not doing any useful work whatsoever. 
INT: Did you walk to and from work? KHM: Oh, yes.  INT: Yeah. KHM: It was a very long way. And-- and the-- the walk was through the forest. And-- and at that stage I remember we were actually eating acorns. There-- there were forests. There were sort of acorns in the forests. And this is what we brought back to the camp. And we actually made a meal of acorns. The camp soup, strange enough, was better than we'd encountered in many of the other camps. And my mother always used to keep some for me, so when-- when I got back to the camp there was always some hot soup for me that my mother prepared, [INAUDIBLE] because she no longer went out to work there.  INT: Did-- did the local population see you marching to and fro? KHM: No. We, no, no, no. We didn't-- we didn't see-- we didn't see anyone other than some personnel, German personnel, that were working in the plant. But what we didn't know is there were-- in the surrounding area there were numerous other camps. We were only one of the camps. And then there were others. There were-- apparently there was a sort of a former hotel which had been converted into a concentration camp. And they had male prisoners there working in-- in some of the quarries and-- and some of the plants, because they constructed the plant, I believe, you see. So there were lots of other prisoners there. But we never saw them, and we never met them. This is-- this is only what I learned after the war. 
INT: Were you still aware of the progress of the war? KHM: No, because by that time we'd been taken so far west that we had lost any sound of-- of-- of a front approaching. And of course we didn't know how far the Allies were on-- on-- on the Western Front. We were talking-- this is now March-- March-- we're talking about March already, 1945. And we didn't hear. I mean there's only what you could hear. And we didn't hear any gunfire and-- and artillery fire at that stage round there whatsoever. So we didn't know where the front was. There were rumors that there was approaching Allied armies. But we didn't know where they were.  INT: When did you become aware of the-- of the war coming to an end? KHM: Well, during March we were-- early March possibly it was, yes-- we were actually-- there was again what was known as a Marschbefehl. In other words, there was an order to evacuate. And we realized then that if they are beginning to evacuate from the west, and are beginning to drive us back to the east, there must be-- the-- the Allies must be approaching from the west. This is how we became aware that there was a approaching front. But we-- we didn't-- we-- we could hear aircraft. But we didn't know where they were, and we didn't even know who they were. We-- it might have been German aircraft. You couldn't see them. I mean, we were, as I say, underground. And when we walked in the forest, we couldn't-- we couldn't identify who the people were, what sort of aircraft there were. But the next thing that happened was that there was a roll call, as usual. And during that roll call there was some commotion. We-- we got mixed up within the other prisoners-- the Dutch prisoners. And some-- some of our group [INAUDIBLE] was on one side, and the other, it was on the other side. And we realized that they were starting to count people. We could soon tell when they-- you were being counted. You were lined up in fives, and you were being counted. And Henya, my friend, decided she would-- she'd started a commotion. And she started to run to the other side and cause this commotion. And as there was a commotion, one of the SS officers approached. And as he approached I realized there was going to be trouble. And I hit her really hard, and knocked her down, and just to keep her quiet, and in event saved her life. Because she was trying to reach the other side of the column with her mother and with her sister. [INAUDIBLE] we were all together. We didn't know what was what at the time. But it transpired, when they were counting people, they divided the group, one group one way, one group another way. And the group that wasn't on my side was eventually led to the forest. And they were machine gunned. And we lost quite a lot of our Auschwitz girls, because Henya tried to reach our Auschwitz group. And I was on the other side of the group with the, as I say, Henya, whom I'd knocked to the ground. And eventually we rescued her. And there were possibly about between 12 and 14 or 15 of us of the Auschwitz group who were led away. And we'd lost at that point-- almost a point of liberation-- we lost a large part of our original 100 group at that point, because there was no room on the trucks. That's all there was. There was only room for so many on the trucks. And we didn't know that, but they divided up the group. And one group was led away, and in-- and after the war we were told they were machine-gunned. They-- they-- they didn't take them anywhere. And we were led back to the trucks. And we were taken-- we gathered we were taken northeast. And they took us to a place called Fallersleben, which was a bombed-out, disused DKW factory-- car factory. By that time, I think they thought there was still some work to be done there. But there wasn't. The whole building-- building had been damaged. And it was bombed out. And there there were some other prisoners there that were in that particular factory. And we-- we joined that factory. We joined these prisoners. And we were there some days. I couldn't tell you how many days. In fact, we were hardly guarded in that place by that time. If you wanted to, you could have escaped if you knew how to-- where to go, and how to escape. But of course it was not a possibility. 
INT: How did you look after yourself? How did you-- KHM: In-- in-- in.  INT: [? --feed? ?] KHM: Well, in that factory we did quite a few incredible things actually. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] They had a SS kitchen there. And they had air raids. Now one day there was an air raid. And they said, everybody's got to go in-- into the air raid shelters, into the bunks. And they shoved us down below into some cellars. And we saw some airtight doors. And it just rang alarm bells in some of our brains. People who knew what airtight doors meant thought there was only one thing, and that is gas. And one of us said, 'Gas!' Just that [INAUDIBLE] one word. And there was a stampede. And everybody started rushing out. And there was an SS officer. And he said, halt. What's the matter? And there was one of our girls who went up to this SS officer. He was [INAUDIBLE] fat man. And she [? kind of went ?] up to him. And she shook him by the belt. And she said, we're not going in there. We're not going to be gassed in there. If you want to shoot us, kill us here now. We're not going in there. And he said just a minute. Tell me more. And he led us into a hall and wanted to know what this was all about, this gas. And that's where some of us explained to him about the gas chambers in Auschwitz, which he didn't know-- or said he knew nothing about. He was shaken-- visibly shaken. 
INT: Did you have a name for this man? KHM: No, no. I didn't have a name.  INT: No. KHM: No. Anyway, what happened after that is he said, all right, when there is an air raid you don't have to come down to the air raid shelter. You can stay up there. And if you want to get killed, you stay up there. Everybody else goes into the air raid shelters. Now of course there were-- there were raids all the time by that time. The next raid came almost a few hours later. And all the guards, and all-- a lot of the other prisoners went into the air raid shelters. But we stayed behind. And what did we do? We raided the SS kitchens while they were all in the-- in-- in-- in the shelters. And this is how we got food.  INT: Can you remember any of the food? KHM: Yes I do. I remember it very distinctly. We-- we got into this SS kitchen. And they had a great big bowl-- it was absolutely enormous-- full of grated raw potatoes. And I think that we're going to make potato cakes, or some such thing. And we-- we just got hold of this great big dish of grated potatoes. And this is what we ate-- raw potatoes. And we found other provisions and things like that that we raided this kitchen.
