And of course, when they came up, they were absolutely furious. But there wasn't any punishment after that. They were just, you know, just fuming and shouting and screaming. But nobody was actually beaten up at that stage anymore. Possibly they knew what was to come. [LAUGHS] They knew the Allied armies weren't too far away. But we didn't know. But the next thing that happened, we were led out of the factory and back into trucks and taken we didn't know where. But it transpired it was Belsen. And when we got there we didn't actually go into any camp whatsoever. They just took us into an enclosure-- simply an enclosure. There were no huts. There were no tents. There was nothing. We didn't see any people. We were not together with anybody else. We were just in an enclosure. And I-- I-- don't ask me how long we were there. There was nothing there. 
INT: Was it a fence, or a-- KHM: It was just a fenced-off area. Whether it was the perimeter of the main camp or whatever, I don't know. We were just put into that enclosure, and we were just left there.  INT: Were you told where you were going? KHM: No, no, no, no. No idea. We didn't-- this only we knew afterwards that that's where we were. No, we didn't know. We-- we knew we were outside Bergen. That's-- [INAUDIBLE] we knew it was Bergen. I can't remember how we knew that. I think we saw a name of a station somewhere, or something like this. But nobody told us. We were there. And the next thing is we were led out of there. And we were marched. They marched us out to some sidings. And I think-- I don't know, but I think it was somewhere outside Celle. And this is while it was all around the area of Belsen. And Celle is not very far from Belsen. That was the main road junction at Celle. And it had a lot of sidings. And there stood a number of trains-- cattle trucks. From some of the cattle trucks you could hear voices-- moans, cries, shouts of some of the trucks. But of some of the trucks you could hear nothing at all. Now you didn't know [INAUDIBLE] were they empty or were they not. But what we realized at that point that they were going to load us into these trucks, and that we were going to be abandoned, and sealed in the trains, and left to die. We-- we realized at that point this is what was going to happen. But [INAUDIBLE] before anybody collected their thoughts, we were pushed into the trucks. 
INT: What made you think that's what was going to happen to you? KHM: Well, it made us think, because we heard moans and muffled sounds from some of the trucks. And we realized that these people must have been in the trucks a long time, and the sound that came from them. They were weak, or weakened. And we realized there were people in there. They must have been there for some time. And they weren't going anywhere. There weren't any engines. There were just simply trains standing there.  INT: What could you see? KHM: Trains. Trucks.  INT: But what could you see-- KHM: Out?  INT: --on the trucks? KHM: Oh, no. We don't-- nothing.  INT: On the outside? KHM: No, just cattle trucks. Yeah.  INT: You could hear the-- KHM: You could hear voices.  INT: Yeah. KHM: You could hear voices from some of the trucks.  INT: Could you smell anything? KHM: They were sealed. They were sealed-- sealed cattle trucks.  INT: Were there any guards around? KHM: No. No, we didn't see anybody. That is what-- what made us think that these trucks-- these people were left abandoned. I think there were some trains that were moving backwards and forwards somewhere or other. But I-- I don't remember seeing any people around. No.  INT: Did you-- KHM: Not outside the trucks.  INT: Did you have any guards with you at the time? KHM: Yes. There were guards. They were pushing us into the-- into the trucks. And the trucks that they-- I was pushed into, it became very apparent what the truck was, because it smelt of cheese. And we suddenly realized that that's going to be airtight. It didn't have any-- it didn't have any air come in whatsoever. You know, there-- there wasn't any spokes. There wasn't any slots. There wasn't any windows. The-- there was no barbed windows. There was just nothing. They were sealed trucks. And we-- at least we were pushed into that sealed truck, my group that I was together with. 
INT: Right. KHM: Pushed into that truck.  INT: We'll take a break now.  INT: [INAUDIBLE] Tape 14, Kitty Hart-Moxon, 9th of June, 1998. Kitty, you're in a place outside of Bergen in northern Germany somewhere. And you-- you've just been pushed into a truck by your guards, your group. KHM: Yes. We-- we were pushed into this truck, and the truck was sealed. And it was-- it was absolute total darkness. And soon we realized that it was pretty airtight, because there-- there wasn't any opening. There wasn't any window. There wasn't-- there were no-- no bit that was barred. And it became-- within the shortest time-- it became incredibly stuffy inside. And the-- the walls were wet with perspiration, you know. There was just running-- running water, or condensation, shall I say, not-- condensation down the sides of the truck. People started to scream and shout, and eventually become unconscious. And I realized that there was only one way to have air, and that is to have a look on the floor. And sure enough, there were gaps in the truck. And you could actually see the track. You could see-- you could see through the gaps. And I-- I had a knife, which was a forbidden weapon for any prisoner. But I managed to enlarge a crack. And my mother and I took it in turns to breathe through this crack. And believe me, this was the worst experience of the whole war, because at that stage we thought we were going to suffocate. And no way were we going to get out of this truck alive. People were getting unconscious, and some were dying, and-- 
INT: Was the truck moving? KHM: Eventually the truck moved. But it didn't go very far. Now we didn't know whether it moved because they were loading other people in from different places. And eventually it stopped. We didn't know where it stopped. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] And it became very quiet. And people were becoming extremely drowsy. And really at that stage everybody thought that we were going to die, because we were just left abandoned. There wasn't a sound from outside. If you were quiet, it was total silence. There wasn't a sound. Don't ask me how many hours we were there. We were-- impossible to say. We were in total darkness. And it could have been hours. It could have been 24 hours. I don't know. Suddenly we heard some footsteps, like on a gravel. It was obviously gravel outside. And you could hear footsteps-- not many, but some footsteps. And it certainly wasn't a lot of people. So it wasn't people being loaded in or anything like that. And as we heard these footsteps, as they became closer, we started screaming and shouting and banging at the side of the truck, open up, open up, because we are all suffocating in here! Open up! And suddenly, for some unknown reason I'm sure-- never know, one man began to unbolt the truck from the outside. We could hear-- because it was actually nailed to. We could-- we could hear some-- some nails of something going in as they were hammering it and-- and closing as a sort of iron bar. And all of a sudden we heard this release of this bar. And-- and there was this one SS man. And he opened up the-- the truck. And we were all pressing against the wall of the truck. And believe you me, everybody that was alive just fell out of the truck. And we fell out on-- on two or three men. I can't remember. It was two or three. And there were just these three men in green uniforms. And we fell onto them, fell straight out. And they were startled. Of course they didn't know what they had done, possibly. We were no longer in the same siding. We were seemed to have been elsewhere. And when we got out we were screaming and shouting and saying, kill us now! Just kill us here! We're not going back into this truck. We want to-- we want to be killed. And my mother got up, in her best German said, we want-- we demand to be taken into a concentration camp. So we actually begged to be taken into a camp at that stage. By that time nobody really wanted anything but breathing air. Nobody wanted a food or drink. All we wanted is to be able to breathe. And so these men conferred. And one of them went off in a bicycle. And he said he knew of a camp nearby, and he'd speak to the commandant. And if the commandant is willing to take us, they will take us there. And do you know, this was the only truck from this whole train that was opened. They didn't open another truck. And there were-- there was no sound from-- from-- from the other trucks at all. Now I now know where the siding was, because I actually went to see it two years ago outside the town of Salzwedel. There was-- there was a-- a-- a-- a siding with-- and-- and there were five lines there, apparently, five railway lines. And there were some five trains full of prisoners which were left abandoned. And these-- these corpses were found after the war. And the population of Salzwedel actually had to bury some of these people that they found in these trains that were sealed. And we were one of-- the one truck that was opened. There-- there were-- there were other trains that were brought to this siding, people that were taken to a place called Gardelegen, where they had the most huge, enormous massacre of men. And maybe I tell you that at a different stage, about the Gardelegen massacre. But some of the prisoners from Gardelegen were also on-- on this-- brought to this particular siding. And so I will tell you more about this siding. The result was that they-- they marched-- those that could still walk, because we lost quite a lot of people in this truck. Quite a lot of girls died during the period that we were locked in in that truck. We were taken to the small camp of Salzwedel, which was indeed just on the outskirts of the town. And it was a small work camp. And most of the prisoners, women prisoners who-- who were in that camp, came from the Lódz ghetto. And they worked in a chemical plant. But there were also some Hungarian girls. And they worked in a sugar refinery. And this is where I think we arrived, around about the beginning of April of 1945. 
INT: How many of the original 100 girls were in that group arriving? KHM: Well, I think we were around about 12, because we lost-- we lost most of them when we were segregated and divided outside Porta Westfalica. Because there, during this commotion, a lot of the Auschwitz girls got on to one side, and the ones that were in the-- in the-- in the other group, as I said, were machine gunned. So we have lost-- we-- we-- we-- at liberation we were around about a dozen Auschwitz-- original Auschwitz group at liberation.  INT: So we're at the beginning of April. KHM: We-- we come to the beginning of April.  INT: Did you walk? Who guarded you to this-- to Salzwedel? KHM: Well, the-- the-- the men who actually opened the truck took us into the camp of Salzwedel. And we were just dumped in in this camp. There wasn't any room for us there anyway.  INT: Were they glad to get rid of you? KHM: I should think so because the-- they couldn't get us back into the truck. They would have had to kill us. Or maybe at that stage they didn't want to do that, because it was pretty well the end of the war. And so they thought the best thing was to dump us in the next camp. And that was the nearest camp. And we were literally on the outside of the town. The siding was on the outside of-- outskirts of Salzwedel, because I've actually been to see the siding, where all these trains were left abandoned. 
INT: Did any of the local population see you at this stage? KHM: Not-- not at this stage. No, we were-- we were marched straight into the camp. And there was no more work in progress. Obviously the-- the-- the front was very close. And there were fighting. There were artillery-- by that time there was artillery fire. And we knew the front was very close. We didn't know who the front was-- were they British, were they Russian, were they Americans. We didn't know. We had no contact with a-- with-- with any civilian population whatsoever. The-- the girls who worked in the factories no longer worked, you see. They had all work-- there was no more work in progress. The-- everything had stopped. And they were just-- in-- in the camp there was no more food, in fact, handed out as such. There-- they were-- they were throwing in heaps of cabbages and raw beet. But-- but no food ration. That there just wasn't any more food available.  INT: Who was throwing in the food? KHM: From the SS stores they brought in cartloads of-- of cabbages, and sugar beet. And this is-- this is what we lived on the last two weeks of the war. But what happened in that camp was we realized that some of the girls that were in this camp were in a pretty good condition. Well, in a not too bad condition, shall I say. Of course, many of them were on a starvation diet, just like everywhere else. And they worked in the chemical factory. But there was a group of girls that worked in the sugar refinery. And we, some of our Auschwitz girls, realized somewhere in this camp there is food. Now we didn't know where. And we decided we've really got to have a look who hordes food and why and where. And if they do, they'd have to share it. And so one night we went on a raiding party into one of the neighboring huts, and overpowered some of the Hungarian girls who were working the sugar refinery, and found that they were hoarding sugar in their bunks. And so we had a fight. And our raiding party succeeded in obtaining some sugar. So the last few days in the camp we lived on some of the sugar that we raided off some of the girls that were hording the-- the food. 
INT: Did that create any animosity amongst-- KHM: Oh, yes. Oh, oh, yes. Enormous animosity, that's right. There were fights. But at that stage, there wasn't any more food in the camp. And whatever food there was had to be shared out.  INT: Were these Hungarian Jewish ladies? KHM: I think they were Hungarian working in the-- the-- yes. Because all the Lódz girls were working in the chemical plant. So I think there were-- the Hungarian-- a group of Hungarians were working in the sugar refinery. That's what we think, of, you know. Going by the language that they spoke, we'd concluded they were Hungarian.  INT: Who was responsible for sharing out the sugar when you discovered where it was? KHM: No, no, no. We're not sharing out sugar. There wasn't such thing as sharing out sugar. We simply stole it from them. No, no, no they-- they were not going to share it at all. We had to fight for it. Yes. There was actually a fight, yes, a battle between the prisoners over-- over some of the--  INT: How many people involved in that? KHM: I don't know. I don't know. But our raiding party was the whole Auschwitz party. We-- we-- we did-- we instigated the raid. [DRINKS]  INT: Were you joined by any others? KHM: No, we-- we think the others still had some provisions when they were working in the chemical plant.  INT: What did people do all day in this camp? KHM: Nothing at all anymore. Everybody was just sitting around, wandering around. Most people were dazed by that time, because they were-- as I say, food-- there just wasn't any more bread. Bread was life as far as-- 
INT: What about water? KHM: --rations were concerned. There wasn't any running water. There were tubs of water, buckets of water. But there wasn't any running water. Not-- not water that we could have access to, anyway.  INT: Where did this water come from? KHM: I don't-- couldn't tell you. It was probably polluted. But nobody cared at that stage. It wasn't-- it wasn't handed out as such.  INT: And what was the physical condition of the guards at that time? KHM: Oh, the guards? You're talking about the German guards? Oh, their physical condition was, the-- the SS guards-- they-- they were still patrolling and coming in and out. But they-- they-- they were not patrolling the camp too much. And now and again the camp was left totally unguarded on the inside. We didn't see any ruling hierarchy anymore, nor the prisoner hierarchy. So we don't know whether they actually had a hierarchy, as they had in another camp, or whether they had kapos or whatever. I don't know. We were the-- only there really literally a fortnight. But the next thing that happened was that the-- the-- the guards no longer began to come into the camp. Gradually they came in in civilian clothes. And so we realized, why are they coming in not in uniform? The next thing that happened is that-- and that was probably a few days prior to liberation by that time. Oh, there was-- there was a battle, not far, raging-- obviously the front was not far away, because bullets were flying, you know, and grenades right through the camp. And you had to duck and lie and dodge the-- the-- the actual front, the actual battle that was going on not all that far away. 
INT: Did you see any military? KHM: Not-- not at that-- no, no. We didn't see any military. All we heard was the whistling of the bullets and that were flying around. And it was very dangerous to actually be about. And some people actually got-- I don't know if they got killed. But they certainly got injured at that point. And so therefore, even the SS personnel no longer wanted to come into the camp. About a few days prior to liberation, a lot of leaflets began to-- scattered leaflets become to appear from nowhere. And these leaflets came from liberated French prisoners of war. There was a large French prisoner of war camp, also, prisoners working somewhere in the-- in the plants and things. And they had already been liberated. That's talking about a few days prior to liberation, but-- but somewhere not far from the camp. And these leaflets said that the whole camp was mined, and they were intending to blow up the camp.  INT: The camp you were in. KHM: Yes, that camp. They were going to blow it up prior to liberation, prior to being liberated by the Allies, because the order was that no prisoners were to fall into Allied hands, as you know, alive. No prisoners were to be allowed to be liberated. They also said that they were going to cut the electric fence at a certain stage when they knew it was safe to do that. And we were just sitting there tense waiting for something to happen, either to be blown up, or to be liberated, or what-- we knew something was very near. But many of us were too dazed by that time to really take an awful lot of notice. I remember sitting [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] at a fence looking out at the SS stores, because there were SS kitchens just the other side of the fence. And you could actually see whole rows of loaves, bread. You could see it. And it was just across the fence-- across the electrified fence, that is. And I was sitting there. I was sitting there for days, I think, just staring at this bread. By that time we were too weak to even walk around. Because we, you know, been going on for two weeks with hardly any food by that time. No way to organize, apart from a bit of sugar that we managed to organize from-- from these women. And you couldn't eat the-- the rotten sugar beet that were being thrown in, because you-- you would have been sick. And then about 24 hours prior to what happened next, one of the SS ventured into the camp.
And he didn't carry a gun. Well, that was quite apparent. Well, for a start, we knew that there was something up when they were not-- no longer carrying guns. They were no longer armed. Some of us threw ourselves onto this one lone SS personnel, and we tore his uniform to shreds. And as we tore his uniform, we all threw ourselves at this man, because he didn't have a firearm. He had a dagger on-- on this side. And I grabbed his dagger. I wasn't going to kill him at that stage, obviously, because we didn't know what was going to happen. But I-- I did manage to grab his dagger. At that point I think I grabbed it, because I knew if I didn't, he might kill us. But he was no longer armed. Anyway, we-- we-- we-- we actually beat him up, and tore all his clothes off him. And he escaped. And he got out. And that was possibly the last of the SS that we saw. What I learned after the war was that the commandant was actually quite a-- a decent sort of commandant. And he refused the order to blow up the camp. He refused it, because if he hadn't refused it, we would have been blown up, because the whole place was mined. Now we didn't know if the French prisoners of war had actually cut the wires. No one was prepared to touch the wires, because it meant instant death and suicide. And we-- we were just sitting there waiting. And it became deathly quiet. There was no more artillery-- nothing. And we thought, well, the-- the-- the front has moved, because there was no more artillery. There was no more shooting. There was no more firing, no more bullets flying. But what we didn't know, what we learned later, is that the town had capitulated actually. That we learned later. This is why it was quiet. The-- the-- the-- the actual town, the-- the-- the actual citizens of the town apparently negotiated with the German units and persuaded them not to fight and to destroy the town. Because there would have been total destruction of the town. And the town capitulated. And we didn't know this at the time. But the very next thing that happened was some very strange tanks started to move past the fence. And I was still standing there, sitting there, rather, watching the loaves, not taking any notice of anything else. Just mesmerized and hypnotized, looking at the loaves, waiting for a minute for anyone to say the high tension is off, or something. But the next thing that happened is somebody shook me and said, look. Look at these tanks. These are not German tanks. They've got a star. And we didn't know what the star was. But they were American tanks. And they just were just rolling past, and didn't even notice us. And there were screams and shouts, and everybody was screaming and shouting. And all of a sudden, out of nowhere, a tank just drove through the perimeter, right through the gate of the-- of-- and broke the fence. And it came straight into the camp. The first American tank came in. And that was the point of liberation. Now at that point I smashed the window of the SS stores. That was the first thing I did. I didn't even take any notice of these Americans. And I got into the SS stores, and I came out with a handful of loaves which were just across the fence of-- of-- of the-- of the perimeter of the fence. On the other side were the SS stores. And behind me was a mob-- mob of hundreds of women that threw themselves on me. And I eventually managed one loaf, which I managed. And I run back to my mother. And that was the first piece of bread, or first piece of food that I'd seen for two weeks-- or perhaps three weeks. Might have been three weeks. And that was the point of liberation. 
INT: What did you do immediately after that point, when you'd had your loaf with your mother? KHM: Well, we sat and chewed and chewed and chewed, as I say. And my mother kept saying, don't eat so much. Don't eat so much, because you're going to be ill. And, you know, we mustn't eat when our stomachs are not accustomed to eat. Just a little bit. Just a little bit. I know the gates were open. And what we didn't know is that there were quite a lot of forced labor camps, and Poles, and, well, who, who in the surrounding area also working in different munition plants. And they had already been liberated some days previously to us. We were the last lot to be liberated. And they had been looting the town. And so when our gates opened we joined all the people that were pouring into this small town of Salzwedel. And our first [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] thoughts were to destroy everything that came in our wake, everything that we saw. We had to destroy it. We didn't want anything. The only thing we really needed at that point was food. Everything else had to be destroyed. Now my mother, my mother was very weak at that point. In any case, she couldn't have no longer gone-- I didn't know where we found strength to do that. Because by the time I was weighed eventually and examined, I weighed some 5 stone. So my-- my mother was incredibly weak, and she could hardly walk by that time. But she did manage to get to-- to the outside of the gates and speak to the Americans. And her report from those very first days was almost the very first report that went out about Auschwitz to-- to the outside world. Because don't forget Auschwitz were liberated by the Russians. There was no communication with a-- with a-- what happened over on the Russian side. People didn't really know what had happened in Auschwitz. We were one of the first lot to be liberated on the 14th of April, because Belsen wasn't liberated till the 15th of April. Some places were not liberated until May. So we were almost one of the first to be liberated. There were one or two other pockets of places that were liberated. My-- my-- but my mother's report was one of the very first that went out, because she could speak English as well. So she was just sitting there talking and explaining all about Auschwitz, and all about what happened. But I joined the crowd into the city of-- into the little town of Salzwedel on a three-day rampage, which went on from morning to night, from house to house.
And we went from house to house. Mostly the population was hiding in cellars and in lofts. And some of them had left the town. We-- well, we did the most incredible things. We-- we got into a milk dairy, or something, and we upset all the churns, and actually bathed in milk, and drank it with our hands, and upset it all, just-- just to destroy it. We got into people's apartments and set fire to it, and just destroyed everything in our wake. We-- we didn't really want anything. We did look for food-- food-- mostly-- In one place we dislodged a bath. I remember that-- a bathtub. We got it into the street. And we filled it with jams and sugar and bread and provisions, and brought it all back to the camp. So all we were in search of was food. But really, the purpose was just to go on the rampage and vent our anger at whoever there was. Now we got in-- I got into, with a group of people, I got into one house. And we-- we couldn't-- we couldn't see anybody, but we could hear some voices. And they were all in the air raid shelter, in a-- in a cellar. And when we got down there, it was semi-darkness, really. And there was a group of people huddled in one corner. You couldn't really see who they were. But vaguely you could tell there were one or two children there. There were some women. There were some elderly men there. And when we got into there, we were on the point of killing. There was no doubt about that. All we wanted was revenge. And I was the only person that had a dagger. And I was urged on, come on, we must kill. We're here. We've got to kill them. And then, of course, when these people in the corner saw us, they-- they started screaming, and children started crying. And suddenly I realized there was the sound of these children crying, which I'd heard before, children crying on the point of death. And I thought, no, no way can I do the same thing as I've seen being done to others. I can't kill. I don't know if these children-- they're innocent. They may be innocent. These people might be innocent. I'm not going to kill them. And I was urged on by the mob to kill. And the next thing I remember is I took this dagger, and I threw it in a different direction. And it actually hit a door, and it sort of stuck in the door. And I-- I just threw myself down and started crying. And these-- these Germans all came up to me and started shaking my hand, you know, thanking me for what I've not done, I take it. And-- and all the people behind me started moving out. And I just ran out of there and went straight into another place. So we were on the point of killing, but we didn't do it. 
INT: At that point we'll stop.  INT: Today's date is June the 21st. This is a continuation of an interview started on June the 9th. The survivor is Kitty Hart-Moxon. The interviewer is Corrine Oppenheimer. And we are in Birmingham, England. And the language spoken is English. Tape 15, Kitty Hart. June the 21st, 1998. Kitty, you have been liberated after a-- a-- a long trail, and you are somewhere in North Germany. Where was that? KHM: Well, this was a little town called Salzwedel, not all that far from Bergen-Belsen.  INT: Right. Did you know what was happening with the war at this point? KHM: No, we had no idea what was happening with the war. We naturally knew the front was very close, because shells were penetrating the camp already a few days prior to liberation. We knew the front couldn't have been far. But we didn't know who it was. We didn't know who the Allies were. Were they the German-- the Americans, were they the French, were they the Russians, or were they the British? We had absolutely no idea. It was a total surprise when we saw an American tank come through the gates of the camp.  INT: And did you speak to anyone at that point? KHM: I did actually-- I already said before that I was totally mesmerized, looking at the SS stores, and I didn't even see the tanks pass. By that time, we hadn't eaten for about two weeks. And all I could see were loaves of bread across the SS stores. And I was just sitting there, watching these-- these loaves, waiting for something to happen. And it wasn't until someone actually tapped me on the shoulder and said, look, can you see what's passing by, that I turned around, and I-- I saw these tanks that were actually just passing the camp and didn't-- didn't even see us and didn't even take any notice. And then suddenly, one tank burst through the camp, and we realized that these were actually American forces that came to liberate us. 
INT: What happened then? KHM: Most of us made a beeline for the town itself, those that were still able to walk. Many people were too weak or were just lying around. My mother couldn't go. But she'd got outside the gates, and she was one of the first speaking to reporters and talking about Auschwitz, which at that time, of course, was liberated by the Russians. It was behind enemy lines, sort of, and there was absolutely no news filtering through. So she was one of the first people to give reports to the world press. But--  INT: Who were the world press? Were they military people, or-- KHM: Well, there were-- there were American reporters. Yes. That came to interview people that could actually talk. But most of us went onto this rampage into the town of Salzwedel. And I think I have already explained one or two incidents that happened in the little town of Salzwedel.  INT: Who were the people in Salzwedel? Were they Americans? KHM: No. Those were the German population.  INT: Yes. KHM: We rampaged through the city. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] And I think I explained on previous-- on the 9th of June, I gave you an account of what happened in one house, where I almost killed some of the people that were hiding in the cellar. So I will just carry on from there and give you more details of what happened in this town. We went from place to place, setting fire to some of the contents. And, of course, we didn't really want anything. We just simply went on a-- on a rampage of destruction. There-- there were people that hadn't been in the camp too long, and they were absolutely transformed. They took clothes and shoes, and immediately they-- they looked different. But people like myself and my friends, my Auschwitz friends, we simply just looked for food, nothing else. We didn't really want anything else. And that went on-- 
INT: What sort of food did you find? KHM: Well, we-- we were looking-- of course we were looking for bread, but we found sugar and jam and provisions. And we loosened up a bathtub in one place and filled it up and dragged it back to the camp. That went on for about three or four days. In one house, incidentally, in one house, I-- I-- there was a woman. And she-- she had something in the-- in the bed. We tried out beds, because, you know, we hadn't been lying in beds for years, so we wanted to see what it was like in the-- to lie in a proper bed. And there was something hard. And I put-- picked up a picture of the fuhrer, Hitler. And I said to this woman, what is this? You know. She said, oh my god, my fuhrer, my fuhrer. Please don't destroy this picture. And I remember, I just smashed this into thousands of pieces, and we ran out of this house. And as I said, this rampage went on for days. And there wasn't really anything left. The population was hiding, you know, in the cellars. And they must have taken most of the stuff with them into the cellars. And they were barricaded in the houses. But on about the third or fourth day-- the third day, I think. My mother said, look, you-- you've been there so many times. You're still barefoot. How about finding a pair of shoes? And I also need a pair of shoes or something to cover ourselves up with, a blanket. So on this third day, I found two blankets, which I brought back. And I was looking for some shoes. And we got into a shoe shop, and it was absolutely stuffed with boxes. And there weren't any shoes. I mean, there were just, literally, empty boxes. In-- in the end, I found two-- two men's shoes, two left shoes, which I brought back to the camp. My mother said, what am I going to do with this? Two left shoes? I need a right and a left. Anyway, we-- we-- we kept these-- these men's shoes. And then later on, in the DP camp, in the cobbler's workshop, which I helped to set up, they actually-- they transformed the left into a right, and I had a right and a left. And so we-- we actually looted nothing whatsoever, just two blankets. After that came a decree, of course, you know, that this rampage had to stop. And all people were confined to the camp. Our camp was then burned down. And we were there, watching the ceremony. And we were transferred to-- I think to the airport, the former aerodrome, where they billeted us.
And I was taken, and my mother, to interpret for the American forces. Now, I recall going into a-- a room with high-ranking American officers and some Russian officers. And on the table was a huge map. And I was asked to translate for the Russians and for the Americans. I could just understand and speak a little bit of Russian at that time. And they were actually carving up the territory around there and drawing lines on the map, which was going to be east, which was going to be west. How far the Americans were going to-- to-- to remain and how far the Russians were going to come forward. And they decided to hand the town over to the Russians. They drew a line. And Salzwedel was almost a border town of East Germany and West Germany. And that became, in fact, the-- the-- the-- the East Sector, the-- later on, the GDR, the Democratic Republic, the Communist part of Germany. And the Americans were going to withdraw or go forward to-- to-- to the south of the country. Because they were still fighting, don't forget. There was still a war on. Of course we became very alarmed at that, most of us. And we didn't want to stay with the Russians. So we begged the Americans to take us with them. And so we left practically on-- on the very last American tank, further to the west. And eventually, we-- we asked them to drop us at the-- at the British sector, which was-- by that time, established the British sector. And for the-- the British Army of the Rhine took over the town of Brunswick, and we asked them to drop us there. And so some of our Auschwitz-- original Auschwitz group, there were about eight of us, stayed in Brunswick. And we almost immediately were taken over by the British military government and worked for the-- for the occupying forces. It was the 822 British Army on the Rhine, military government. And I worked for a Major Wilson, who was in charge of this unit, and the same with my mother. And we did quite a lot of work there. And they billeted us in a house in Brunswick. Well, soon after that, we were requested to move to the aerodrome a-- a section of Brunswick called Broitzem because they were going to establish a huge, huge displaced persons camp there. Actually, it was meant to be a Polish camp, Assembly Center 296 or something. And it was nicknamed Gypsy, but-- Gypsy Camp, by the-- by the authorities. But it wasn't a gypsy camp, of course. It was really for Poles, for Polish forced laborers, who were all around that area, to be repatriated back to Poland. But of course they came under the heading of displaced persons. And we didn't know who these people were. There were not many concentration camp survivors there, because there was Belsen not all that far away. And of course, most of them were still in Belsen, the-- the survivors. 
INT: What date would this be now? KHM: I-- I think that was-- I would say we're going into May '45, possibly almost into June '45. The-- the-- the Quaker Relief Team, RST 100, were the very first Quaker-- were the very first relief team following the Army into Belsen. And they did a lot of relief work in Belsen. And then of course UNRRA moved in, other relief organizations moved in. And this unit, the Quaker Relief Team, were asked to set up this displaced persons camp. And they needed somebody like myself and my mother to interpret, and Henya, my friend, as well. She could speak a little bit of English by that time. And so about seven or eight of us moved into the DP camp, and we became the official interpreters to the Quaker Relief Team.  INT: Were you paid for this work? KHM: I think I was paid a nominal sort of sum. I can't remember in what currency, actually.  INT: Did you have accommodations? KHM: Yes, we had very good accommodation. And food was scarce, actually. It wasn't particularly good. But we were certainly not starving. Food was difficult in Brunswick while we were working for the British, because we were sort of living among the Germans, and we-- we had no access to food. So we were better off in the DP camp, actually. That was a very interesting time, actually, in the DP camp. Because we helped to set up. Not only, of course, did we work to try and persuade the Poles to go back home. Many of them didn't want to go. A, they were afraid to go back to-- under communism, and many had other reasons for not wanting to go back. And we were afraid that they were also-- under the guise of displaced persons were a lot of former SS people, Ukrainians, Lithuanians, ethnic sort of Germans. But of course, it was very difficult to say who was who. We set up a large registration office, and I was actually working. My boss, one-- one of the Quaker chaps who set up this, he was-- he-- I was his interpreter, and we set up the registration office. I actually worked in that registration office, because you-- you also had to interpret there. We had staff working there already, you know, from the DP camp. But-- but-- you-- you did need people who could actually communicate also liaison with the-- with the-- the Quaker relief team. And it-- it was a very important part of the displaced camp, because we registered people, and we reunited a lot of families. Because people from all over came to look for their relatives or whatever. And we had this proper registration system. So that was the very first thing that we set up. We then set up all kinds of other things, like a cobbler's workshop, a tailor's workshop, to keep these people occupied. And schools for the-- there were quite a lot of children there. We set up some schools and got the DP people themselves to do some teaching. And my mother was incredibly busy, because she immediately started to teach English. And she set up English classes in the DP camp. We were both sent on the welfare course, which was very nice, an-- an UNRRA welfare course, somewhere in the south. And so we had a little bit of training. I-- 
INT: Did you-- sorry. KHM: Sorry.  INT: Can you tell me the name of your-- your boss in this? KHM: Oh, yeah, my-- the name of my boss was [? Errol ?] [? Hall ?] Williams. He'd studied law and then joined the Quakers as a-- as a relief worker. My mother's boss was a-- a lady called Jane Levison. She was a Jewish Quaker, actually, a social worker who joined the Quakers because that was the only way she could quickly come to do some relief work. And she was eventually responsible for getting us to England. My mother was her interpreter, you know. So she-- my mother was going-- going with her, and I was going with [? Hall ?] Williams. One of the very-- in the very early days, I realized that what I must do to try and document what actually happened to me and my mother. And so very early on, I set about of writing a small-- a-- a small sort of account. I couldn't write English too well. I couldn't actually write German at all. I didn't want to particularly write it in Polish, so I-- I did it as best I could in-- in English. And this was really the very first-- the beginning of me documenting of what happened. But I realized I must do-- do it immediately, because so many things had happened. And while it was fresh in our minds, and the same with my mother, we-- we decided we have to set something down, pen to paper. We worked in the DP camp for-- well, from about probably oh, May, June '45 until September 1946. In the meantime, we tried very hard to locate members of our family. We've tried-- there was a very large tracing center in a place called Arolsen. And I-- I traveled all around Germany, with my friend Henya, to most of the DP camps, just trying to locate people. See where our friends are, who is alive, any relatives, if there's any news of my father and of my brother. And so we did an awful lot of searching around and tracing. And it soon became apparent that there is no one alive. 
INT: Who-- who took you around? How did you get around? KHM: Oh, it was amazing. We simply-- Henya and I, we used to board trains. And without any money, without any tickets, we simply showed our concentration camp number, and doors were opened for us. And we just went into the trains and traveled around.  INT: This is by the-- the nationals-- German nationals? KHM: Everywhere.  INT: Everyone. KHM: Everywhere. Everywhere we had a free hand. We also hitchhiked lifts everywhere, from the army, from the-- the French. Well, you were not supposed to go onto the French side and onto the German side, but we-- we traveled all over. We went onto the American side and visited most of the DP camps on-- on the-- in the American-- in the southern sector of Germany. We've been far north. We-- we traveled all around. And we simply checked into the nearest DP camp and searched for people and stayed the night and then carried on.  INT: In your-- in your base camp in Broitzem-- KHM: Yes?  INT: --were there many Jewish people? KHM: No, there were very few. Because the main Jewish camp, of course, was in Belsen, you see? So there were very few of us. There was just a-- a handful of the-- of-- of the girls, really, the original girls. And who were asked to come there to interpret. It was by and large a Polish repatriation camp. But of course-- and of course, we-- we actually took these people to the trains. Because there-- there-- there was a-- a terminal there somewhere. And the trains came right in, and we decorated the coaches with green and branches, you know, for them to-- to-- to welcome these Poles and try to get them to go home. And many of them did. We did manage to persuade. We used to go around from hut to hut and persuade people to go home. You know, telling them there's time they go home to their families. Most of them had their families there. There was no reason why they shouldn't go home. Oh, it was difficult for us. I mean, some had political reasons, I suppose. But others had economic reasons. Others had criminal reasons they didn't want to go back, you see? So there-- there were people with different reasons. But something I must really tell you what happened early on. When the trials of the SS women and the SS commandants in Belsen took place, which took place, I think-- I think it was Lubeck, but I couldn't be-- I couldn't swear to this. The-- north of-- not all that far from Brunswick and not all that far from Belsen. We-- we got to know that there was going to be the trial. And of course they hung the the-- commandant of Belsen, Kramer, and I think they hung one or two SS women as well. And some of them had been given heavy life sentences. But we didn't realize that there was also a commandant of Auschwitz, named Hossler, who was on trial. 
INT: Where was this? KHM: Well that was-- I think it was Lubeck where the trials were-- I-- I don't know the exact town. I think it was a place called Lubeck. I don't know what the other town was called. Anyway, northern Germany. They held the trials, the British. It was in the British sector, anyway. We-- my mother and I decided we really ought to go-- really get up there. And the-- the reason was that this man, Hossler, although he participated in many selections, at the ramp, no doubt. I didn't see it, but he must have done. He was quite a lenient sort of man. And he actually saved my life by transferring me at the crucial moment from the crematoria back to the camp and onto the transport with my mother. And I just felt we need to go and speak up for him. But I'm afraid when we got there, they didn't want to hear. They didn't want to know, and we were not even allowed to-- to proceed.  INT: Who were they, didn't want to know? KHM: I couldn't tell you. I don't know. At the-- at the place where the trial took place. It was the tribunal, the main tribunal. And he was hung. So that was the end of Hossler.  INT: And this was 1946? KHM: I think it could have still been in 1945. Yes.  INT: All right. Right. Can we talk a little bit about your mother and yourself? What was your health condition like immediately after the war? KHM: Well, my-- I-- I was not sick in any sense of the word, but I had lost an awful lot of weight. And by-- by the time we were examined and medically examined by the Americans, incidentally, still, I weighed about five stone. So I'd-- I lost most of my body weight, half my body weight. My mother was-- was in poor health by that time. She also lost the same as me, an awful lot of weight. And she-- it was apparent that her health had deteriorated, very much so. And she never, never really fully recovered. She'd had blood pressure problems and heart problems ever since the time of liberation. And it transpired that she'd also had TB, which she didn't even know. 
INT: How old was your mother at the end of the war? KHM: Oh, she was-- she was 55, yes. So you know, she was no longer young. Yes. And she was-- she certainly wasn't in good enough-- in good health anymore to pursue any career or occupation sort of after the war. She was all right interpreting and still teaching in the DP camp, but it was apparent she wasn't going to be able to do that for very long.  INT: Were you given any medical attention? KHM: Oh, in-- in the first instance, by-- by the Americans, we were given a lot of medical attention. Because there-- we all had these horrible boils all over. And I-- I had a-- a huge, huge boil somewhere here, on my temple. And that had to be lanced. And so yes, the-- the American military, the military gave us quite a lot of medical attention. We were X-rayed, and yes. And-- and of course, they tried to feed us too much. I remember there-- there was a time when they stood over us and said, you eat five eggs. You know. There was no way could-- could we do that. So a-- a-- a lot of it, of course, they didn't quite know how to handle. Yes. But a lot of people were taken to hospital in Salzwedel, and a lot of-- a lot of the women died after the war in the hospital in Salzwedel. In fact, somewhere I have a list of the ones that died after the war. There's still a list available. It was handed to me when I went to Salzwedel, to a reunion in 1996. 
INT: Did-- did you ever spend any time in hospital at that-- at that time? KHM: No. No.  INT: Did your mother? KHM: No, no. We-- we didn't. No.  INT: Were there any children around at this point? KHM: Well, there were a lot of Polish children around in the DP camp. I-- I don't know who they were. There must have been women that gave birth during that time, as they were forced laborers. But Poles were all forced laborers, you see, that had been taken to Germany. So there were quite a lot of-- not very small children. There were sort of-- yes. I don't remember very tiny children. There-- there-- of course there were post-war children, don't forget, as well. Yes, they were giving birth.  INT: What about-- what about Jewish children? KHM: There were no Jewish children in-- in the-- in this-- in this DP camp in Brunswick, none at all. Oh, perhaps-- perhaps one, I think. I recall one. In-- in fact, I don't think he was a Jewish child. He came over to England after. So I think he was a Polish child. He came over to England. I met him in England, some years afterwards.  INT: Did you see any Jewish children at all in the DP camps on your travels? KHM: Very few. Very, very few. One or two, mostly young girls that-- young girls and young boys in the American sector. No, very few. Mind you, we didn't look out for the children. We were looking-- we were looking for adult friends and relatives. But of course, soon it came apparent, you see, that-- that no one of my family was alive. We-- we made contact with the-- with the town, with Bielsko. We wrote to the community. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] We did have some information pretty soon that none of my family had actually come back to the town. Because if-- if anybody had been alive, that's the first place they would have gone, to the Jewish community, to register that they are alive, and they hadn't. 
INT: Did you try to go back to your hometown? KHM: No, my mother would never let me go back. That's one thing she didn't want me to do. She didn't-- no--  INT: Did she ever tell you why? KHM: Well, she said there was nothing to go back to. I mean, she just couldn't bear the-- the memories. She just didn't want to go. And she didn't want me to go. And it wasn't until she died in 1974 that soon after that, I actually made the first trip back.  INT: Did you ever-- were you ever aware of survivors with any mental illness at that stage? KHM: Yes, I think so. There were-- there was a lot of mental problems. I think so, yes.  INT: Did you actually know anyone who committed suicide at that stage? KHM: No. Everybody was too glad to be alive. Although of course they knew-- they didn't know what the future was going to hold. But no, I-- I don't recall anyone actually committing suicide.  INT: What about female survivors? Were there more or less than male survivors? KHM: I think so. I think I've-- I've always felt that more women survived than men. Because women somehow managed to look after one another. And as I've mentioned before, it was almost impossible to survive on your own. And men were not very good at forming sort of families and looking after one another. And women took humiliation better than men as well. Although they were not treated any better than the men. I think more women survived. I don't know what the statistics say, but it seemed to be so when we went around the camps. We saw more girls and more women than men. 
INT: Can you recall any problems, health problems, that they had? KHM: Well, they-- most people had some health problems. I mean, my-- my main health problem is that I lost most of my teeth, because a lot of my teeth had to be extracted, because I had holes made, you know, so that I could go and see the dentist. I had these diamonds in my teeth. And so a lot of people actually lost their teeth because of the diet as well. So that-- that was one of the main health problems, I think, they've had.  INT: Right. KHM: I must just tell you that we then had information about my father. The next thing that came through was that my father was discovered where he was hiding. And he was lodging with a-- a farmer. And one day, they came for my father outside this town of Tarnow. And he was shot, and his body was thrown down a ravine. I went to have a look at this ravine last year.  INT: We'll take a break there, Kitty.  INT: Tape 16, Kitty Hart, 21st of June, 1998. You were telling me about finding out about your father. Was there anything else you found out about your father? KHM: No. We never really found out how he was found out. Whether he was denounced, or whether they found any of his documents that were not in order. I'm afraid we-- we could never get to the bottom of it. All we knew is that they shot him. 
INT: What about your brother? KHM: Well, I think I did explain that my brother left Lublin in 1939, with most of the young people, to-- to go east, hoping to get into Russia, or, via Russia, into Romania, like everybody else, and eventually either go to Palestine or go through China or somewhere and join the Allies. But of course, he got as far as a place called Lwow. And he stayed there for-- for some time. And that, in 1941, was then occupied by the Germans. When the Germans declared war and invaded the Soviet Union, they occupied Lwow. And my brother, I think he ran further into the Soviet Union. And we have got some documentation, and we've got some cards, postcards that came, via the Red Cross, to England, to my relatives in England. So he has written in 1940, 1941, and as-- as-- I think there is one card dated 1942, all the way from Samarkand, somewhere in the midst of the Soviet Union. Well, after that, we believe he was drafted into a Polish unit. Now, how we knew about that is a friend of my own friend-- a-- a brother of my friend, who lives in Sweden, was the commanding officer of this Polish unit. And my brother served under him. Now, this chap survived the war. And after the war, he told us what had happened, that my brother was drafted in, under his command, into a Polish unit. And he was fighting, a Polish unit under the Russian-- in the Russian Army, until 1943. And he died under the battle of Stalingrad. Because there was a siege of Stalingrad, and they were dug in there for I don't know how long. And one night, apparently, my brother went out to get something, I don't know what. And he was hit by a sniper. And he was killed. So he actually died on the Stalingrad, during the siege. But we don't know the date. 
INT: How did-- how did you find out this information? KHM: Well, we got from my friend's brother. My friend lives in Sweden. Her brother came back after the war, and he became quite a well-known commentator, sports commentator, in Warsaw. And he's-- he remained in Poland until 1956, I think, where the Poles purged all the Jews from their posts. And he then went to live in Germany, where he-- he now still lives in Germany. He's now in his 80s. He now lives in Germany. And he still worked for German television. And he gave the account of what-- what happened to my brother.  INT: And do you know his name? KHM: Yes. Yes. Konrad Gruda. Yes.  INT: How do you spell the last name? KHM: G-R-U-D-A.  INT: All right. And your friend's name? KHM: Oh, her name-- well, her name is [NON-ENGLISH]. She-- she was also in Russia during the war. And she married also a refugee. Her name-- and she lives in Stockholm. She was a dentist in Stockholm. That was her brother.  INT: Yes. And how did you make contact with her again after the war? KHM: I made contact with her after the war in-- in Bielsko. She came back to Bielsko, and she eventually studied dentistry in-- in Wroclaw, that's formerly Breslau. And her mother-- her mother was also a teacher of English, with my mother, together. They were very close. They were distant, sort of related, almost. And her mother became a professor of English. But they all had to emigrate, in 1956, when there was a purge of Jews in Poland. And they all left. They went to live in Sweden. 
INT: What were your hopes and special wishes for-- for you-- you yourself, after you were liberated? KHM: Well, because my mother decided we were not going back, and we-- we soon realized there are no-- there is no one alive from-- from Bielsko. If there is anyone alive, it is only people who had escaped pre-war or had gone to Russia, like those cousins of ours that were, at that time, at a-- on a frontier town. And they eventually got to America. And of course, my mother had a sister in Vienna, and they emigrated to Birmingham in 1938. So we realized that both my mother and I, we were totally on our own. And the-- the closest relatives were actually my mother's sister. And my mother, having studied in England, knew England well, and, of course, had a sister here, naturally thought that possibly this-- eventually this is where we will have to go.  INT: What was your aunt's name? KHM: My aunt's name was Dr. Muller, M-U-L-L-E-R. And she was at that time-- I'm sure-- she was a dentist, but she was also a doctor. She'd studied medicine and dentistry. Well, when she came over, she-- she actually worked as a doctor at the Birmingham Accident Hospital. So she-- she was actually working as-- as a surgeon at the time. Anyway, after much deliberations, we didn't quite know where we were going to be permitted to go. Because it was-- you know, we-- we were not-- it's blood relatives that I think had priority, if you had a mother or a father. If you were close like this. But of course I was a niece, so I wasn't considered close enough, and I was no longer a minor. I was 18. So naturally, we had to wait for some immigration or immigration policy. However, my mother, having worked, and myself as well, for the British military government, the American military government, for the Quaker Relief, from the minute of liberation, we had a very strong case for emigration. And Jane, who was, as I said, a Jewish Quaker, she had friends, MPs, who took up our case. And both my mother and I got special permits to actually come over to England in September 1946. But we also almost simultaneously had affidavits to go to the States. However, my mother decided we ought to go to England, because this is where she-- her closer relatives were. And this is how, one day, in September 1946, the Joint, American Joint, paid our fare, by train and boat, to come over to England. 
INT: Before we-- you go on with that, did you ever try, or did you ever wish to go to Palestine? KHM: Not at that stage. No.  INT: Was anyone else with you and your mother? KHM: Yes, quite a lot of girls went. Yes. They-- they-- they were on the boat that was then shipped to Cyprus. Yes.  INT: Mhm. So continue. What happened in September 1946? KHM: In September 1946, I left my friends behind in the DP camp, Henya and her sister. They-- they were-- we were very close by that time. And they didn't know where they were going to go. They hadn't-- they couldn't find their own relatives. They didn't want to go back home. And they eventually moved to the American sector, because the DP camp was gradually being wound up, and everybody moved pretty well south, to the southern part of Germany. Most of my friends went to a place called Feldafing, which was in the American-- DP camp in the American sector, southern Germany, outside Stuttgart, or some-- somewhere like this. But my mother and I were met in Dover, by my uncle, who-- the reason my uncle came, actually, to England, was that, pre-war, he had some business connections with a firm of Elkington's, which were jewelers, jewelers or some-- something. And he got permission to come to-- to Birmingham in 1938. And this is how he came to Birmingham. And he had already set up a small jewelry business in Birmingham. And my aunt was already working as a doctor. And they had two-- two daughters, two cousins of mine, which were not all that-- one was younger than me, and one was a little older than me. Anyway, my uncle met us in Dover. And he drove us-- and he-- he had his own car by that time. He drove us to London. And we stayed in London, which was incredibly confusing for me. Because the first time I-- I saw a city in years. Because Brunswick was totally destroyed, and all of Germany was just in ruins. And there it was, this hustle and bustle of shops and traffic lights and things that I'd never seen or couldn't remember. And my uncle made a remark, which altered the course of my life, I would say. He said the following. Just remember, in my house, I don't want you to say a word of what happened to you. I don't want to know. And I don't want my girls upset. This is what he-- these were his words. And from that moment on, I realized, one thing I must do, keep talking, even if it-- nobody wants to listen. You're just going to talk for the next 50 years. And I knew that's what I had to do. That was my duty. And it's really-- I knew I'd have to make enormous sacrifices. At that time, I-- I-- I didn't know how it-- I was going to do it. But I knew that somehow, in my life, I'd have to make a-- sacrifice my career or whatever. That I'd have to set time apart for educating people and telling them exactly what had happened. 
INT: What immigration status were you given when you arrived in this country? KHM: We were given-- we were given temporary permits, 12 months alien permits. And I was not allowed to take on any employment other than in a hospital. And my mother was not allowed to teach, naturally. Only-- the only employment she was allowed was as a domestic, domestic help. That was-- that was the proviso for the 12 monthly permit.  INT: Where did you actually live? KHM: Well, when we arrived, we-- we went to my uncle and my aunt's flat, which was a very nice, big flat, not all that far from here, in Edgbaston.  INT: Which city? KHM: Here, in Birmingham. Yes, it all happened in Birmingham. A mile away from where we are now, I would say.  INT: How did the rest of the family react to you? KHM: Well, I'm-- I'm afraid my cousins didn't relate to me, whatsoever. They practically ignored me. My older cousin, she was-- she was already going to university. My younger cousin was still in school. So they had their own friends, and I was sort of left on my own, and I was an outsider. And for-- for two or three weeks, I was just doing nothing at all. Just wandering around the town. Well, eventually, of course, I got to know there was this International-- Birmingham International Center, and that became just my-- my second home. And I spent most of my time there meeting people. 
INT: Did your mother go with you? KHM: No. My mother, well, immediately got a-- a-- a job in the same building where my uncle and aunt lived, in the same block of flats. It was a-- a widower, who had lost his wife, a Mr. [? Getzler. ?] He came from Vienna. He was an extremely nice man. And he-- and my mother became his housekeeper. And it-- it was a godsend for her, because, you know, she had this little house to keep. And he was such a nice chap, a very, very nice man. And in fact, he-- he-- his family were in Australia. And he was going to emigrate. And he wanted my mother to come. And he wanted to marry her. But my mother didn't want to go, because I-- by that time, I was already in training. And she didn't feel it was right to-- for me to be upset and relocate. And she wouldn't leave me. So she decided against it. And he eventually emigrated. And she had to take another job.  INT: Tell me about the International Club. Who did you meet there? KHM: Oh, I met so many people in the International Center. The only people I didn't meet were people who actually came out of the camps. I found that there wasn't anyone in Birmingham that I could relate to. In fact, at that time, all-- all I knew, people from the camps, was just one other person, and that was [? Magda, ?] [? Magda ?] [? Blum. ?] But of course she was adopted by a-- a family here in Birmingham and immediately had sort of her own family. And she didn't even come to the International Center. I just met her once or twice. So I had no one. I was pretty well an outsider. I didn't fit. I-- I had a feeling that I didn't actually fit in anywhere. There were no survivors, from the camps, in Birmingham except me, not at that time. Of course, we're talking about 1946, maybe they came later. None of it-- nobody of my age. There were a lot of refugees that came over in 1938, 1939. Most of them came from Germany and Austria and Czechoslovakia. And there was-- there was no one from Poland, so I-- I didn't-- 
INT: Did they ask about your experiences? KHM: No, no. I-- everywhere I went, I was met with a-- a total indifference. No one-- I would say, no one ever asked me one single question of what happened to me. Not one person. I couldn't-- I couldn't-- I couldn't possibly find one person that would have asked me one question in those days.  INT: Did you see a lot of your mother? Did you-- KHM: Yes, I saw a lot of my mother. Well, in 1940-- when I first came over, yes. Because she was in the same block. So we used to talk quite a lot about the past. An awful lot, because we both felt, you know, we must not forget. It was so easy to, you know, forget and get on with your life. But we knew, especially I knew, that I'd seen certain things that perhaps there's nobody alive, I don't know, that have seen what I've seen. I must document it. I must think about it. And soon, I must talk about it.  INT: Did any organization or any one person or group of people ever want to contact you about your experiences? KHM: Well, in those days-- we're talking about 1946?  INT: Mhm. KHM: No. All-- all I know is it was a very, very difficult time for me. Because, in 1946, I-- as I say, I was told, I was told, that I have to do something for my living. And this is not what I wanted to do. I wanted to go to school. I made this plain to my uncle, that what I wanted to do is I wanted to go to school. Because I'd lost six years or seven years of my schooling, of my most important years of schooling. I needed to go back to school. That was the first thing. But my uncle said, where are you going to live? Who is going to keep you? You know, I can't keep you. I've got two daughters. They're-- they're at the university and at school. I can't afford to pay for your education. So I went all around to different places. I went to the Jewish Community, first of all. And of course they said, well, you-- your relatives guaranteed for you. It's their responsibility. We can't help you. And so-- and-- and I went to places like the education committees and whatever, just to see if I could get a loan. Well, everywhere, it was the same thing. I wasn't given a loan. I couldn't have a loan. There were-- there was no such provisions for people like me. Social security, income support, or any such thing, there was no such support, whatsoever.
And so I pretty soon realized that there wasn't going to be any help, and nobody wanted, really, to-- to know or to help me. Once, I think, I was asked, would I come and speak about the DP camp, because they were sending old clothes to-- you know, sending clothes to the DP camps for-- for people. And I remember getting up there and saying, it isn't old clothes that these people want in the DP camps. They've got plenty of old clothes. They want other things. They want to be taken away and have a new life. And they want money. They don't want old clothes. That-- that was the only occasion. And that was, perhaps, in the early days of 1946, when I came over. But the next thing that happened to me was that I was told, you are going to do nursing. And of course, this is not what I wanted to do. But at, at that stage, of course, I had no choice. I said, well, all right. I'd give it a try. So I was taken to the Birmingham Accident Hospital, where my aunt worked. And I was taken to the pre-nursing training. It's called the PTS, I think, Pre-nursing School-- Training School. And I was-- I was a resident in the-- in the nurses' home and started my nursing course. But it was mostly academic, the first three months, anyway. And then you had to pass an exam before they let you onto the wards. And I passed this exam. And I started on the wards. And then I realized that no way was this what I really wanted to do. So I said to my aunt, and I said to everybody in the hospital, I'm leaving. I'm not staying. I don't want to do nursing. And everybody said, all right, all right. Fine, fine. Well, one day, I packed my suitcase, and I left. But, of course, I didn't know that you have to give a month's notice, and you have to formally leave, and neither did I know where I was going to go, but I simply walked out. And I went missing. Well, of course, the next thing was that the police were looking for me. Because I was an alien, and I wasn't allowed to be anywhere, you know? I had to report to the police and places like that, a place of residence. But in fact, what happened is that Jane, the Quaker person, relief worker, she was back in England, doing social work in London, in Bethnal Green. And she was in contact with me, and I was in contact with her. And she said, come to me.
And this is what I did. I packed my suitcase, and that's where I was. And I stayed with Jane for about seven or eight weeks. She said, you come here, and you tell me what you want to do, and you sort yourself out. You don't-- you're not going to do something you don't want to do. And you're not going to be made to do something. And I stayed with her. And I had a marvelous time in London and talked about nothing but just, you know, tried to live for a few weeks. Anyway, I then returned back to Birmingham, now going into probably 1947. And I decided, well, what I really wanted to do was some physical training. I always said I wanted to go to physical training college. But that obviously wasn't-- was no longer possible for me to do. I thought I'd like to do physiotherapy, which was the nearest thing to physical training. However, it was a very, very costly course, and I couldn't raise the funds. Again, I couldn't raise a loan. I couldn't raise the funds from the Jewish community, from the-- from the Jewish Council. I went to Singers Hill, to the-- to the offices, to see the president and people like that. And I just-- I just couldn't raise any funds to-- to help me do that. In the end, I met-- well, I saw one of the doctors at the Accident Hospital, who was the radiologist in that hospital. And the thing that took my attention, I think, was the X-ray department. And I thought, well, that's perhaps something that I would be interested in to do. I had no idea what it involved or what you've got to have and how you get set about it. I knew that this man, Dr. [? Brailsford, ?] was in charge of-- of this department. And one day, I turned up on his doorstep. Simple as that. I went to see him, and he was sort of taken aback. He had never seen me in his life. And I said, I've just come to see if you can help me. And I explained to him that I've been-- And he was the first person that asked me some questions about what happened to me during the war. Now by that time it was 1947. And I told him a few things. And I said to him, I am on the point of going back to Germany, back to the DP camp, because that's where my friends are, and I shall start again. I don't know where I'm going to land up. He says, what? You're going-- you're not going back to Germany. No, no. I can't see you do that. Please, don't do that. I'll see if I can help you. And he went to the faculty of radiologists, which was in London, and put my case to them to see if there was some way that I could do a training, a radiography training. Well, of course, you had to have your matriculations and such, all these examinations. And I had lost five years-- seven years, of schooling. However, I did pass my PTS, and, apparently, I passed it very well. And you know, that was my strange language. And-- and I passed it without any problems, whatsoever. And so they decided-- he did. He actually-- he was-- he was-- this was in the days pre NHS. And he was sort of on the-- one of the governors of the Woodlands Hospital, the Royal Orthopedic Hospital in Birmingham. So he-- and, and that was run, at that time, by the Cadbury family, who were also Quakers, if you recall. And they all decided to take me on, at Woodlands, on probation, give me a chance to-- to see if I could train in radiography. And this is how I started on my career. In March 1947, I became a resident, a student radiographer, at the Royal Orthopedic Hospital in Birmingham. And I had to go into the wards, of course, and do some nursing. Mostly, they only took girls that had already done two years orthopedic nursing. But because I had done some pre-nursing training, and because I was prepared to go onto the wards first thing in the morning and do some nursing, I was taken on. And I started on my career. 
INT: Wonderful. So what happened to your mother at this time? KHM: Well, my mother then worked for the next person. Again, she could only do domestic work. And she wasn't in terribly good health, so-- as I've explained. So she-- she worked as a housekeeper for another gentleman, and he was the chairman of Tube Investments, a-- a very nice people. And she was there-- well, she stayed there until, eventually, I could take her to come and live with me.  INT: So how long was your training? KHM: Well, my training wasn't too long, actually. Because nowadays, it's four years. In those days, you could do it in two years. And I-- I managed to do the diagnostic and the radiotherapy all in the two years. And I had a horrendous time, because I was ostracized. I was the sort of odd person out. You know, I didn't fit in. I didn't fit into the-- the environment. I didn't fit into the rigid nursing regime that there was. You had to be in, locked in at 10 o'clock at night. And I didn't like that too much. And I did the most horrendous things, like climbing up drain pipes into other people's bedrooms, and come in at night. And you know, I just didn't like to obey these rules. To me, it was like being in another prison and not-- losing my freedom. However, I eventually was the only person to qualify, [LAUGHS] out of-- out of all of us. I think there were about seven of us. I-- I was the only one that eventually got the-- the qualification. And the reason for that was that the girls used to get together, and they-- they-- they actually played around so much and didn't work. But I used to go to the International Center, where I used to meet my friends. And then I used to lock myself in, in the reference library, and work there, because I couldn't work anywhere else. And I also had friends that helped me with math and physics, which I, you know, didn't know. So I had to have a little bit of tuition. And there were quite a lot of chaps that helped me along. And-- and this is where I was. I was actually working incredibly hard. And as I say, I was the only person to eventually qualify. 
INT: Apart from your mother, was there any other person who gave you support and emotional help? KHM: No. You-- you were left totally to your own devices, as far as your emotional health was concerned. No. I-- I very quickly realized that I have got to come to terms with what had happened to me. And it's no good shutting it out. And I think this, in a way, helped me. I was determined to talk about it. Although I never talked about it at-- at work. But wherever there was the opportunity, I'd take the opportunity to start talking about it, and people didn't want to hear, but I made them-- I made them listen.  INT: Can you recall how you initiated the subject? KHM: Well, it wasn't very difficult, because I had a number on my arm. And you could-- and people would remark or something, and you came from there. So it wasn't very difficult. I wouldn't sort of go up to people and say, you know, so-and-so and so-and-so. But yes, it wasn't very difficult. We had the arm number. My-- my mother, when she was in company, some, she used to cover her arm up, because she didn't like people asking her questions and looking at her. But I-- I didn't do that. I preferred to have my-- my number visible. There was a time when I actually had to have it removed, because it-- because--  INT: Right. KHM: Yes. Because it became-- it didn't become an embarrassment. I don't say that. People, of course, in the hospital, were looking at me. When you wear-- you wore short sleeves, and it was visible. And you're constantly being-- you know, remarks being made behind your back. But there were some irritating instances which I was no longer prepared to put up with. And that was one of the reasons when I eventually had it removed. I was working in the Children's Hospital at that time. And there were one or two people, and one was a doctor, who, whenever he saw me, would make some peculiar remark, such as, is this your boyfriend's telephone number? CREW: OK. 
INT: Tape 17, Kitty Hart, 21st of June, 1998. You were talking about your life in 1946. What was your social life like? KHM: Well, social life was very, very good in the-- the International Center, where I found all the friends that I needed. In-- in fact, I didn't go anywhere else but the International Center. We had dances there, and we had discussions, and outings, and just the-- the whole of my social life revolved around the International Center. And that-- when that closed down, of course, by that time I was already married. I met my future husband at the International Center, who came over with a kindertransport, and-- the very last boat on the in 1939. So then I got married--  INT: Did he know about your experiences? KHM: Well he knew about it, but he didn't know any details. No, he didn't know any details. He didn't-- he didn't ask me any questions. Exactly the same as anybody else. He-- he didn't ask me a single question. In fact, my own husband never asked me one single question of what happened to me. If he hadn't read my book or seen my video, he wouldn't know what happened. So that's just how it's been with everybody.  INT: Did you have children? KHM: Yes. I got married in 1949, and by that-- we only had a very tiny little accommodation. And so I realized I couldn't have any children until it's possible to have a house. Again, my friend Jane lent me deposit for the house, which was an awful lot of money in those days. And we purchased a first house 1953-- May '53, and in June '53 my son David was born. My mother-- immediately we had a house, my mother came to live with us and my mother helped look after the children. Peter, my younger son, was born 1955, and I started work-- well, I never stopped work. I'd worked right through my pregnancy, and I worked immediately after-- after I gave birth. But I did realize that I will never take a full-time appointment because I had to set some time aside for my Holocaust work. So in-- in effect, I knew I had to sacrifice part of my career, but I worked weekends, evenings, nights, sessional work, and my mother took charge of-- of-- of the household. 
INT: Did your-- what year did you have your number removed? KHM: Well, I had my number removed while I was still working at the children's. So just before my elder son was born. As as I explained before, there were circumstances where I just could not have this number any longer by-- mostly by the ignorance of most people who should know-- should have known what this number was, but always made these ridiculous remarks.
