And it-- it just irritated me so much that one day I decided that I've had enough, and I-- I was going to have this removed, and I was going to have it mounted in a specimen so that it lives forever. Even when I'm not here my number will live on in some museum. And this is when I had my number removed.  INT: As your children were growing up, did you ever talk to them about your experience? KHM: My mother and I never stopped talking about our experiences. It came into our everyday life. It came in-- in the discussion during meal time, it came in play time. My children were brought up in it from a very young age. Of course, we'd never talked about the most horrible atrocities. Very often we talked about the funny things that happened. And children used to tease, oh, here we go. Oh, little bits of food have got to be put into the fridge. Nothing must be thrown out. Because remember, during the war you didn't have any bread. Now you mustn't throw it out. We must go and collect it from everybody's dustbins if they throw it out. So they were brought up in this constantly. They didn't know the depths of it, and the ins and outs of it, but they were in that environment-- brought up in an environment of knowing what had actually happened to us. 
INT: Do you think your experience affected the way you brought your boys up? KHM: No, I don't think so. I think they-- they took it in their stride pretty well. They didn't-- they didn't show any particular stress, or any inclination to any revenge, or anything like that. Not at-- not at all. As a matter of fact, what we did-- what we used to do when my children were small, most of our holidays were camping holidays because money was-- we had a very, very difficult time and money was incredibly tight for many years. And this is why I had to go out to work so much to help support the household. That we-- we had camping holidays all around Europe. And we usually set up tent-- a tent next to a German family so that we mix with a German family. And just to see how my children were going to get on with some of these German children, and the Austrian children. And mostly they got on very well.  INT: What about the adults? How did you-- KHM: With the adults, it was-- I would mix with people that were younger than me. I would never mix with people that were my age or older because I never knew who they were, what they did during the war. And if ever I made a remark, well, there was just a deathly silence and that was the end of the conversation. And it normally started, how come you speak such good German? And I would make a remark like-- although because my German was-- my mother tongue was German, sometimes I would say, my mother was-- was born in Austria. But if I really wanted to see a reaction I would say, oh, I had a marvelous university that I went to where I learned German. And the university was called Auschwitz. And that, of course, provoked a-- a stunning silence. Stunned silence, shall I say. And then you could sort people out of whether you could actually talk about it. 
INT: And did you ever meet people you could talk to about it? KHM: Yes. Oh, yes. Yes, I met people I could talk to. I-- I was friendly with a-- a-- a whole group of Germans who were just slightly younger than me, as a matter of fact. So they were sort of in the Hitler Youth, I think, during the war. And we used to meet up with these people quite often on a holiday in Italy. And-- and we-- we used to talk about it a lot. They didn't, of course, know-- knew what went on in the camps, and they were all younger people than-- than I was. So yes, we-- we-- we did-- we did actually make friends with some of-- some of the Germans. We are talking about now in the '60s and in the '70s. And by that time I-- I'd already published my book, and it had been printed in Germany, and these people have actually read it in German. So-- and I remember sending them signed copies. So we've-- I've had quite a lot of contact. On various occasions we've had contact with the Germans and Austrians, yes.  INT: Have you ever been involved in any war crimes trials? KHM: Well this happened-- yes, I have. Of course, I-- I've-- when my first book was published I said to Lord Russell of Liverpool, who was one of the chief prosecutors at-- at the Nuremberg trial, if ever you get Mengele, remember, I will come to give evidence. And he said, yes, you should-- you should be one of the first to come give evidence. But of course-- 
INT: When did you write your book? KHM: Well, I wrote my first book in 1961. That was when I saw Lord Russell of Liverpool.  INT: Yes. KHM: But of course, this never happened with Mengele, as we know.  INT: Were you contacted at all for any trial? KHM: Yes. The-- the-- the--  INT: When was that? KHM: Well the next thing that happened is, of course, I-- I did-- I went back to Poland. I went back to my hometown. I went back to Auschwitz. I was one of the very first.  INT: When-- when did you get back to-- KHM: That's right.  INT: --Auschwitz? KHM: I went back to Auschwitz 1978, and-- and I-- I was one of the first because that was still under communism. And I made-- I made the video, Following this, of course, there are lots of things happened.  INT: Who initiated-- who initiated the video? KHM: Oh, the video was initiated by Yorkshire Television in Leeds.  INT: Who-- who was the contact? KHM: Well, there was a-- a film director called Peter Morley who'd done many documentaries. And he wanted to do-- he did the queen's jubilee, he did-- he did very, very famous documentaries.  INT: How did he-- how did he know of you? KHM: Well, he knew of me because of my book. My book was published already in the '60s and had been reprinted in the '70s. It had a reprint about 1974 and 1976, and that-- that was in 1978. And they were looking for somebody-- they were not looking for someone to come-- go back to Auschwitz. They were doing women at war, and they wanted to do a half-hour episode on me. And when they came to me I said, I'm terribly sorry, my story does not fit into the half-hour documentary. If you really want me to give you a documentary, I want to go back to the camp. And he said, you go back to the camp? No, no, no, that's-- nobody goes back to the camp. I said, oh yes, I want to go back. I want to show people what it was like and talk about it there because that's the only way people will understand if they physically come into the camp. Of course, in those days you couldn't just go because as I said it was communist country. 
INT: What organization did Peter Morley represent? KHM: Yorkshire Television.  INT: Right. KHM: And-- and he said, well, I can't let you go on your own. I said, well, that's all right. My son was, at that time, working in Canada. I said, I'll give him a ring and ask him. So I put a call through, and it happened to be 3 o'clock in the morning in Canada where he was. And I said, David, do you want to go to Auschwitz? He says, yes, can we go tomorrow? And that's it.  INT: How old was he at this stage? KHM: How old was he? 50-- was born 1953. '53-- 25.  INT: And what was his occupation? KHM: He was a doctor. He was already working as a doctor in Canada.  INT: So what happened then? KHM: Well, we flew out and we-- we, as I said, did the whole We did some taping in the house, pre-Auschwitz, post-Auschwitz, the reflections on Auschwitz. And the things that happened, it was just like a catalyst because in this country very few people knew anything about Auschwitz. In the States you have so many survivors, and so many groups of-- of people that know about it, but in this country it was unknown. And this caused the most enormous storm, I would say, in this country. And I received mail bags upon mail bags of post from-- not just from this country, from all over the world, because it-- because it went right through the world. 
INT: Saying what? KHM: People didn't know. Everybody appalled that they hadn't been taught in school of what happened in Auschwitz or what happened to people in the camps. There'd been two generations grown up by that time with hardly any knowledge of what had happened.  INT: Were you ever asked to testify at all after that? KHM: Well, this is really what happened. Because of my videotape, I was asked to testify at-- at the war crimes trial in 1985, '86. I was contacted by the German embassy in London and asked whether I knew a man called Gottfried Weise, but I'd never heard of the name, which I explained. But nonetheless, I said I'd come down and give an interview, which I did. I went down to the embassy and I was interviewed by a lawyer who actually interrogated me on my time in the Kanada Kommando in Auschwitz. And you know, the next thing he said, I've got a-- a picture album I'd like you to look through, see if you recognize anyone. And I flicked through this album and I saw one person sometimes sitting with a girlfriend, sometimes in uniform, sometimes without uniform, sometimes with a peaked cap in different poses at different times. But I picked out that same person on perhaps six or seven different pages, and this lawyer closed the book and said, Mrs. Hart, you've just identified Gottfried Weise.  INT: Did-- did he have a name? KHM: I knew him by a different name. Well, I gave him the name of Wunsch because there was a man like that called Wunsch. But there was also-- I also gave him the name of der blinder, the slepy in Polish. He had a glass eye. And I-- I recognized this man instantly. I knew his face belonged to the Kanada Kommando a-- a-- a section of the camp. 
INT: Did the solicitor have a name? KHM: The lawyer? No, he was-- he was just a lawyer-- an embassy lawyer. I don't know, I'm afraid I don't know. The-- and the next thing that happened, I had a subpoena from the high court in Wuppertal in Germany to come and attend a trial. When I got there, I was just met by one person who looked after all the witnesses. Ruth Cunning, her name was.  INT: I'm sorry? KHM: Her name was Ruth Cunning. She-- she died-- she died since. She looked after the witnesses who came from all over the world, but we never saw any of each other. There was only one person and one person there for so many days-- I can't remember how many days-- and we never met them, we didn't know who the other witnesses were. So I was in court just on my own and I was confronted with this Gottfried Weise. I was asked to identify him, which I had not the slightest difficulty because the man hadn't changed. He looked a lot older, but I would have recognized him anywhere. He wore glasses.  INT: Did you to go alone? KHM: Yes, I went on my own.  INT: Were you offered any protection at all? KHM: I was-- I was looked after. I was very, very well looked after, yes, by different people, yes. There were some teachers--  INT: Were all your expenses paid? KHM: Yes, all my expenses were paid, yes. 
INT: Were you ever threatened? KHM: No.  INT: Did you ever feel threatened? KHM: No, no. I was never threatened. In fact, they brought the schools in-- the local schools came to listen to-- to the proceedings. And-- and-- and of course, they also had television-- they were televising it, as well. I don't think they allowed to televise the actual proceedings. I had television interviews there. And what they did have, which was very important, which they didn't have before, was my videotape. They showed the video me returning to Auschwitz. They showed the film there and they showed the terrain where it all took place. And I had to point out all the different landmarks of where things took place. And from that point of view, this was-- the videotape was absolutely crucial in-- in-- in-- in the court proceedings.  INT: Yes. Did you-- were you interviewed before the trial-- KHM: No.  INT: --or after the trial? KHM: Oh, you mean by the television people?  INT: Yes. KHM: Oh, during-- during break of proceedings, you-- you were-- you could be interviewed, yes. You could be interviewed by-- by the press, yeah.  INT: So the trial was going on and you were interviewed? KHM: Yeah, you were interviewed, but not-- not in the courtroom.  INT: No. KHM: Yes, yes. Outside. Yes, we were interviewed by different-- different-- it's all written up in the German press. Such-and-such a witness has arrived from so-and-so, giving evidence against Gottfried Weise, who was actually [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] eventually convicted of five specific murders, although he committed many, many other murders. But he was convicted on five counts and given life. And the judge actually wrote the whole proceedings up, which I've-- I've got a copy of the-- of the whole proceedings. 
INT: Were you involved in any other way in a pre-trial court? KHM: No.  INT: No. KHM: No. This was the actual trial, and he was-- I think I attended in 1945, but the-- the court went to Israel, the court-- the whole court went to Israel, I think. Because some people were not fit to travel to Germany. And eventually he-- he was convicted in 1986.  INT: Do you know where the trial-- you said Wuppertal-- KHM: Wuppertal.  INT: Do you know the name of the street where the courthouse was? KHM: No. It's a high court in Wuppertal.  INT: Right. KHM: No, I'm afraid I don't.  INT: Where did you stay? KHM: I stayed with-- with this lady, Ruth Cunning, in her house. And-- and I also stayed in a hotel, I think, but I then moved into her-- into her house, yes.  INT: Just one defendant you were testifying against? KHM: I was testifying just against one, yes.  INT: Was there any sort of security in the court? KHM: Yes, there was a lot of security in the court, but it wasn't-- it wasn't really very obvious. But yes, there was quite a lot of security. But there-- there were no demonstrations or anything. In-- in fact, the press was absolutely horrified at-- at the allegations, you know, because this man committed the most horrendous murders, as I have talked before. And everybody just wanted to see this man convicted. It was a question of conviction, of getting people to actually testify of-- of-- of-- the crucial thing was to identify this man because he lied and didn't-- in his CV he totally left out his time in Auschwitz. So it was crucial for us to-- for people who remembered him to identify him. And that-- that was so very important because he could have said it had been a mistaken identity. Well, it couldn't have been because people came from all over the world to-- to actually testify. But another thing is there was another SS officer called Wunsch, whom I-- I think I gave the wrong name. He came to testify for the defense. But of course, his testimony was also crucial because he identified the fella that he was there. Oh, he'd-- of course he-- he said he never saw him kill anyone. Came in his defense. But the other thing that he did, he drew the only plan of the Kanada that is available today. Because of course, everything was razed to the ground and the prisoners couldn't produce a plan because they had no free access to-- to the whole-- they didn't have the run of the place. But there was this man, Wunsch, and he drew the plan for the-- for the court. And this is the only plan that we have got today. 
INT: Were you in court for the entire trial of this man? KHM: No, everybody had their own allocated time. I think I was there a few days.  INT: No, were you in the court? KHM: In the court? Yes.  INT: Yes. KHM: Yes.  INT: When you were invited into the courtroom, who brought you into the courtroom? Can you remember? KHM: I couldn't tell you. I don't know. I was just called-- I was in the anteroom and I was just called in. It was my day. There-- there wasn't-- there wasn't another person being interviewed, you see. You were there-- the only witness at that time. And when you departed from the country, then they brought in the next witness so there was no collusion with witnesses, you didn't know who the other people were-- not until after-- after the trial.  INT: Can you describe the room? KHM: Well, it was just a-- it was just a straight courtroom, and on one side was sort of a semi-circular table. And there was Gottfried Weise with his lawyers sitting on one side.  INT: How many lawyers did he have? KHM: I don't know. I couldn't tell you. Going back 10 years, I don't know.  INT: What about how many prosecutors were there? KHM: Quite a lot of prosecutors. There was a chief prosecutor, and there were a lot of-- and a judge, and there were a lot of legal people around going backwards and forwards. And then, of course, there were quite a lot of a gallery with the public that were allowed in, yes, to-- to listen to the proceedings. And then they had this huge, huge screen where they were projecting my film. 
INT: What about-- what about the actual dock in which Weise was sitting or standing? Can you describe that? KHM: He-- he wasn't. He was sitting-- he was sitting with-- with his-- with his-- with his lawyers. Well, his defense counsel, whatever. He was sitting. He wasn't on his own. He was sitting in a row with-- with these people, and he never--  INT: At a table? KHM: Well, a kind of long semi-circular bench, I think. But he-- he never said a single word. His silence actually went against him. He didn't deny anything, and he didn't confirm anything-- total silence. And the judge actually remarked that that went against him.  INT: Was there any sort of protection or security for him? KHM: No. Well, I didn't see it. No. No. This is-- this is all I can tell you about the trial.  INT: How many hours do you think you were given to that trial? KHM: Well, I think one day I was there about six hours. It was a long, long time because I was cross-examined under my most minute details, not just details pertaining to his murder-- or murders-- but the locations within the camp itself. For-- for instance, I was asked, do I consider that the barrack-- I said it was divided up, on the other side was a duty room-- the SS duty room, and we were sorting out clothes. Do I consider that the-- the construction of the barrack was such that the-- there were vertical gaps or horizontal gaps. And what I could see over the other side. I said, I think it was vertical gaps. That's what I thought. But this-- that's how I was cross-examined. I think I was cross-examined to see if I was actually there so that I could give detail of my own time there, not just of what I saw. Because there were so many other people that confirmed some of these murders, but it was a question of confirming that you were actually in the place yourself. 
INT: Where were you when the verdict was announced? KHM: I was-- I was back in Birmingham when the verdict was announced. I was told-- I was told the verdict by the judge who actually telephoned me and told me the verdict. And he sent me a copy of the-- of the whole-- of the trial. That's how I got to know. But it-- but it was in the papers, of course. I've got all the newspaper cuttings, as well, which I asked Ruth Cunning to send to me, and she also sent me a videotape. I have somewhere got a videotape of the-- of the court proceedings, so I think some of it was-- I think it wasn't actually during the trial, but his-- he-- his interview with the press or whatever. So somewhere I have actually got a videotape on-- on Weise, on the trial.  INT: You already told me once, but will you tell me again when was the verdict announced? KHM: I think it was 1986.  INT: And what was the verdict? KHM: Well, the verdict was life imprisonment. But of course, what happened was the man himself wasn't in custody. He was out on bail. He was allowed on bail, although he was on-- on a murder charge, he wasn't in custody all the time that the trial was conducted. The man was free. So when the trial was over, before the verdict was announced-- which was a lapse of time, I believe-- he disappeared. And Interpol was looking for him. And what happened next was that he'd fled to Switzerland, and he had a stroke. He was taken to hospital, and there, of course, he was identified. And he was helicoptered back to the-- and then, of course, he was told the verdict and that's it. He got life. They brought him back to Germany. They actually allowed him out on bail, though he was convicted of-- alleged convicted murderer. 
INT: What was your reaction? KHM: A reaction of relief because we didn't know whether he would actually get life because not many-- not many of the Nazi war criminals had actually got life. We know that so many have been let off very lightly, and that was possibly one of the very last trials. But because this man committed the most gratuitous, horrendous murders, he actually got life, and there were so many witnesses testifying to that effect.  INT: Right. Thank you very much. We'll take a break now.  INT: Tape 18, Kitty Hart, the 21st of June, 1998. You have two sons. How did you choose their names? KHM: Well, David we chose at random, but the second name, Paul, is after my husband's-- my first husband's father. Peter again at random, and Charles after my own father's. So they each have got the name of their grandfather. But we didn't-- we didn't feel we want to call them that every day so we gave it to them as a second name.  INT: And what language did you speak at home with them when they were little? KHM: Well, we-- we brought them up on English because, well, my husband, although he came over with the kindertransport and his mother tongue was German, his German was not all that particularly good. And we didn't feel we want to speak German at home. What I was very much insistent on is that my mother spoke German to the boys so that they get the feel of the language. So very often I spoke English with them, and then my mother tried to speak German with them so that they are bilingual, or at least understand English. Polish I couldn't actually teach them because I had no one to converse with myself. So I couldn't really teach them the Polish language. 
INT: What kind of education did they receive? KHM: Well, unfortunately I couldn't afford to send my sons to any preparatory or private schools so they both went to the local primary school, which was absolutely appalling. And so I set upon educating my sons myself. And I taught them the basics, practically, of reading and writing. And eventually as they passed their 11-plus and went on to the grammar schools I actually educated myself through my boys. I-- I tried to-- when they did homework, I tried to do some homework. And in that way I went through the school system via my-- my children by the-- the system that I'd missed-- you know, the years that I'd missed. I caught up not totally, but I certainly learned most of the things that you learn in-- in a secondary school through my boys.  INT: What age where they when you first told them about your experiences? KHM: Well, they were brought up in it from-- from very-- very young age. They-- they literally lived it because in 1961 when my first book was published they were only very small. And David was seven or so, and Peter was only about five. So they knew there was a lot of publicity. People came and ask a lot of questions. I had television interviews, radio interviews, and talking about it in different places. And so they-- they knew about it from a very early age, and they took it in their stride completely. They-- they never had a problem. 
INT: Did your children get married? KHM: Yes, they-- they both married in 1981. They both married at the same time more or less. My youngest son, I think, married first, yes. Peter married first. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] He got-- he was at that time working for British Aerospace. He went through university and studied in Manchester. And David, my eldest son, got married-- again, after he finished university he studied medicine in Birmingham. And he'd been working in Canada and in Holland and then came back to England and married also in 1981.  INT: And the ladies that they married, what-- what were their origins? KHM: Well, Peter married a-- a girl from Saint Albans from quite a religious background. And in fact, he's sort of more religious now than he'd ever been because, you know, she keeps a kosher household. And-- but Peter married a-- a non-Jewish girl whom he met in London.  INT: Do you have grandchildren? KHM: Yes. Well, I have-- David has got three daughters. The elder one, the doctor, he's got three daughters. And Peter, my younger son, has got four sons. So--  INT: What are their names? KHM: [LAUGHS] Well, the girls' names are Lucy, Sophie, and Juliet, and the boy's name is Jonathan, Simon, [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Daniel, and Michael. I tried to get them in the right order. [PAUSES FOR 4 SECONDS] And the eldest will be 15-- or the girl will be 15, and Peter's eldest had bar mitzvah last year. And three more bar mitzvahs to go, and the youngest is five. 
INT: All right. Do you see a lot of them? KHM: Of course, they are a good way away. Yes, that's right, we see quite a lot of them, yes. But it isn't so easy because, of course, they are quite a long way away-- Bedford and Harpenden. It's not just in Birmingham.  INT: Are you involved in any Holocaust activities at all? KHM: Well, I've never stopped being involved in-- in Holocaust activities. I've been on numerous committees in the Holocaust educational trust, and I'm a patron of the Imperial War Museum. And because of the video I've been right through the States lecturing. I've been involved in many, many school projects and American bases in England, boarding schools, universities, functions. In fact, if-- if-- if I wouldn't be really keeping a-- a close eye on it, I would be involved full time. Which of course, I can't afford to do. Well, at the present time I'm-- I'm retired, but while I was working it-- it was very difficult because I had to get time off and do these projects in between work. And sometimes I couldn't actually be paid. You know, I had to give up work and lose so much of my salary to take time off to do these projects. And it-- it is a costly business these projects, because you don't always even get your expenses.  INT: How is your health today? KHM: I-- I do have my legacies from during my time in the camp when I've-- I've had injured-- I had my-- my spine was injured. I've had two operations on my back and I've had a fractured skull, of course. And so I have got a few legacies from the camp, but-- but you know, by and large it hasn't been too bad. It's my mother who never really recovered, and she died in 1974. She-- you know, she had heart disease and-- and high blood pressure from-- from the time she came out of the camp. 
INT: Have you ever received any psychological counseling? KHM: No, I've never ever had any counseling whatsoever at all from-- from any sources. There-- there was no such thing as post-traumatic counseling. No-- nobody thought about that. That's a sort of present-day phenomenon. We had to cope-- most of the survivors had to cope as best they could. The worst of it was the being ostracized, I think. That what hurt most. And people not really wanting to-- to know what had happened. And some of us were just determined to make sure that history was not going to be rewritten. Because where do historians get their information from if it isn't from the survivors? And so if the survivors are not going to speak out, history will be rewritten. And I was very conscious of that. I felt it was my duty to make sure that this wasn't going to happen.  INT: Do you ever have dreams or nightmares about your experiences? KHM: Not very often. Some-- something might crop up, yes, but mostly I can shut it out. I can separate it, yes. I can talk about it and then separate it from my actual life. The only time I couldn't separate it-- when I actually went back to Auschwitz the first time, I felt I'd never been away. And I almost got confused. It was only when I saw the taxi that was taking me away I realized that I was only there as a visitor. That perhaps was the only time where it-- it did hit me. But I've been back on field trips. Taking people back to Poland is a very important part of my work-- taking them back to different places, back to the ghetto, back to the Lublin ghetto where I was, back to-- to Belzec where I nearly landed up and where my-- my grandmother died. Back to where my father was killed in Tarnow, and of course, back to the-- back to the camp, and back to my hometown as well to show them the remains of where I was born and-- so it's been a very important part of my work, taking people back to Poland. 
INT: How did your experiences affect your Judaism? KHM: Well, I think it probably did affect my Judaism. Because the first thing you question-- or I questioned is, how could a god allow this to happen? And so you immediately begin to think, well, if there is something then they should have not allowed these innocent people to be killed the way they were being slaughtered. So you do question your religion very much. And because I was never brought up in a religious environment in the first place, it wasn't difficult for me not to believe.  INT: Have your experiences affected your Jewish identity? KHM: No. It never has altered my Jewish identity. I will always identify my Jewish-- my Jewishness.  INT: Are you affiliated to a synagogue? KHM: Yes, I've always been-- I-- I was married in-- in a synagogue. I was married at Singers Hill Synagogue in Birmingham. But of course, I've been-- since I've been-- I've been divorced and I remarried. I no longer belong to this particular synagogue [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] since-- since my divorce because I've remarried Philip, who is not Jewish. But [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Philip understands the issue of the Holocaust very much more so than my first husband whose mother and two brothers-- well, his mother and his one brother were killed in Auschwitz. And yet he didn't want to know-- he didn't want to know any detail. He didn't want to know what actually had happened, and he didn't take an interest in my work. And he-- I wouldn't say he actually obstructed me in a physical sort of way, but in a subtle way, yes. He-- he didn't support my work, and I think it probably [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] contributed to the breakup of my marriage. Because I needed someone who was going to support my work. 
INT: Mm-hmm. KHM: And I found Philip supported my work wholeheartedly. And so this was what really happened.  INT: Did you apply for restitution? KHM: Oh, yes. I mean, restitution was very-- it was just really nominal when you think about it. When you think in present day restitution, people are expecting, I-- I have just been told if someone's been in prison for the extra 50 days or something they're going to get thousands of pounds because of wrongful imprisonment for a few days-- extra days in prison after they'd committed some sort of crime. Whereas for the actual time spent in prison we got nothing. Yes, I did get a-- a small pension-- just a small disability pension because I have got certain problems, and I was proved to be-- I don't know, so many percent disabled-- a few percent. And so I do receive a small pension. But my mother received a pension for-- because she lost her occupation. Strange enough, I didn't receive any money for my loss of education. Now, this really was the most unjust because you had people who came over to England, [INAUDIBLE] the kindertransport pre-war, went to school here in England, yet because they-- they went to German schools in-- oh, they couldn't go to German schools in Germany during the war, were granted restitution-- were granted compensation for loss of education. Whereas I, who genuinely lost my education, couldn't get any-- any-- not-- nothing at all because I didn't attend a German school. But nonetheless, they should still have paid for a loss of education. So yes, my-- my mother also got something for the loss of my father, but I mean, we did-- had no restitution for property, which still belongs to-- still in Poland. 
INT: We're nearing the close of this interview now. Why did you come forward to tell your story? KHM: Well, I came forward to tell my story because I've been telling it since 1945 and because I believe that this was certainly a worthwhile project. It will be there when I have gone. Hopefully people will come and listen to it. And because so many generations have missed out on this story, I'm hoping that the future generations will not allow history to be rewritten. And they will listen to what the survivors have got to say.  INT: What would you like to tell future generations? KHM: I think the most important thing to tell future generations is to learn from the past. Because up to now people haven't actually learned very much. We've had ethnic cleansing in Bosnia, in Rwanda, in Cambodia. It's called ethnic cleansing. The Germans called it [NON-ENGLISH], special treatment, but the result's the same. It's murder under a pseudonym, under the guise of-- of murder. They just give it different names. So the future generations have got to be vigilant and not allow dictators to come to power. And that, I think, is the most important thing of all, isn't it?  INT: Is there anything else you'd like to add? KHM: Well, I think we've said enough, haven't we? KHM: This--  INT: Where was that taken? KHM: This photograph was taken at Harpenden.  INT: Thank you. PHILIP MOXON: Do you-- do you want me to say anything about Kitty's work with me?  INT: Yes, you can. Briefly. KHM: No, unless it'll come into it. Come into it, yes.  INT: Kitty, would you like to introduce your husband, please? KHM: Well, this is Philip. And I have known him for about 40 years. And we got married in 1992.  INT: Philip, how did you get-- how did you meet Kitty? PHILIP MOXON: She was introduced into my private practice by one of my partners, who brought her in. I thought she was rather a sad little woman. She was brilliant at her work, but I got to know the reason why she was rather sad, and that she was the principal breadwinner in her family. She had two children and her husband. She was working full-time for the health service and part-time in private practice. So she'd every reason to be rather introspective at that time. [PAUSES FOR 3 SECONDS] Later on, that partner left to take a-- a job in America. And my other partner died. And so I was left running the practice on my own. And Kitty, by that time, was very, very experienced, not only in-- in radiology, but in administration and also on a good deal of the-- the pathology, the work which we did. She was able to run the practice for me extremely well. Well, we started to get more than professionally associated when we took up a mutual interest in sport. There was skiing, which we'd taken up later in life, but we became quite obsessional about, so that we were eventually spending about a year on the snow. And then in the summer, we used to do wind surfing. So with all these sporting activities, and the-- the work, and my radiology practice, we were in close association. And then we were-- I was rather taken over by events that my wife died. And Kitty thought it was-- we both thought it was time that we separated her from her first husband. So we got married. 
INT: Very nice. How much of Kitty's wartime experience did you know about? PHILIP MOXON: At first, very little, until I-- I read her book. But subsequently, I had been quite a-- [INAUDIBLE] quite a lot. I had been to Poland with Kitty on her various expeditions there, and I'd been around several concentration camps, and I found it fascinating.  INT: How did it affect your relationship with Kitty, do you think? PHILIP MOXON: Not-- not to any detrimental way. But I must say, living with Kitty, you're never far away from Auschwitz. She tends to assess people as how they would behave under those circumstances. And she-- she suffers fools-- doesn't suffer fools, I'm afraid. She-- I'm very much aware of the fact that her previous experience [INAUDIBLE] all her decisions.  INT: Is there anything more you'd like to say about Kitty? PHILIP MOXON: Well, I-- I-- I always remember Kitty as a-- an absolutely fearless skier. No run was too dangerous or too black that she wouldn't take it full crack. Much quicker than me. I sort of go down carefully. At the same run, Kitty would be at the bottom, waiting for me to catch up. But eventually, she paid a price for this, and she damaged both her knees. So she had to stop Alpine skiing. And she took up [INAUDIBLE] at a time when the new technique of ski skating was-- skate skiing, rather, was-- was just starting. And she became very, very good at that. So if I had a-- I had an injury happen to me to take me off skiing, I should have been terribly depressed. But for Kitty, it didn't mean anything at all. She immediately switched to something else, which I would have been quite incapable of doing. 
INT: Kitty, is there anything you'd like to say about Philip at this point? KHM: Well, I'd like to say that he supported me in my Holocaust work all the way. And he gave me the opportunity to-- to do so many things which I wouldn't have been able to do otherwise. He gave me time off from work, which was very difficult. It wasn't easy. We had to get somebody else to do-- cover me, do my work, while I carried on and gave interviews and lectures and went around America. And he encouraged me to do that because he knew it was important. Also, some of the filming we actually did on his premises, in-- in the X-ray department, in Philip's practice. So he supported me all the way. And I think that's brought us much closer together as well.  INT: Thank you very much, Kitty Hart and Philip Hart. KHM: No, it's Philip Moxon.  INT: Thank you very much, Philip and Kitty Hart-Moxon. Is it right now? PHILIP MOXON: That'll do.
